# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: *Apostolic Constitutions* (*Constitutiones Apostolicae*; *Διαταγαὶ τῶν ἁγίων ἀποστόλων*). Skaballanovich provides the critical patristic citation: *Διαταγαὶ τῶν ἀγίων ἀποστόλων διὰ Κλήμεντος τοῦ Ῥωμαίων Ἐπισκόπου τε καὶ πολίτου, ἢ καθολικὴ διδασκαλία*, in J.-P. Migne, *Patrologiae cursus completus, series graeca*, tom. 1, col. 556–1156; Russian translation by I. N. Kazansky, Kazan, 1864.

[^2]: Thus do the first printed Typika designate themselves on their colophons (title leaves), up through those published under Patriarch Joseph, as well as the contemporary Old Believer Typikon—in the Edinoverie edition.

[^3]: *Peregrinatio ad loca sancta*, 34, in *Pravoslavny Palestinsky Sbornik*, vyp. 20 (St. Petersburg, 1889), p. 54.

[^4]: Luke 24:53.

[^5]: Psalms 150; 149; 1:3.

[^6]: Psalms 21:23, 26 [22:22, 25]; 49:14, 23 [50:14, 23]. Cf. Hebrews 13:15.

[^7]: Numbers 10:10.

[^8]: Sacred trumpets also took the place of our bells among the Hebrews: by them the people were summoned to the Tabernacle, and later, presumably, to the Temple; for this purpose there were two silver trumpets at the Tabernacle; when both were sounded together, it was a summons to the whole congregation; the sound of a single trumpet summoned the leaders alone; to strike camp an alarm was sounded (Num. 10:1–7). This passage from the Book of Numbers is read in our Church and in the Roman Catholic Church at the blessing of bells. At the Temple the number of trumpets reached up to 100 (1 Chron. 15:24; 2 Chron. 5:12; cf. Flavius Josephus, *Antiquitates Judaicae* III, 12, 6). The trumpets had an exclusively sacred use. They resembled the horns with which muezzins currently summon Muslims to prayer. A. Olesnitsky, "Ancient Hebrew Music and Singing" [*Drevneevreyskaya muzyka i penie*], in *Trudy Kievskoy Dukhovnoy Akademii* (1871), no. 11, p. 117.

[^9]: 1 Chronicles 16:40–41.

[^10]: Judges 9:13; Talmud, *Arakhin*, Gemara 11.

[^11]: Perhaps the places in the Psalms where such a raising of pitch and trumpet accompaniment occurred are designated by the enigmatic term *selah*. This term appears in 39 Psalms (e.g., 7, 21 [22], 22 [23]) and the Canticle of Habakkuk; 16 Psalms have it once, 15 have it twice, 7 Psalms and the Canticle of Habakkuk have it three times, and one Psalm (88 [89]) has it four times. The Septuagint translates this term as *διάψαλμα* (*diapsalma*); the Slavonic Bible omits it. The term, as well as its translation in the Septuagint, has been explained in various ways (the most ancient explanation being "unto eternity" / "forever"), but the majority of scholars incline to the opinion that it indicates the beginning of strophes or a change of voice and musical pitch, most probably an upward elevation of the pitch. (J. Sommer, *Biblische Abhandlungen*, vol. 1, Bonn, 1844, I: "Erklärung des Sela", pp. 1–84). It is also thought that *selah* signifies the place in the canticle where one choir paused and the other repeated what was sung by the first, and that the word forms an acronym of the words: *sor lemahla hashar* ("return, singer, to the beginning"). (A. Olesnitsky, "Ancient Hebrew Music and Singing", *TKDA* [1871], no. 12, p. 371).

Alekseev, *Bogosluzhenie, prazdniki i religioznye obryady nyneshnikh evreev* [Worship, Feasts, and Religious Rites of Contemporary Jews], Novgorod, 1861, p. 14. Also in Russian on synagogal worship with its history: N. [^46]: The *Kaddish* prayer is remarkable for the similarity of certain expressions to the Lord's Prayer. It begins thus: "May His great Name be magnified and sanctified in the world which He created according to His will, and may He manifest His Kingdom."

[^47]: The formula "Bless ye the Lord" is also mentioned by the Mishnah (a part of the Talmud, 3rd c.), *Berakhot* VII, 3. Cf. our liturgical exclamation: "Blessed is our God," not used at the Liturgy, which is a predominantly New Testament service. "Blessings" (*berakhot*) are the customary name for prayer and the most commonly used prayer formula among the Hebrews. It is prescribed by the Talmud for all occasions: "During rains and upon hearing good news, they pronounce: 'Blessed is the All-Good Benefactor'; upon hearing evil tidings: 'Blessed is the righteous Judge'; upon seeing a rainbow, they pronounce: 'Blessed is He Who is faithful to His covenant'; upon seeing a Black man, a red man, an albino, a giant, or a dwarf, they pronounce: 'Blessed is He... Who maketh varied His creatures'; upon seeing a handsome person or a beautiful tree, they say: 'Blessed is He... Who hath created beautiful creatures in His world'; upon seeing an idol, they pronounce: 'Blessed is the Long-Suffering One'" (*Berakhot* IX, 1).

[^48]: Therefore, concerning the *Shema*, one does not say "to pray it," but only "to recite it." E. Schürer, *Geschichte des jüdischen Volkes*, vol. II, p. 529.

[^49]: Talmud, *Berakhot* I, 4.

[^50]: Cf. our Great Doxology: "Blessed art Thou, O Lord, God of our fathers; teach me Thy statutes; extend Thy mercy unto them that know Thee"—all these are scriptural expressions, yet their selection is characteristic.

[^51]: Cf. in our tradition the recitation of the Creed at the Midnight Office (*Mesonyktikon*) and Compline (*Apodeipnon*).

[^52]: Flavius Josephus, *Antiquitates Judaicae* IV, 8, 13.

[^53]: Luke 10:26.

[^54]: E. Schürer, *Geschichte des jüdischen Volkes*, vol. II, p. 541.

[^55]: Although certain prayers of the *Shemoneh Esreh*, namely the initial and concluding ones, even modern Jewish scholars (L. Herzfeld, *Geschichte des Volkes Israel*, vol. II, pp. 5, 13, 186; vol. III, pp. 200 ff.; L. Zunz, *Gottesdienstliche Vorträge*, pp. 367 ff.) are inclined to date to the era of Ezra, mentions of them begin only with rabbis of the end of the 1st century A.D., and they likely acquired their present form around A.D. 70–120, judging from the references in them (the 14th and 17th) to the destruction of Jerusalem and the cessation of the sacrificial cult. The latest of these prayers is the one containing an imprecation against the *minim* (heretics / sectarians). It was added by Rabbi Samuel the Little, a disciple of Gamaliel II (the grandson of Gamaliel I, the teacher of the Apostle Paul). According to the Talmud (*Berakhot*, fol. 28), by *minim* are meant the Sadducees; according to the Church Fathers (St. Epiphanius, *Panarion / Adversus Haereses* 29, 9; St. Jerome, *Commentary on Isaiah* 5:18–19), this prayer contained an imprecation against the Nazarenes, i.e., Christians; the recently discovered Cairo Genizah text of this prayer explicitly contains the designation *nozrim*, i.e., Nazarenes (E. Schürer, *Geschichte des jüdischen Volkes*, vol. II, pp. 543–544). Naturally, in the earliest era of Christianity, an imprecation against Jews converting to Christianity could have been inserted into the prayers, which was subsequently in all likelihood expunged and replaced by a general curse upon apostates and enemies of Judaism.

[^56]: In them scholars point to expressions similar to the Lord's Prayer: "Forgive us, our Father, for we have sinned; pardon us, our King, for we have transgressed." In the Canticle of Zechariah at the birth of John the Forerunner (Luke 1:68–79), which begins with the word "Blessed" (*Benedictus*), expressions similar to these prayers are likewise pointed out. A. Nikitin, *Sinagogi*, p. 228. (Cf. Prof. D. I. Bogdashevsky, *Ekzegeticheskie zametki* [Exegetical Notes], vyp. 4, pp. 29 ff.—*Censor's note*).

[^57]: Talmud, *Berakhot* III, 3; IV, 1.

[^58]: Babylonian Talmud, *Berakhot* IV, 3, fol. 29a (Russian translation by N. Pereferkovich, St. Petersburg, 1909). Cf. in our rite the varying number of stichera appointed at "Lord, I have cried" (*Господи, воззвах*).

[^59]: Talmud, *Berakhot* IV, 7.

[^60]: Talmud, *Berakhot* V, 2; *Talmud: Mishnah and Tosefta*, trans. N. Pereferkovich, St. Petersburg, 1899, vol. I, pp. 43–46. Such slight modifications for feasts within the text of standard prayers are customary in Roman Catholic worship (cf. our liturgical dismissals [*apolyseis*]), as is the division of the liturgical year according to climatic seasons: the Roman Catholic Breviary (the liturgical book corresponding to our Monthly Menaion and Triodion) is divided into *partes*: *hiemalis*, *verna*, *aestiva*, *autumnalis*.

[^61]: E. Schürer, *Geschichte des jüdischen Volkes*, vol. II, p. 531; L. Zunz, *Die gottesdienstlichen Vorträge der Juden*, pp. 3 ff.

[^62]: Talmud, *Megillah* IV, 4. Cf. Luke 4:17.

[^63]: Moses Maimonides, *Hilkhot Tefillah* VIII, 3.

[^64]: E. Schürer, *Die religiösen Alterthümer der Bibel*, Münster, 1878, p. 157.

[^66]: A. A. Olesnitsky, *Trudy Kievskoy Dukhovnoy Akademii* (1871), no. 11, pp. 389–391.

[^67]: Talmud, *Berakhot* V, 1; *Ta'anit* II, 2; cf. *Berakhot* I, 3 on the *Shema*. Sitting during divine worship and cross-shaped prostration upon the floor have been retained by the Roman Catholic Church. According to St. John Cassian (5th c.), ancient ascetics did not approve of the second rite: "Upon him who lieth long upon the ground, they say, there assault not only intrusive thoughts, but also sleep" (*De institutis coenobiorum* II, 7; Bishop Theophan [the Recluse], *Drevnie inocheskie ustavy* [Ancient Monastic Rules], Moscow, 1892, p. 533).

[^68]: The influence of synagogal worship upon Christian divine services was exaggerated by Protestant scholars in the interests of polemics against Roman Catholicism, with its exalted view of the origin and mystical significance of the Liturgy. Among the more prominent investigations in this vein: J. Lightfoot, *Horae Hebraicae et Talmudicae*, Basel, 1661 (wherein synagogal worship, through an intentional rearranging of its constituent parts, was made to assume the appearance of the Roman Catholic Mass).

[^69]: F. C. Conybeare, *Philo: About the Contemplative Life*, Oxford, 1895; P. Wendland, *Die Therapeuten und die philonische Schrift vom beschaulichen Leben*, in *Jahrbücher für classische Philologie* (1896); N. Smirnov, *Terapevty i sochinenie Filona Iudeya O zhizni sozertsatelnoy* [The Therapeutae and the Treatise of Philo Judaeus 'On the Contemplative Life'], Kyiv, 1909.

[^70]: Eusebius of Caesarea, *Historia Ecclesiastica* II, 16–17.

[^73]: Hypophonic chanting (*пение ипофонное*).

[^74]: Epiphonic chanting (*пение епифонное*). Both forms were practiced also in Old Testament Temple worship.

[^79]: Thus is named among the Greeks the 7th ecclesiastical tone (*Grave Tone* / *Barys*).

[^80]: Philo, *De vita contemplativa* §§ 19, 11, *Opera omnia*, tom. V, ed. stereotypa, Leipzig, 1901.

[^81]: To such chanting in ancient Temple practice reference is made perhaps in Psalm 146:7 [147:7]: "Sing alternately" (Heb. *'anu*, "respond," yet in Greek *ἐξάρξατε*, Slavonic *начните* ["begin"]); the expression can be understood also of the two other genres of singing: epiphonic and hypophonic. The musical term *Selah* is likewise interpreted as indicating antiphonal chanting. It is thought that antiphonal singing was borrowed by the Hebrews from Egypt, where choral songs were divided between priests and people, between men and women, between children and adults. Such singing accompanied the worship of the golden calf: descending the mountain and hearing the tumult, Moses says: "It is not the voice of them that shout for mastery, neither is it the voice of them that cry for being overcome, but the noise of them that sing do I hear" (Exodus 32:18); more literally from the Hebrew: "It is not the alternating voice [*'anot*—answering] of victors, nor the alternating voice of the vanquished, but alternating voices do I hear." Philo perceives the first instance of antiphonal chanting in the Canticle of Moses upon the crossing of the Red Sea. How accurate is this? The execution of this canticle is thus described in Exodus: "Then sang Moses and the children of Israel this song unto the Lord, and spake, saying: I will sing unto the Lord, for He hath triumphed gloriously..."; after the exposition of the canticle it is added: "And Miriam the prophetess, the sister of Aaron, took a timbrel [tambourine, drum, a dance musical instrument] in her hand, and all the women went out after her with timbrels and with dances [Heb. *mecholot*, choral dances, Greek *χορῶν*]; and Miriam answered them: Sing ye to the Lord, for He hath triumphed gloriously; the horse and his rider hath He thrown into the sea" (Exodus 14:31; 15:1, 20–21). Commentators interpret this description diversely (and not merely in the sense of antiphonal chanting): according to some, the men repeated directly after Moses and according to his melodic pattern every verse of the song, and then the same verse was sung by the women (as in our rite the prokeimenon, troparion, or exapostilarion is chanted thrice); according to others, the women sang one verse, the men another, and so on alternately; yet far more directly from the context of the discourse emerges the understanding that after Moses the verse intoned by him was repeated by the men, while the women constantly repeated the single first verse, changing the first person "I will sing" into the imperative "Sing ye," as if thereby inviting their husbands to sing more harmoniously the hymn to the Lord—all the more so since only those standing closest to Moses could distinctly hear the words of the canticle. A. A. Olesnitsky, *Drevneevreyskaya muzyka i penie* [Ancient Hebrew Music and Singing], in *Trudy Kievskoy Dukhovnoy Akademii* (1871), no. 11, pp. 115–116. Our liturgical order for the execution of this canticle on Holy and Great Saturday is founded upon this latter understanding. (Cf. also C. F. Keil, *Manual of Biblical Archaeology*, Russian trans., Kyiv, 1879, vol. II, § 136).

[^82]: Acts 2:42.

[^83]: Thus is entitled the recently discovered second-century monument (*Didache / Teaching of the Twelve Apostles*), containing diverse injunctions in the name of the Apostles regarding church order and divine worship. Yet here in "the doctrine of the Apostles" a different content is presupposed.

[^84]: "Breaking of bread" is the technical designation of the Eucharist in the New Testament: Acts 20:7; 1 Corinthians 10:16; 11:24. Wherefore the Peshitta renders this expression here and in 20:7: "in the breaking of the Eucharist." Between the words "in fellowship" and "in the breaking of bread" certain codices lack "and," by virtue of which "the apostles' doctrine and fellowship" are separated as an independent pair alongside the other, "the breaking of bread and prayers"; the Vulgate, on the contrary, conflates the second member with the third: *in communicatione fractionis panis*.

This portico, presenting a place sheltered from heat and rain with benches for rest, was a favorite corner of the Temple also for the Savior (John 10:23; cf. Acts 3:11).

[^125]: Jude 1:12.

[^126]: In the parallel passage of 2 Peter 2:13, instead of *ἀγάπαις* stands *ἀπάταις* (Slav. *питающеся лестьми своими, ядуще с вами* [sporting themselves with their own deceivings while they feast with you]); yet a series of codices read *ἀγάπαις* (Vaticanus, Vulgate, Ethiopic, Peshitta, et al.). P. Batiffol (*L'Agapé*, in *Études d'histoire et de théologie positive*, Paris, 1902), who denies the historical existence of agape meals, reads *ἀπάταις* also in Jude 1:12 (following certain codices). Indeed, this is the sole locus in the New Testament and in all contemporary literature where the word *ἀγάπη* bears such a technical meaning. Everywhere else in the Old Testament, in the Septuagint, in the New Testament, in the Apostle Jude himself (vv. 1 and 21), and in Philo (where *ἀγάπη* occurs only once), *ἀγάπη* signifies solely love. P. Sokolov, *Agapy ili vecheri lyubvi v drevne-khristianskom mire* [Agapae or Love-Feasts in the Ancient Christian World], Sergiev Posad, 1906, pp. 73–75.

[^127]: Metropolitan Philaret, *Slova i rechi* [Discourses and Speeches], Moscow, 1843, vol. 2, p. 293.

[^129]: The fact that apostles and prophets are included in this list does not argue against the fact that we are dealing here primarily with the celebrants of divine worship: concerning the Antiochian "prophets and teachers," among whose number was the Apostle Barnabas, the writer of Acts notes that they on one occasion ministered (*leitourgountōn*) unto the Lord and fasted (Acts 13:2). *Leitourgein* is the customary word for the temple ministry of priests in the Septuagint: Exodus 28:35; Numbers 4:39; Ezekiel 40:46; Judith 4:14; Hebrews 10:11; cf. [^216]: These explanations are as follows: the Apostle Paul desired to set an Aramaic phrase against the Greek pride in wisdom and eloquence (St. John Chrysostom, Bl. Theophylact); to heighten his warning or hint at his Judaizing opponents in Corinth (Hofmann, Heinrici); to certify the authenticity of the epistle by writing this word with his own hand (Billroth, Rückert); that this was some memorable expression from the Apostle Paul's visit to Corinth or a Christian password (Ewald); or that it was the Apostle Paul's personal signet stamp upon the epistle, subsequently transcribed in Greek letters (Godet).

[^219]: *Horologion of the Kyiv St. Michael Library*, no. 311 (404), 165, fol. 41v. This vocalized refrain (*popevka*) was termed *nenenayka*. In addition to it, there was known in the Greek Church the *terirem* refrain with a fourfold or greater repetition of the initial syllable *te*, but with different vowels for different tones: for Tone 1 *te*, Tone 2 *ti*, Tone 3 *to*, etc. Vocalized refrains (*terirems* / *popevki*) are even now used among the Greeks, South Slavs, and among our Old Believers and Russian Athonites. In the Monastery of St. Panteleimon on Mount Athos, thanks to such refrains, the chanting of the troparion *Bogoroditse Devo* (*O Virgin Mother of God*) at Vespers extends for an entire hour. *Zametki poklonnika sv. gory* [Notes of a Pilgrim to the Holy Mountain], Kyiv, 1864, pp. 32–33; D. Razumovsky, *Tserkovnoe penie v Rossii* [Church Chanting in Russia], issue 1, Moscow, 1867, pp. 108–109.

[^220]: Both terms are used especially in our Order of Holy Baptism at the mingling of the oil with water, and *Alleluia* in the Roman Church at Pascha.

[^221]: Especially in the Roman Church at the litany: *Kyrie, eleison; Christe, eleison*.

[^222]: The gift of tongues is represented in the New Testament as such a miraculous, sublime, and reverently awe-inspiring phenomenon that it can in no wise be acknowledged as a phenomenon of the same order as the glossolalia of Russian and foreign sectarians (Khlysts, Malevantsy, Irvingites, Mormons), consisting of entirely nonsensical combinations of syllables and words (for example: *rentre fente rente fintrifunt—trrr, tlim, tlim, dze*; D. Konovalov, "Religiozny ekstaz v russkom misticheskom sektantstve" [Religious Ecstasy in Russian Mystical Sectarianism], in *Bogoslovsky Vestnik* (1908), no. 4, pp. 738, 744), representing a purely nervous phenomenon accompanied by convulsions (*ibid.*, no. 10, p. 200; no. 4, p. 754), whirling (no. 3, pp. 734, 755), nervous laughter, and weeping (no. 4, p. 753). On a purely neuropathic basis there may also appear in certain individuals speech in a foreign tongue previously heard by them and unconsciously retained. Both phenomena were known also to early Christians, yet were sharply distinguished by them from the gift of tongues imparted by the Holy Spirit; they were explained as the influence upon man by evil spirits. Eusebius cites the narrative of an ancient historian concerning the glossolalia of the Montanists: "A certain one named Montanus, it is related, from among the recent converts, through an inordinate desire for preeminence, fell under the influence of the adversary, and suddenly, falling into a state of possession and ecstasy, began to speak and utter strange sounds (*lalein kai xenophōnein*), that is, he prophesied contrary to the custom transmitted from of old and preserved by succession in the Church... He [the devil] even raised up two women and filled them with a lying spirit, so that they also, like the aforementioned Montanus, spoke senselessly, inappropriately, and strangely" (Eusebius, *Historia Ecclesiastica*, V, 16). According to the account of Bl. Jerome, a certain Frankish official, having fallen under demonic possession from childhood, in the presence of St. Hilarion began to speak, scarcely touching the ground with his feet and turning exceedingly red, in Syriac and Greek—languages completely unknown to him—with entirely correct pronunciation (*Life of St. Hilarion*, ch. 22). Among Russian and foreign sectarians, however, speaking in unknown foreign languages has not been observed, but solely the utterance of unintelligible sounds.

[^229]: 1 Corinthians 14:26.

[^230]: Not uncommon even in contemporary divine worship: the most important hymns are chanted first by a single reader, the canonarch, or the presiding celebrant (*proestos*) himself.

[^231]: 1 Corinthians 14:16. "He that occupieth the room of the unlearned" [literally "simple man / commoner"], Slavonic *nevezhdy*, Greek *idiōtou*. *Idiōtēs*—a person who does not hold an official or leading position, or a non-specialist, for example ordinary citizens as distinguished from the king (Herodotus II, 81, et al.), the general public as distinguished from the scholars of a school (Plutarch, *Quaestiones Convivales* I, 1); non-ecstatics and non-mantics as distinguished from the visionary (Pausanias, *Corinthiaca* 13). In the LXX in Proverbs 6:8, as in Herodotus. In the Talmud—an ordinary man as distinguished from a rabbi, craftsman, magistrate, and the like. In agreement with all these meanings, the word here clearly signifies each member of the liturgical assembly as distinguished from him who cometh forward with some revelation, in this particular case with speaking in tongues. Consequently, the word stands not in the sense of a casual visitor to the assembly, for example an unbeliever, as in verse 24, where the very same word is used, nor in the general sense of an ignorant or uneducated person, still less in the modern sense of this word, which some also admitted, rendering it, for example, in German as *Idiot* (A. J. Binterim, *Die vorzüglichsten Denkwürdigkeiten der Christ-Katholischen Kirche*, IV, 1, Mainz, 1827, p. 319). "He that occupieth the room" indicates that the status of the *idiōtēs* is not permanent, but occasional: all, except him who actively ministereth during divine worship, are *idiōtai*, and each becometh such at a given moment when he hath no revelation (Th. Zahn & Ph. Bachmann, *Der erste Brief des Paulus an die Korinther*, Leipzig, 1904, pp. 420–421). Here, consequently, is already given a rather sharp (more so than in the synagogue) division of worshippers into clergy [those who minister sacred rites] and laity, although this demarcation is not yet rigid, but fluid.

Theodoret relates that when Bishop Leontius altered the doxology (the Lesser Doxology) in an Arian spirit, the presbyters Flavian (according to Nicetas Choniates subsequently Bishop of Antioch) and Diodore (subsequently Bishop of Tarsus), having broken communion with the bishop, began to assemble in separate churches and there sing psalms antiphonally: "They were the first who, having divided the choir into two halves, established the antiphonal singing of the Psalms of David, and this began in Antioch." This report was reconciled with Socrates' account already by Nicetas Choniates through the supposition that these presbyters merely translated from Syriac into Greek the antiphonal hymns introduced by St. Ignatius. (Archbishop Philaret [Gumilevsky] of Chernihiv), *Istoricheskiy obzor pesnopenvtsev i pesnopeniya grecheskoy tserkvi* [*Historical Survey of the Hymnographers and Psalmody of the Greek Church*], Chernihiv, 1864, p. 51.

In Augusti's opinion, testimony unto the profound antiquity of the liturgical use of the Gospels is furnished by the addition unto the Lord's Prayer of the concluding doxology. Upon this doxology early critics of the New Testament text already looked *non ut scripturae sed ut liturgiae partem* [not as part of Scripture, but as part of the Liturgy] (J. J. Griesbach, cited in Augusti, *Denkwürdigkeiten*, p. 72). It is absent from the 4th-century codices: Sinaiticus, Vaticanus, Old Latin translations, and the citations of Tertullian, St. Cyprian, and Origen; but it is present in the Peshitta (3rd cent.), the Gothic translation (4th cent.), and the citation of the *Didache* (*Teaching of the Twelve Apostles*, 2nd cent.)—albeit everywhere in diverse forms (D. Bogdashevsky, "Kriticheskiye etyudy po Novomu Zavetu: IV. O molitve Gospodney" [*Critical Studies on the New Testament: IV. On the Lord's Prayer*], in *Trudy Kiyevskoy Dukhovnoy Akademii*, 1908, no. 4, pp. 500–511).

[^339]: Recently in Russian scholarship the opinion was advanced that the ancient veneration of martyrs in the 2nd–4th centuries also included religious dancing (D. Konovalov, "Religiozny ekstaz v russkom misticheskom sektantstve" [*Religious Ecstasy in Russian Mystical Sectarianism*], in *Bogoslovskiy Vestnik*, 1907, no. 10, p. 351; 1908, no. 4, pp. 707–728). In the acts of the martyrs, which frequently conclude with descriptions of liturgical assemblies in honor of the martyrs, there is no mention of dancing in their honor: to interpret the phrase in the *Martyrdom of Polycarp* concerning celebrating his commemoration "with jubilation and joy" (*agalliasei kai charai* [ἀγαλλιάσει καὶ χαρᾷ]) as referring unto dancing (Konovalov, p. 707) is arbitrary. Only a few monuments of the 3rd and 4th centuries speak of such dances as a local and tolerated phenomenon. Thus, according to the testimony of St. Gregory of Nyssa, St. Gregory the Wonderworker (3rd cent.), yielding unto the inclinations of his flock in order to divert them from pagan festivals, permitted a little leaping/dancing (*ti mikron hypokirtan* [τὶ μικρὸν ὑποκιρτᾷν]) in honor of the martyrs (*Hom. in Eccl.* 6). A 4th-century homily on the commemoration of the martyr Polyeuktos († 259 in Armenia) invites: "according to the established custom let us exult/dance (*choreusōmen* [χορεύσωμεν]) in honor of the martyr" (B. Aubé, *Polyeucte dans l'histoire*, Paris, 1882, p. 79). There is also cited an expression of St. Basil the Great: "we dance with sacred dances (*entheois choreiais* [ἐνθέοις χορείαις]) at the tombs of the saints" (cited in F. C. Conybeare, *Philo: About the Contemplative Life*, Oxford, 1895, p. 154: *Bas.*, t. 1, p. 512 B). In Abyssinian worship to this day dancing has been preserved at molebens and processions of the cross, in which the priests also take some part (B. Turayev, "Abissiniya," in *Pravoslavnaya bogoslovskaya entsiklopediya*, vol. I, St. Petersburg, 1899, col. 71). Orthodox Arabs in Jerusalem also permit it around holy shrines (A. N. Muravyov, *Puteshestviye ko svyatym mestam* [*Journey to the Holy Places*], part 2, St. Petersburg, 1849, p. 124). Thus all these were religious excesses, occurring only in such enthusiastic localities as Asia Minor and Armenia, and never approved by the Church. St. John Chrysostom regards dancing as a demonic enterprise (*Hom. in Matt.* 48). St. Gregory the Theologian advises dancing as David did, understanding David's dance in an allegorical sense (*Oration* 5). Bl. Augustine expresses indignation at the former dances in the martyrium of St. Cyprian and directly before the basilicas of the saints (*Sermon on the Feast of St. Cyprian*; *Sermon* 215 [265]). The Council of Carthage (397), Canon 71, prohibited "abominable dances (*orcheseis mysaras* [ὀρχήσεις μυσαράς]) performed in sacred places, even on the days of commemoration of the blessed martyrs." The Council of Laodicea (late 4th cent.), Canon 53, forbids even at weddings to leap or dance (*ballizein ē orchesthai* [βαλλίζειν ἢ ὀρχεῖσθαι]). In view of such an attitude toward religious dancing in the Church and such rarity of it among ancient Christians, one can hardly see a ritual survival of it in the circular procession during Baptism, Marriage, and Chirotony (Konovalov, p. 723).

[^462]: *Ibid.*, ch. 18. This rite even under the apostles had very wide application during divine worship, judging from 1 Pet. 5:14, 2 Cor. 13:12, and others. Under Tertullian they still kissed at prayer without distinction of sex: "what pagan will permit his wife to approach one or another of the brethren for a kiss?" (*Ad Uxorem*, II, 4). Yet in the *Apostolic Constitutions* men kiss men, and women women (VIII, 15). The intermediate link between both practices is found in St. Ambrose: the deacon kisseth the deaconess, and she the women (*Epistle* 6 *Ad mulieres*).

The tracing of the cross upon the forehead with a finger, as the simplest form of the sign of the cross, must have been, as is evident from the present passage, its primitive form (now preserved in the Roman Catholic Mass: by the priest before the reading of the Gospel). Of this form St. John Chrysostom likewise maketh mention (*Homily on Christ Being God*); yet unto him the sign of the cross upon the lips and breast is already known (*Homilies on Matthew*, Hom. 87 [88]), as likewise unto Blessed Jerome (*Funeral Oration on Paula*, Ep. 108 [27]). The formula "In the name of the Father..." is found first in St. Ephrem the Syrian (*On the Whole Armor*); it arose from the commandment concerning baptism (Matt. 28:19). Cf. X. Schmid, *Der christkatholische Kultus*, Passau, 1840, vol. I, p. 245.

[^514]: It is commonly thought that by "stations" (*stationes*) in Tertullian and writers of that era is meant the fast of Wednesday and Friday. But Tertullian calls "station" also the three-week fast of the Montanists (*De Jejuniis*, ch. 19), and neither he nor other writers apply this term exclusively unto either fast, but unto fasts in general. Hermas, unto the Shepherd's question: "Why hast thou come hither (unto the mountain) so early?" answers: "Because, sir, I am keeping a station..." "And what is a station?" he asked. "That is, I am fasting, sir, said I" (*Shepherd of Hermas*, Similitude 5). Only the following passages from Tertullian might speak in favor of such a narrow meaning of the term "station": "we dedicate unto stations the fourth and sixth day of the week" (*De Jejuniis*, 19); and concerning a Christian carried away by military service: "and stations doth he perform, either rather unto someone else than unto Christ, or on the Lord's day, when (they are performed) in any case not unto Christ" (*De Corona Militis*, 11).

[^527]: One cannot therefore, with certain Western scholars, suspect the authenticity of all mentions of the Forty-Day Fast (Четыредесятница / Lent) found in 3rd-century monuments solely on the ground that other monuments of that century do not yet know such a prolonged fast. Thus Achelis in his edition of the Canons of Hippolytus regards the word "40" in the aforementioned passage of this monument as a later insertion (Achelis, *Die Canones Hippolyti*, p. 102). The testimonies of Origen are suspected on the ground that Origen's commentary on the Book of Leviticus, where it is found, hath reached us in the translation of Rufinus († 410). Now Rufinus was a very free translator, which he not only admits, but repeatedly warns the reader of—for example, in the preface unto the translation of Origen's work *De Principiis* [printed: *«Περιὶ ἀοχῶν»*, i.e. *Περὶ ἀρχῶν*]. In the postscript unto the translation of Origen's commentary on the Epistle to the Romans, he remarks concerning his labor: "we supply that which was stated by Origen for the Church of his time not so much for the purpose of explanation as for the purpose of edification, as we did also in the homilies or discourses upon Genesis and Exodus; and especially in the homilies upon the Book of Leviticus, what was spoken by him for beauty of style we render in direct language for clarity; this labor of supplying what was lacking we undertook that the Latin reader might not encounter difficulties from questions raised and left unresolved, as Origen is accustomed to do according unto the requirements of homiletic art." Once such principles were laid at the foundation of the translation, then, as the editor of Origen in Migne's Patrology remarks, the reader can never be certain whether he is reading Origen or Rufinus. True, even so broad a freedom of translation ought not to extend unto such purely factual data in Origen as the indication of the existence in his time of the fast of the Forty Days (Lent). But here they recall Rufinus' translation of Irenaeus' words concerning 40 hours of fasting... Furthermore, in Origen's expression concerning the Forty Days—"the days of the Forty-Day Fast consecrated unto fasting"—attention is drawn unto the inappropriate floral verbosity of the phrase, which doth not answer unto the simplicity of that which followeth it: "we fast both on Wednesday and on Friday." Funk thinks that Origen here was speaking of the forty-day fast of the Savior (F. X. Funk, *Kirchengeschichtliche Abhandlungen und Untersuchungen*, vol. 1, Paderborn, 1897, p. 254).

[^609]: These "tempest-tossed" in the order of the Liturgy in the *Apostolic Constitutions* (VIII, 6) are more precisely designated "those possessed (*energoumenoi*, ἐνεργούμενοι) by unclean spirits." In the era of the monument, consequently, the phenomenon of "demonic possession" had no small dissemination, unless prayers for the "possessed" were then such a relic of antiquity as prayers for the catechumens serve today. Characteristic is the place occupied by this class of worshipers: between the catechumens and those being illuminated; the catechumens prior to exorcism were likewise considered to be under the influence of unclean spirits, like all pagans.

[^613]: The monument was discovered by the Italian archaeologist Gian Francesco Gamurrini in 1884 in a Latin manuscript of c. the 12th century of the Confraternity of St. Mary in Arezzo. The opening leaves of the work are lost, for which reason neither the title of the work nor the name of the author has been preserved. In the margins of the manuscript in two places in the corner stand the letters *PE*, which are considered an abbreviation of the title. Indirect evidence in the work points unto the end of the 4th century as the time of the origin of the *Peregrinatio*: the Jerusalem edifices are described in such a manner that Constantine's structures can be recognized in them; mention is made of a recently built church in Edessa, which occurred in 372 under the Emperor Valens; of the Persian possession of the left bank of the Tigris, which was ceded unto them by the Emperor Jovian in 363; of the Thebaid ascetics (among them St. Anthony the Great, † 356, and St. Pachomius, † 340); on the other hand, the traveler is unacquainted with Jerome's translation of the Holy Scriptures, or the decree of Pope Gelasius of 494 against the reading of apocrypha—the Letter of Abgar, the Acts of the Holy Apostle Thomas, and of the Holy Martyr Thecla; Justinian's buildings in Sinai and Constantinople are not mentioned. The pilgrim came unto the Holy Land *de extremis porro terris* (from the distant ends of the earth); judging by the fact that she compares the Euphrates with the Rhône (*Rhodanus*), she was presumably a native of Gaul; she was an educated and well-read woman; she travels as a noble personage—with a retinue; she is provided with a military escort; bishops receive and accompany her. In Palestine she remained three years, dwelling in Jerusalem and making excursions from thence. Having returned unto Constantinople, she described her pilgrimage unto the "sisters" (fleshly sisters or nuns?). The identification of the pilgrim by some with Galla Placidia, daughter of Theodosius the Great, and by others with Sylvia, sister of the Prefect of Constantinople Rufinus (an Aquitanian), is rather arbitrary. The monument is, of course, of invaluable liturgical importance, yet one cannot but regret that it belongs unto a foreigner and a woman: the pilgrim observes only ceremonies, litanies, how they approach the bishop's hand, and the like, while concerning the content of divine worship she restricts herself unto the remarks: "psalms and hymns were sung," "prayers were read." Nevertheless the order of divine worship is presented rather distinctly. The monument was published in the *Pravoslavny Palestinsky Sbornik*, fascicle 20, 1889, from a transcript made directly from the manuscript itself, and provided with a Russian translation and preface (by I. V. Pomialovsky), whence the present data concerning it are also drawn (pp. I–IX).

[^614]: *Peregrinatio*, § 37: "*Ante crucem...* This is an open place (*subdivanus*), that is, as it were a very large and rather beautiful courtyard (*atrium*), situated between the Cross and the Resurrection (*Anastasis*)."

[^615]: *In ecclesia majore, quae appellatur Martyrio, quae in Golgotha post crucem* (*Peregrinatio*, § 27).

[^616]: *Peregrinatio*, § 30. From it is distinguished the locality *post crucem*: "after the dismissal in the Martyrium they go behind the Cross" (§ 35). All these churches were built and richly adorned by the Emperor Constantine (§ 25).

[^617]: *Peregrinatio*, § 25 ff. N. F. Krasnoseltsev, "The Divine Services of the Church of Jerusalem at the End of the 4th Century" (*Bogosluzhenie Ierusalimskoy tserkvi v kontse IV v.*), *Pravoslavny Sobesednik*, 1888, II, p. 355.

[^618]: *Peregrinatio*, § 25.

[^619]: It is unknown whether *missa* signifies the dismissal or the Liturgy, which is designated by the pilgrim at times as *oblatio*, *offertur*, and rarely *sacramentum*. The name *missa* was adopted for the Liturgy in the West either from the solemn formula of dismissal therein: "*Ite, missa est*", or from the fact that it is celebrated after the dismissal of the catechumens and constitutes the conclusion of lengthy church services. If in the pilgrim *missa* signifies the Liturgy, then daily in the Church of Jerusalem two Liturgies were celebrated—one after Matins, and another after Vespers (on Great Thursday and Holy Saturday two Liturgies—*oblatio*—morning and evening, are clearly indicated by the pilgrim, yet in different churches: *Peregrinatio*, §§ 35, 38), inasmuch as the description of Vespers in the pilgrim also concludes with the words *missa fit* (the opinion concerning two Liturgies was expressed by G. F. Gamurrini, *S. Hilarii tractatus de mysteriis et hymni et S. Silviae Aquitanae peregrinatio*, Rome, 1887, pp. 77–78). If, however, *missa* here signifies the dismissal, then on weekdays there was no Liturgy (so thinks A. de Waal, "Die Feste des Kirchenjahres zu Jerusalem gegen Ende des IV. Jahrhunderts," *Römische Quartalschrift für christliche Alterthumskunde und für Kirchengeschichte*, 1887, no. 4, p. 313; N. F. Krasnoseltsev, "The Divine Services of the Church of Jerusalem at the End of the 4th Century," *Pravoslavny Sobesednik*, 1888, II, pp. 351, 359).

[^620]: *Peregrinatio*, § 24.

[^702]: An example of a responsory in the Roman Catholic office: at Matins, following the customary introductory prayers recited silently, "the verse (*versiculum*) is spoken aloud: **'O Lord, Thou shalt open my lips.'** Responsory: **'And my mouth shall declare Thy praise'** (Ps. 50:17 [LXX; Masoretic Ps. 51:15]). Verse: **'O God, attend unto my help.'** Responsory: **'O Lord, make haste to help me'** (Ps. 69:2 [LXX; Masoretic Ps. 70:1]). **'Glory be to the Father...'** (*Breviarium Romanum*, 2). The priest recites the verse, and the singer or choir executes the responsory."

[^709]: An example of antiphons in the Roman Catholic Church: at Sunday Matins, at the beginning of the first nocturn before the division of the first six psalms, the antiphon is: **"Serve the Lord"**; after this division of psalms: first antiphon: **"Serve the Lord with fear, and rejoice unto Him with trembling"**; second antiphon: **"God is a righteous judge."** On feasts and fasts other antiphons are appointed (*Breviarium Romanum*, 3). Such antiphons in Roman Catholic worship occupy as broad a place as stichera and canons do among us, and they chiefly adapt the office unto feasts; whereas hymns of Christian composition are very few there: three or four for each feast.

[^816]: Concerning the acclamation "Glory to Thee, O Lord" before the reading of the Gospel from the 4th and 5th centuries there are no reliable testimonies. In a homily on the circus or hippodrome, formerly attributed unto St. John Chrysostom, it is stated: "When the deacon beginneth the course (*dromou*) of the readings, we immediately arise, proclaiming: 'Glory to Thee, O Lord'." In the Mozarabic Liturgy (the order of which has been preserved more or less unchanged from the 6th–7th centuries), unto the naming of the prophecy and the epistle the people respond: "Thanks be to God" (*Deo gratias*). Thus originally and in certain localities the acclamation "Glory to Thee, O Lord" or the like accompanied the reading of other scriptures as well, besides the Gospel. In the Mozarabic Liturgy and in the Rule of St. Benedict of the 6th century (ch. 11), after the reading of the Gospel the people pronounce "Amen," which, according unto the explanation of other commentators, possesses here the sense: "May God grant us firmly to abide in the evangelical teaching." Bingham, *Origines Ecclesiasticae*, book XIV, ch. 3, § 3 (vol. IV, p. 82).

[^902]: Palladius, Bishop of Helenopolis, *Historia Lausiaca* (*The Lausiac History*), ch. 28 (according unto the Russian translation, St. Petersburg 1850, ch. 25). This collection of narratives concerning the ascetics of the 4th century, written by a friend of St. John Chrysostom unto the imperial *praepositus sacri cubiculi* (grand chamberlain) Lausus c. 420, is one of the most valuable and trustworthy documents concerning ancient monastic asceticism. Closely related unto it in places is the *Historia Monachorum in Aegypto*, inscribed with the name of Rufinus, presbyter of Aquileia († 410), who was merely the translator of this work of an anonymous author; this monument is somewhat older than the preceding (402–404) and in places entered into its composition.

[^907]: *Apophthegmata Patrum* (*Sayings of the Holy Fathers*), Anthony, § 1 (Ἀποφθέγματα τῶν ἁγίων γερόντων; J. B. Cotelerius, *Ecclesiae Graecae Monumenta*, Paris 1677, vol. I; Migne, *PG*, t. 65 [in Russian source mistakenly t. 85]; Russian translation under the title: *Memorable Narratives Concerning the Asceticism of the Holy and Blessed Fathers*, Moscow 1845; the monument belongs probably unto the 5th century, for sayings of fathers later than the 5th century are not adduced). [^913]: *Apophthegmata Patrum*, Poemen, § 186.

[^914]: Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 128 (145), and many others. St. Isidore († 491) said: "it is not so harmful for a man to eat meat as to become proud and puffed up" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Isidore, § 4). In other Christian lands abstinence from meat among monastics was not so unconditional as in Egypt: according unto the words of St. Epiphanius, "some of them (monks) abstain from all meats, both of quadrupeds and of birds, and from fish, and from eggs, and from cheese; others only from quadrupeds, permitting the use of birds and the rest; others abstain also from birds, using only cheese and fish; others taste not even fish, but only cheese; while others eat not even cheese" (*Expositio fidei*, § 23).

[^915]: "It happened that certain fathers came unto the house of a Christ-loving man, among whom was also Abba Poemen. At table meat was set before them. All began to eat except Abba Poemen (5th c.). The elders, knowing his discernment, marveled why he did not eat. When they arose from the table, they said unto him: 'Thou art Poemen, yet what hast thou done?' The elder answered them: 'Forgive me, fathers. You ate, and no one was scandalized; but if I should eat, many brethren coming unto me would be scandalized and say: Poemen ate meat; why then should not we also eat?' And the elders marveled at his discernment" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Poemen, § 170). The "fathers" who visited Theophilus, Archbishop of Alexandria, ate calf meat offered unto them; but when Theophilus, handing a piece of meat unto the elder next to him, said: "here is a good piece, eat, abba," the elders said: "until now we thought we were eating vegetables; if this be meat, we will not eat"; and not one of them ate any more (*Ancient Patericon Arranged by Chapters*, IV, 75 (63). This collection, closely related unto the *Sayings of the Holy Fathers* but somewhat younger than it, is described by Patriarch Photius in his *Bibliotheca*, codex 198, under the title Ἀνδρῶν ἁγίων βίβλος. It was translated into Latin in the 6th century by Pelagius and John, Roman deacons; the translation was published by H. Rosweyde, *De vitis et verbis seniorum*, Antwerp 1628, and in Migne, *PL*, t. 73, and is preserved in Greek in an 11th–12th century manuscript of the Moscow Synodal Library, No. 782, etc. The Greek text in places differs from the Latin; from it was made the Russian translation published by the Athonite St. Panteleimon Monastery, Moscow 1891; the numeral in parentheses indicates the section according unto the Latin edition).

[^916]: "Clerics forewarned Abba Simon (presumably of the 5th c., of Sinai): 'Abba, prepare thyself, for a certain magistrate, having heard concerning thee, is coming to receive a blessing from thee.'—'Well, I will prepare myself,' said Simon. Putting on a *kenton* (a garment fashioned of multicolored rags) and taking into his hands bread and cheese, the abba sat at the door and ate. The magistrate came with his retinue; beholding the elder, those who had come looked upon him with disdain, saying: 'Is this the hermit concerning whom we heard?' And they immediately departed" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Simon, § 2).

[^917]: Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 128 (145), on St. Candida. St. Hor († c. 390) and Aphre tested each other's obedience with fish: at the wish of the latter, the former praised a spoiled fish stew and declared poorly cooked a well-prepared fish (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Pistus, § 1).

[^918]: "A brother asked Abba Xoi: 'If I drink three cups (*poteria*) of wine, is it not too much?' The abba answered: 'If there is no devil, it is not too much'" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Xoi, § 1). "Unto Abba Xanthias on account of his arduous labor they brought a little wine. A possessed person was brought unto him. The demon began to revile the elder: 'Unto this wine-bibber have you brought me!' The elder did not wish to cast out the demon, but because of his reviling said unto him: 'I trust in Christ that before I finish drinking this cup, thou shalt depart.' And as soon as the elder began to drink, the demon cried out: 'Thou burnest me, thou burnest me!' And before the elder had finished the cup, the demon went out through the operation of the grace of Christ" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Xanthias, § 2). Even St. Sisoes did not refuse wine; but when a third cup was offered him, "the abba accepted it not, saying: 'Desist, brother; knowest thou not that there is a Satan?'" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Sisoes, § 8).

[^919]: St. Hyperechius equates wine with meat: "it is better to eat meat and drink wine than to devour the flesh of a brother through slander" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Hyperechius, § 4; *Ancient Patericon*, IV, 59 (51)). When unto a certain elder at a festal meal a cup of wine was offered, he said: "take away from me this death" (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 63 (53)). Peter Pionita, a Sinaitic ascetic of the 5th century, would not allow himself in old age even a slight mingling of wine with water (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 33 (35)). St. Paphnutius (4th–5th c.), who drank no wine his entire life, when he drank it at the demand of a brigand chieftain, thereby converted him unto repentance along with his whole band (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Paphnutius, § 2). "Certain persons recounted unto Abba Poemen (5th c.) concerning a monk that he drank no wine; the elder answered: 'Wine is not a thing for monks at all'" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Poemen, § 10). St. Athanasius the Great, reproaching the monks, said: "we see bishops who drink no wine, and monks who drink" (*Epistle to Dracontius*).

[^920]: Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 73 (86), etc. Water at times had to be gathered from dew, as in the case of St. Onuphrius: *Veterum patrum analecta nova*, Venice 1781, pp. 122–127.

[^921]: Abba Agathon († 435) had two disciples who lived separately in the desert. One day he asked one of them: "How livest thou in thy cell?" He answered: "I fast until evening, and then I eat two rusks (*paxamatia*)." The elder said unto him: "Thy manner of life is good and not overly arduous" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Agathon, § 20). St. Megethius of Sinai, a disciple of Saints Sisoes and Poemen of Egypt, ate one loaf of bread every two days (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Megethius, § 2). The bread used was for the most part barley rusk (*paxamos*), which can keep for half a year and was commonly used by the poor population of Egypt (St. Athanasius the Great, *Vita Antonii*, § 12); they ate it soaked in water (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 68 (56)). Freshly baked bread, which could be had only by hermits living near villages or monasteries, was considered a luxury, and unto it, as unto a treat, neighbors were invited (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 76 (64)). Cooked food, for the most part in the form of lentil porridge, was prepared only for guests (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 68 (56), 69 (57)). A certain elder refused to eat porridge made from sebesten tree plums/buds (*myxaria*) even during severe hemorrhaging (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 77 (59)). Another "elder during illness was visited by a brother, who prepared a cooked meal for him from his own provisions and offered him to eat. The elder said unto him: 'In truth, brother, I had forgotten that men have such consolation'" (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 78 (66)). Concerning St. Isaac it was recounted that he ate his bread with ashes from the liturgical censer (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Isaac, § 6). St. Macarius of Alexandria († 394), "hearing that a certain monk ate only one litra (12 ounces) of bread, resolved to imitate him: he broke up all his rusks into pieces and dropped them into a jar with a narrow neck, establishing a rule to eat no more than his hand could draw out. Cheerfully he recounted this unto us (saith the author of the *Lausiac History*): 'I would seize a larger handful, but the narrow neck would not permit me to draw it out; my jar would not let me eat at all.' For three full years he labored in such abstinence, eating only four or five ounces of bread and drinking a proportionate quantity of water" (Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 19).

[^922]: Concerning St. Evagrius Ponticus, deacon († 399), it is recounted in the *Lausiac History*: "This blessed man said: 'From the time that I came into the (Egyptian) desert, I have eaten neither lettuce, nor greens, nor any fresh herbs, nor tree fruit, nor grapes; I have not washed my body; I have eaten neither meat, nor bread, nor wine, nor in general anything prepared over fire, except for certain raw herbs and a small quantity of water.' At length, after he had lived in this manner sixteen years without taking cooked food, and when his body was weakened and his stomach disordered, he felt the need of food prepared over fire and began to eat bread; but even during the last two years he ate no cooked vegetables, save only cabbage and pulse" (Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 73 (86)). "St. Macarius of Alexandria resolved for seven years to eat nothing prepared over fire, but nourished himself solely upon raw vegetables, or when it happened, soaked pulse; nothing else did he taste during those seven years; having fulfilled his vow, he nevertheless laid aside such a manner of life" (*Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 19). Heron, a young Egyptian ascetic (a younger contemporary of Evagrius), who soon fell into delusion and repented only before death, "often took food only once in three months, being content with the reception of the Holy Mysteries alone, and if perchance he came upon some wild herb" (*Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 32 (30)).

[^923]: St. Candida "did not eat the flesh of animals; she ate only fish, oil, and vegetables, and this only on feast days; at all other times she was content with water mingled with vinegar, and dry bread" (Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 128 (145)). The aforementioned St. Evagrius, prior to settling in the deep desert, lived for fourteen years in the monastery of the Cells (*Kellia*), and throughout all this time consumed a litra of bread daily, and in three months expended a sextarius (ξέστης—one-sixth of a congius) of oil (*Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 86 (73)). St. Macarius also during his three-year fast upon bread alone expended one-sixth of a congius of oil (*Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 19). St. Dorotheus ate six ounces of bread daily and a bundle of greens, and drank a little water (*Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 2). St. Elias of Thebes in old age ate three ounces of bread and three olives daily (*Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 51 (46)). A certain virgin, after sixty years of enclosure, at the bidding of the local martyr Colluthus who appeared unto her, came in the morning on the day of her death unto the meal in his church, having taken in a basket bread and a few greens; and having waited until the ninth hour, the customary time for taking food, when no one was in the church, she partook of these provisions there, returned home after prayer unto the martyr, and died that night (*Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 123 (139)).

[^924]: Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 123 (139) (see above); *Apophthegmata Patrum*, Macarius of Egypt, § 32: two hermit brethren offer a meal unto St. Macarius of Egypt visiting them only at the ninth hour; Silvanus, § 5: a visitor unto St. Silvanus of Sinai expecteth at the ninth hour to be invited unto the table; cf. Zeno, § 8. Among hermits this was a general rule equally for fasting and non-fasting days. "A brother asked Abba Matoës: 'What should I do if on a fast day, or at dawn, a brother cometh unto me? This troubleth me.' The elder answered him: 'If without qualm of conscience thou eatest with thy brother, thou doest well; but if thou expectest no one, yet eatest, in this is already thine own will'" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Matoës, § 6). But in monasteries, especially in later coenobia, the taking of food no earlier than the ninth hour was made a rule only for fasting days: *Apophthegmata Patrum*, Matoës, § 5; *Ancient Patericon*, IX, 16, 12; X, 37 (30). The following passage from the *Ancient Patericon* scarcely testifieth unto an earlier taking of food: "A certain brother hungered one morning and wrestled with his thought not to eat until the third hour; when the third hour arrived, he resolved to endure until the sixth hour; when the sixth hour arrived, he soaked his bread, and sitting down to eat, arose again and said: 'I will endure until the ninth hour'; the ninth hour arrived, and the elder, having made prayer, saw the power of the devil departing like smoke from his bosom; and thus his hunger passed away" (IV, 70 (58)). Nor does the following incident concerning St. Sisoes testify unto taking food on non-fasting days in the morning: he persuaded Adelphius, Bishop of Nicopolis, visiting him with companions, to take food for the road in the morning, and this was during a fast. When the table was set, lo, brethren knocked at the door; Abba Sisoes said unto his disciple: "Give them a little porridge, for they are weary." "Leave it," Abba Adelphius said unto him, "lest they say that Abba Sisoes eateth in the morning" (in general, and all the more so during a fast). It turned out that the brethren who knocked begged that Sisoes not be compelled to eat, lest on that account he subsequently afflict himself with several days of total fasting (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Sisoes, § 13).

[^931]: Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 19, concerning Macarius of Alexandria; *Apophthegmata Patrum*, Serapion, § 1, concerning an unknown former harlot.

[^932]: *Apophthegmata Patrum*, Serapion, § 1, concerning the same woman.

[^933]: "A certain elder came unto another elder. The latter said unto his disciple: 'Cook us some lentils.' He cooked them and soaked bread (rusk) for them. They remained in discourse concerning spiritual matters until the sixth hour of the following day. The elder said again unto his disciple: 'Child, cook us a few lentils.'—'I cooked them yesterday already,' the disciple answered. And arising, they began to eat" (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 68 (56)). "Once Abba Macarius the Citizen (of Alexandria) went out to cut palm leaves. The brethren said unto him on the first day: 'Come and eat with us.' The elder went and ate with them. On the next day they invited him again to eat; but he went not, and answered them: 'It is necessary for you, children, to eat, for you are still flesh; but I at present do not desire to eat'" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Macarius the Citizen [Alexandrinus], § 1).

[^934]: "Abba Joseph asked Abba Poemen (5th c.): 'How ought one to fast?' Abba Poemen answered him: 'In mine opinion, one ought to eat every day, but eat little, not unto satiety.'—'How then, abba, didst thou in thy youth fast for two days together?' said Abba Joseph. 'In truth I fasted for three days, and for four, and even for a week,' answered the elder; 'but the fathers tested all these things, as being mighty in virtue, and found that it is better to eat every day a little, and they showed us the royal road, for it is more manageable for us'" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Poemen, § 186; also in *Ancient Patericon*, IX, 59 (44)). Unto Megethius, a contemporary of Poemen, who ate one loaf every two days, a "holy man" who visited him advised him to eat half a loaf every day: "and doing thus, Megethius found peace" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Megethius, § 2).

[^935]: Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 19.

[^936]: *Apophthegmata Patrum*, Paul the Great, § 3.

[^937]: Rufinus, *Historia Monachorum in Aegypto*, ch. 7; Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 47 (52).

[^938]: St. Arsenius († 450) "passed the entire night in vigil, and when toward morning nature urged him to sleep, he would say unto sleep: 'Come hither, evil servant'; and having slept a little while sitting, he would immediately arise" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Arsenius, § 14). He said that for a monk, if he be an ascetic, it is sufficient to sleep for one hour (*ibid.*, § 15). St. Bessarion († 466) "for forty years did not lie down upon his side, but slept sitting or standing" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Bessarion, § 8). "God is my witness," recounteth Palladius concerning St. Dorotheus of Thebes, "I never saw him stretch out his legs, or lie down to sleep upon a mat or upon a bed; throughout the entire night he would sit and plait ropes. And from youth for sixty years he lived thus; he never lay down to sleep, but only perchance during work or at table would close his eyes for a few minutes, so that even a morsel of food would sometimes fall from his mouth during his slumber" (Palladius, *Historia Lausiaca*, ch. 2).

[^939]: St. Isaac (5th c.) said: "our fathers and Abba Pambo († after 386) wore old, ragged garments made of palm leaves" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Isaac of the Cells, § 7). Saints Pambo and Isaac said: "a monk ought to wear such clothing as no one would pick up, even if it were cast out of the cell for three full days" (*Apophthegmata Patrum*, Pambo, § 6; Isaac of the Cells, § 12).

[^977]: Under the name of St. Anthony, however, written rules have also come down unto us. They are cited by Benedict of Aniane (9th c.) in his *Codex regularum*; in a somewhat different recension they were discovered in an Arabic manuscript in the 17th century (both recensions published in Migne, *PG*, t. 40; Russian translation in *Khristianskoye Chteniye*, 1880, part 37). Yet the authenticity of these rules is doubtful, because St. Athanasius and Blessed Jerome make no mention of them, and because written rules for monks would conflict with St. Anthony's general outlook upon asceticism; the rules, moreover, are wholly devoid of the spirit of St. Anthony; they do not reflect the theological views of St. Anthony (demonology, polemics against the Arians); and they presuppose a monastic order far too complex for the era of Anthony: subordination unto a hegumen, a church building, assemblies of the brethren, monastic lands, monastic habit with symbolic significance, and the like. These "Rules" represent a compilation of the monastic typika of Saints Pachomius, Isaiah, and others, interwoven with thoughts of St. Anthony. Lobachevsky, *St. Anthony*, pp. 168ff.

[^1007]: Under such a way of life, complete poverty in the monastery is not surprising: "A certain nobleman from a certain land brought with him much gold and entreated the presbyter of Scete to distribute it unto the brethren. The presbyter said: 'The brethren have no need.' But since he pressed the presbyter earnestly, the latter set a basket by the church doors and said: 'Whosoever hath need, let him take.' Yet none approached it, and others paid no heed whatever. Then the presbyter said: 'God hath accepted thine alms; go and distribute it unto the poor'" (*Ancient Patericon*, VI, 23 [19]).

[^1008]: Concerning the agape in Scete, in *Apophthegmata Patrum*, Isaiah (5th c.), § 4: "When at the agape meal the brethren were eating in church and conversing with one another, a presbyter from Pelusium, rebuking them, said: 'Keep silence, brethren! for I saw a brother eating with you and drinking with you the same cups, and his prayer ascendeth unto heaven like fire.'"

[^1009]: A brother asked Abba Sisoes (who had begun his ascetic labors in Scete): "'What shall I do? When I come unto church, an agape meal often taketh place, and they detain me at it.' The elder said unto him: 'It is a hard matter!' Abraham, his disciple, likewise said unto him: 'If it happen that there is an agape on Saturday or Sunday and a brother drinketh three cups (*potēria*, that is, of wine), would that be too much?' The elder replied: 'Were there no Satan, it would not be much'" (*Ancient Patericon*, IV, 45 [37]).

[^1010]: Cassian and Germanus were thus entertained (ca. 390–400) in Scete by Abba Serenus after the Sunday service: "Instead of brine (*muria*, probably water with salt, from *mare*—sea), which was served daily with the addition of a drop of oil, he prepared a little broth (*liquamen*), pouring into it a little more oil than usual; for this trifle of oil each one poureth in daily (a mitigation of the former regime?) not in order to afford thereby any sweetness unto the throat, because they pour in so little of it that it is scarcely enough, I say not to lubricate, but even to reach the gullet, but in order by this consumption to subdue vainglory of heart. Then he served three olives each, seasoned with salt. Afterwards he brought a basket with roasted chickpeas (*cicer frictum*), which they call dessert (*trogalia*), from which we took only six grains (*grana*) each, two plums (*sebesten*, *mixaria*), and one dried fig (*carica*); to take more than this number is accounted blameworthy in that desert." John Cassian, *Conferences*, VIII, 1.

[^1014]: P. Kazansky, *History of Orthodox Monasticism in the East* (*Istoriya pravoslavnogo monashestva na Vostoke*), part 2, Moscow, 1865, p. 152. The order of life in these three principal monasteries of ancient Christian Egypt was so simple that it could not be defined by a written typikon. For this reason and by internal evidence one must recognize as spurious the rules for monks allegedly established by an assembly of four Nitrian and Scetic fathers (Serapion, the two Macarii, and Paphnutius) (Migne, *PG*, t. 34, cols. 971–978), the collection of which is divided into four parts, each part being ascribed unto one of these fathers. The most characteristic of these rules are: "Let there be one superior over all the brethren; let none depart from his counsel and command, but let everyone obey him with joy, as God Himself. In the church assembly let none begin the singing of the psalms without the command of the superior; if the superior delay, let him be notified and proceed according unto his instruction. Let those entering the monastery lie before the gates for an entire week. From the 1st hour until the 3rd, time is dedicated unto God; from the 3rd until the 9th, unto work. Everything in the monastery—vessels, tools, and the like—is sacred. A visiting cleric, even of the lowest rank, begins the prayers at divine service."

[^1043]: Sozomen: "The rules permitted each to eat and drink, to work and to fast or not to fast, as much as each was able, and commanded that upon those who ate more the more difficult labors be laid, while upon the ascetics, lighter ones." Dionysius: "Permit each to eat and drink according unto his strength, and in proportion unto nourishment constrain him to labor; forbid neither moderate eating nor fasting; give the stronger and those who eat heavier labors, and the weaker and abstinent lighter ones." Arabic: "Permit each to eat and drink what shall be needful for him; hinder them not from fasting, but leave this unto their free choice, and give unto those strong in body heavy and burdensome labors, and unto those weak and pious, light and unburdensome ones." In the Arabic this rule standeth in the second place, while in the first place is that concerning cells (which followeth in the Greek *Lausiac History*).

[^1053]: In Dionysius the letters *alpha*, *zeta*, *lambda*, *rho*, *sigma* are additionally adduced as examples, yet without indication of their significance. In the Arabic recension: "and as for the letter *rho*, beware of it and act in accordance with the sign that it representeth."

[^1055]: Sozomen: "Permit not a stranger to eat together with them, unless some traveler be received." Arabic: "Receive not a brother coming from another monastery if he hath in general acquired habits not identical with thine; but receive strangers, comfort them, and show mercy unto the poor in a special place of thy monastery, so as not to eat or drink with them."

Dionysius: "there shall be 12 day prayers (*diurnae orationes*), 12 evening prayers, and 12 night prayers." Arabic: "let each of the brethren make 12 prayers in the course of the day; and at the evening hour, when they assemble to take food, let each of them make three prayers before eating, and six before sleep, and with every prayer a psalm. Let them sleep half the night, and let them spend the other half keeping vigil, for the sake of unceasing prayer and the glorification of God."

[^1061]: Arabic: "these prayers are not few for those who have spent but a little time in such a life, lest prayer become wearisome unto them."

[^1062]: Dionysius: "remaining in their cells, they pray without ceasing." Arabic: "and I know that their days and nights shall be employed in accordance with the laws of God."

[^1063]: Let us recall this practice depicted on pp. 202–210 and we shall become convinced that each rule as it were lowereth its individual requirements by several degrees. From such a point of view the rule concerning sleep will likewise not appear petty (see I. Troitsky, *Review of Sources for the Primary History of Egyptian Monasticism* [*Obozrenie istochnikov nachal'noy istorii egipetskogo monashestva*], p. 62); in place of sleep in a standing or sitting posture, sleep in a semi-reclining posture is permitted. Most strange appeareth the rule concerning the division of the monks into classes according unto the number of the letters of the Greek alphabet. One may regard this rule as a distinctive poetic (with mystical coloring) representation of the pastoral care of the superior of the monastery for the brethren.

[^1065]: The Arabic recension of the typikon defineth the number of prayers only in place of the Liturgy with its Hours ("day prayers"), the 9th Hour ("before food"), and Vespers ("before sleep"), appointing for each service a number of prayers proportional unto its importance: 12, 6, 3; whereas the night office it defineth not by the quantity of prayers, but by their duration: half the night.

[^1068]: The not entirely clear expression concerning this latter "prayer" hath, probably, such a meaning: if the meal on a given day is not common, but separate for each "order" or class of monks, for each house of the monastery, then prior unto it there is chanted one psalm with prayer; whereas if it is common, for several orders (houses), then as many psalms with prayer are chanted as there are of these "orders" participating in the meal. Thus the meal was prefaced by an office in composition not different from other offices; cf. among us the Order of the Panagia; see the explanation of Chapter 2 of the Typikon, end.

The beginning of asceticism in Syria was laid, according to Sozomen, by a certain Aones, a contemporary of the Emperor Valens (364–378): "It is said that this Aones in Syria, like Anthony in Egypt, before all men laid the foundation of strict philosophy" (*Ecclesiastical History*, VI, 33 [al. III, 14]). Yet now it is demonstrated that even earlier—prior unto 325—St. James, Bishop of Nisibis, labored here in ascetical feats, and there even existed, apparently, entire monastic communities under the name of the "Sons of the Covenant" (*B'nai Qyama*). See Hieromonk Anatoly, "On the Beginning and Origin of Syrian Monasticism" (*O nachale i proiskhozhdenii siriyskogo monashestva*), *TKDA*, 1910, No. 4, p. 393.

[^1138]: Thus St. Baradatus "fashioned from wood a small box disproportionate unto his body and dwelt therein continually in a bent posture" (Blessed Theodoret of Cyrrhus, *History of the Lovers of God* [*Historia Religiosa / Ἡ φιλόθεος ἱστορία*], ch. 27; Migne, *PG*, t. 82, cols. 1481–1488; Russian trans., St. Petersburg, 1853: the work consists of the lives of all Syrian ascetes known unto the author and contemporary with him [† 457]). Likewise St. Marcian (*ibid.*, ch. 3; *PG*, t. 82, col. 1329). St. Eusebius placed an iron chain upon his neck and connected it unto a similar girdle, so that, "being thus bent double, he was continually constrained to look upon the ground" (*ibid.*, ch. 4; *PG*, t. 82, col. 1345).

[^1139]: St. James, a contemporary of Blessed Theodoret, "possessed neither hut nor tent, nor would he make use of the shelter of a tree, but dwelt in an entirely open field, where rain drenched him, snow covered him, frost stiffened him, and the sun scorched him" (*ibid.*, ch. 21; *PG*, t. 82, col. 1432). Many Syrian ascetes lived in this manner (*ibid.*, ch. 27; *PG*, t. 82, col. 1484).

[^1140]: St. Salaman (end of the IV c.) lived in a "refuge" lacking windows or entrance, into which one could creep only beneath the wall, emerging therefrom only once a year to gather a supply of provisions (*ibid.*, ch. 19; *PG*, t. 82, cols. 1428–1429).

[^1141]: St. Thalalaeus, a contemporary of Blessed Theodoret, "made two wheels two cubits in diameter and fastened them together with planks separated one from another by considerable intervals; then, having fixed three long poles in the ground and joined their upper ends with timbers, he suspended from them the said cage of two wheels and planks and took up his abode therein; and because the interior space of the cage hath a height of only two cubits and a width of one, while the blessed man is very tall of stature, he sitteth therein not in an upright posture, but continually bent double with his head bowed unto his knees" (*ibid.*, ch. 28; *PG*, t. 82, col. 1489).

[^1247]: The author of the *Historical Discourse on the Fasts of the Orthodox Eastern Catholic Church* (Moscow 1837) (Hieromonk Alexis Soloviev) suspecteth the testimonies of Socrates and Sozomen concerning the diverse duration and time of the pre-Paschal fast of inaccuracy and even of deliberate distortion of the truth, explaining the latter by their inclination toward Novatianism: "The positive ordinances of the Church could not please them; thence it certainly arose that it was deemed necessary intentionally and with effort to seek out differences in the rites of the Church, and in doing so to mingle half-truth with truth or with falsehood" (p. 17). Yet of the ordinances of the Church, the forty-day fast was the least likely to displease the Novatians, the Christian rigorists of that day.

When the *Typikon* permitteth two meals in a day, it doth not account this a fast, even if on such a day fish likewise is prohibited: for example, on the Eves of the Nativity of Christ and Theophany occurring on a Saturday or Sunday, it is said: "there is no fast," and yet "at the trapeza we eat cooked food with oil, but fish we do not eat" (*Typikon*, ch. 48, Dec. 24 and Jan. 5).

[^1265]: J. Bingham, *Origines Ecclesiasticae*, lib. XXI, cap. 2, § 14. In confirmation of this Bingham citeth the following narratives of Sozomen and Eusebius: Unto St. Spyridon of Trimythous "a certain traveler entered from his journey during the Forty-Day Fast, when the saint fasted with his household and partook of food only on a designated day, remaining without food on the others. Seeing that the stranger was exceedingly weary, he said unto his daughter: 'Wash this man's feet and set food before him.' When the maiden answered that there was neither bread nor flour—for a store thereof by reason of the fast would have been superfluous—he first prayed and asked forgiveness, and then commanded his daughter to roast some salted pork that happened to be in the house. And when it was roasted, Spyridon, seating the stranger beside him, began to eat the meat set before them and urged the man to imitate him. When the latter, calling himself a Christian, refused, the saint added: 'All the less oughtest thou to refuse, for the word of God (Titus 1:15) hath declared: unto the pure all things are pure'" (Sozomen, *Historia Ecclesiastica*, lib. I, cap. 11). Unto the martyr Attalus there was a revelation concerning his fellow-martyr Alcibiades, who ate nothing save bread and water, that the latter "did not do well in refusing to make use of the creatures of God, and thereby leaving an example of stumbling unto others" (Eusebius, *Historia Ecclesiastica*, lib. V, cap. 3).

[^1338]: Since the lunar year is shorter than the solar year by 11 and 1/4 days, among those who use lunar reckoning (the Semites) an addition of an intercalary month unto the year is made from time to time; to equalize both reckonings it is necessary in 8 lunar years to make 3 intercalary months. The supplementary month among the Jews was added unto the last month of the year, Adar (corresponding unto February), and was called "Veadar," "another Adar." Yet in that epoch such intercalations were not regulated and depended upon the arbitrary discretion of influential rabbis. And if an intercalation was not made in timely fashion, Pascha fell before spring. In the Talmud there is preserved an epistle of Rabbi Gamaliel (the teacher of the Apostle Paul) unto Babylon and Media: "We inform you that since the pigeons (for sacrifice) are still too tender and the lambs too young, and the season of Aviv (= Nisan, the first spring month) hath not yet arrived, we, in agreement with our colleagues, have deemed it necessary to add 30 days unto the year" (Kellner, *Heortologie*, S. 34). For this reason in every year a special announcement was required concerning the advent of Pascha. When the Sanhedrin existed, it dispatched annually throughout all Palestine special messengers with an announcement as to from which day the beginning of the month of Nisan was to be counted and upon which day Pascha would fall; and in order to make this known unto the Euphrates Jews as well, at the Nisan new moon fires were kindled upon the Mount of Olives and following it upon other mountains as far as Babylonia itself (V. Rybinsky, *On Pilgrimage unto Jerusalem in Biblical Times* [*O palomnichestve v Ierusalim v bibleyskoye vremya*], *Trudy Kievskoy Dukhovnoy Akademii* [1909], no. 11, p. 499).

[^1377]: For the feast upon the 40th day after Pascha there is no mention of such a Gospel or in general of any commemoration of the Ascension. It may be that the Ascension and the Descent of the Holy Spirit were celebrated together at Pentecost. In general these two festivals were not yet everywhere segregated. Thus the Council of Elvira (ca. 305 AD) decrees: 'It behooveth to correct the erroneous practice according unto the testimony of the Scriptures and for all to celebrate the day of Pentecost; otherwise whoever acteth not thus shall declare himself the introducer of a new heresy' (Canon 43). An ancient *epitome* (abbreviated collection) of this Council renders this canon thus: 'Following Pascha let the 50th day be observed, and not the 40th' (J. D. Mansi, *Sacrorum Conciliorum Nova et Amplissima Collectio*, t. II, col. 13). It may be that the Montanists, who believed that only in Montanus did the Holy Spirit first appear upon earth, were opposed unto the celebration of Pentecost (K. J. von Hefele, *Conciliengeschichte*, Bd. I, Freiburg im Breisgau, 1855, S. 145).
