[^1400]: The latest and most complete edition in *Monumenta Germaniae Historica*, *Auctores antiquissimi*, t. IX, vol. 1, fasc. 1, Berlin, 1891. The principal sections of the collection are: *Natales Caesarum* (the birthdays of the Caesars), *Fasti consulares* (official calendar) for the year 354, a Paschal Cycle for 312–358 AD, extended by a later hand unto 410 AD, a list of Roman urban prefects unto 354 AD, the *Depositio episcoporum* (the days of the demise of Roman bishops from 255 unto 352 AD), the *Depositio martyrum* (the burial days of Roman martyrs), and a calendar of pagan festivals, inscribed and artistically illuminated by Furius Dionysius Filocalus (known from catacomb inscriptions as the calligrapher of Pope Damasus). The collection, embellished also with numerous illustrations (portraits of Caesars, views of cities), doubtless constituted a reference manual for a high dignitary; it has been preserved in a VII–VIII century manuscript. K. A. H. Kellner, *Heortologie*, S. 92.

[^1401]: St. Ambrose of Milan, *De Virginibus*, lib. III, cap. 1, § 1; Migne, *PL*, t. 16, col. 231.

[^1402]: St. Hippolytus of Rome, *Homilia in Theophanica*; Migne, *PG*, t. 10, col. 852.

[^1403]: Otherwise K. A. H. Kellner, *Heortologie*, S. 99. Scholars determine variously that which gave occasion unto the separation of the festival of the Nativity of Christ from Theophany. It is thought that the cessation of martyrdom exerted a partial influence here, enabling Christians to value more highly earthly life and the nativity thereof; perhaps they desired to establish a Christian festival in opposition unto the pagan feasts celebrating the December solstice. In Rome from December 18 to 23 the Saturnalia were celebrated; in correspondence unto the grim character of the god Saturn or Cronus (who devoured his own children), this was a mournful festival with gladiatorial combats (St. Cyril of Alexandria, *Contra Julianum*, lib. IV); following upon it came the joyful feast of the 'Unconquered Sun' (*Sol Invictus*), celebrated with particular splendor by Emperor Julian in honor of the rising sun and on the threshold of the new year, falling at the season when the turning of the sun had already become noticeable unto all (Emperor Julian, *Oratio IV* [*In Solem Regem*]). In the aforementioned Roman Calendar of Philocalus under December 25 there is noted: '*N(atalis) Invicti*'—'the birthday of the Unconquered': 'Unconquered' was a customary epithet of the gods, especially appropriate with respect unto the nascent sun (it is possible, however, that the birthday of the reigning emperor was so styled in this calendar, since in other months the calendar designates the birthdays of the emperors—Bishop Christopher [F. Smirnov], *The Origin and Significance of the Feast of the Nativity of Christ* [*Proiskhozhdeniye i znacheniye prazdnika Rozhdestva Khristova*], Kyiv, 1883 [al. 1893], pp. 92 et seq.). In Alexandria on December 25 there was the pagan festival of Kikellia; there were festivals in such a season in other countries as well. The supposition hath also been voiced that the Jewish feast of the Dedication of the Temple on the 25th day of the month of Kislev [al. Tebeth] (= December), unto which indications are found in the Prophets Haggai (2:15–18) and Zechariah (8:19; compare John 10:22), and that moreover in connection with Messianic predictions, could likewise have exerted an influence upon the establishment of the festival upon December 25 (F. Smirnov, *ibid.*, pp. 59 et seq.). — When the festival of the Nativity of Christ was assigned unto December 25, that day came to be accounted the day of the historical event itself. Prior unto that time the question concerning the day of the Saviour's birth was disputed. We have seen that Clement of Alexandria indicates, apparently, three opinions existing in his day regarding the day of the Saviour's birth; some assigned it unto Pachon 25 (an Egyptian month, = May 20), others unto Tybi 15 or 11 (January 10 or 6). Compare above the opinion of St. Epiphanius of Cyprus. In the III–IV century treatise *De pascha computus*, attributed unto St. Cyprian of Carthage, March 28 is accounted the day of Christ's Nativity, whereas in the inscription upon the statue of St. Hippolytus of Rome it is March 25. In the portion of St. Hippolytus's *Commentary on the Book of the Prophet Daniel* newly discovered by Georgiades (in Chalki) there is the observation: 'The first manifestation of our Lord in the flesh, wherein He was born in Bethlehem, occurred on December 25 in the 42nd year of Augustus, 5500 years from Adam; and He suffered on March 25 on a Friday, in the 18th year of Tiberius, in the consulate of Rufus and Rubellius'; but in other fragments of the same commentary (for example, in the ancient manuscript of the Chigi Library) and in the quotation of this passage by the Armenian Bishop George from 714 AD, the day of the month is not indicated here, nor is it required by the context of the commentary (K. A. H. Kellner, *Heortologie*, S. 98). Only in the IV and V centuries did fathers begin resolutely to defend the date of December 25 for the Nativity of Christ—St. John Chrysostom (see below) and Blessed Augustine (*Sermones* 190, § 1; 192, § 3; 196, § 1).

[^1434]: Under Roman law, earthly justice was completely satisfied by the death of a criminal, and his corpse was permitted to be surrendered unto relatives or friends (*Digesta*, 48, tit. 24, leg. 1, 3).

[^1435]: These were recorded by the secretary (*notarius*, *commentariensis*, *exceptor*) of the proconsul or procurator, recording the interrogation, sentence, and execution. Christians obtained copies of them for money. There have survived unto our day such official transcripts concerning St. Cyprian, Bishop Fructuosus of Tarragona, Achillius, and others; see T. Ruinart, *Acta martyrum sincera*, Ratisbonae, 1859.

[^1436]: The most ancient and renowned of such records (denominated for the most part *passiones*) are the Epistle of the Churches of Lyons and Vienne unto Asia Minor concerning their martyrs, and the *Passio ss. Perpetuae et Felicitatis* (African martyrs, † 203 AD; commemorated February 1).

[^1437]: According to the account of the *Liber Pontificalis*, Pope St. Clement I (91–100 AD) divided Rome into seven districts (perhaps corresponding unto the fourteen Augustan regions) and appointed a notary over each to collect records concerning the martyrs; Pope Fabian (236–250 AD) set seven subdeacons over them; Pope Anterus (235–236 AD) collected these records with singular zeal and deposited them in the churches (*Liber Pontificalis*, ed. L. Duchesne, t. I, pp. 123, 147, 148; cf. Archbishop Sergius, *The Complete Menologion of the East*, 2nd ed., vol. I, p. 18). In the Church of Carthage, St. Cyprian took care that the days of death of all who suffered for the faith, even in prisons, should be accurately recorded, in order that memorials for them might be celebrated alongside the memorials of the martyrs (*Epistola* 12 [al. 37]).

[^1442]: On the grounds that it containeth a commemoration of 'Arius, presbyter of Alexandria' and Eusebius of Caesarea, and containeth no commemorations of the Fathers of the Nicene Council, and that the martyrology was compiled during the era and in the region of Arian dominance, Western scholars almost unanimously acknowledge it to be Arian (Duchesne, Egli, Kellner). Against this it is objected (Bickell, Bolotov) that Arius here is a martyr, and such a martyr and presbyter indeed existed in Alexandria; that there is no commemoration of Eusebius of Nicomedia (an uncompromising Arian, whereas Eusebius of Caesarea was a semi-Arian); and that there is a commemoration of St. James of Nisibis, a participant in the Nicene Council (V. V. Bolotov, "Traces of the Ancient Menologia of Local Churches," *Khristianskoe Chtenie*, 1893, vol. I, pp. 177 ff.). In the martyrology, evidently, certain martyrs are entered several times; there are corruptions in the names of saints (for instance, *Axitos* instead of *Sixtus*), and especially in the names of cities; the most substantial discrepancy—if indeed it be an error—is that in comparison with the Pseudo-Hieronymian Martyrology the commemorations of June 8–30 are shifted unto the corresponding dates of July (K. A. H. Kellner, *Heortologie*, S. 196 f.).

[^1451]: "Regarding hierarchs or prelates, it must with great probability be accepted that originally they were numbered among the choir of saints or canonized in the same manner as martyrs, that is, *eo ipso* or by the very fact that they were hierarchs; and that subsequently they began to be canonized in the same manner as the venerable ascetics, whom the Church acknowledged as saints not by reason of their belonging unto a particular order, but because personally she accounted one or another worthy of this recognition. Symeon of Thessalonica stateth that in Constantinople 'in the great Church of the Holy Apostles the departed hierarchs of old were laid within the sanctuary [sacrarium/altar], even as holy relics, for the sake of the grace of divine priesthood' (*De ordine sepulturae*, cap. 367)" (E. [^1485]: *Liber Pontificalis*, ed. L. Duchesne, t. I, p. 141. These fasts were a Christian transformation of Roman pagan festivals associated with different seasons of the agricultural year: *feriae sementivae* (the seed-sowing festival), *feriae messis* (the harvest festival), and *feriae vindemiales* (the vintage festival). (For Christian antiquity, fasting and feasting were almost synonymous, which is why the term *feriae* came to signify in the Roman Church initially a fast-day, and subsequently an ordinary weekday; in the Jerusalem Church the Forty Days' Fast was denominated *heortae*—festivals; cf. *Peregrinatio*, attributed to Sylvia [Egeria], § 27.)

Yet even in the ancient Patericon there is an account concerning a solitary who had a wife (*Ancient Patericon*, IX, 19). [^1494]: The name *Bessoi* (βέσσοι), mentioned by Cyril of Scythopolis already in the *Life of St. Sabas* (namely, that the Jordan Bessi defended the Orthodox and the Lavra of St. Sabas in Jerusalem), was borne by a Thracian tribe, many of whom dwelt in Palestine in the V and VI centuries. Georgian scholars argue that this word is a corruption of *Iberes* (ἰβῆρες), Iberians, Georgians, who are indicated in the Lavra of St. Sabas by the *Life* of the latter: Bishop Theodosius, *Palestinskie monastyri* [Palestinian Monasteries], p. 101.

[^1540]: With the psalms "the lesser antiphons with their alleluiatics (*allelujaticis*)..." "After Pascha until Pentecost on Fridays they take refreshment (*reficiendum*) once, and after the 12th (hour) there are six *missae*, that is, 18 readings must be pronounced from memory and then 18 psalms, 3 antiphons; after the nocturn there must be 3 *missae* from the book until daybreak." This obscure passage of Caesarius presumably prescribes throughout the entire period of Pentecost on Friday evenings before Saturday especially long vigils from 6 p.m. Friday without supper until the very dawn of Saturday (cf. in Cassian, above p. 162), whereby the customary vigil of 18 psalms was preceded by 18 readings and followed by yet 3 readings. All this is in the *Regula ad virgines*, § 66. Caesarius describes the night service more briefly in the *Regula ad monachos*: "The vigils from October until Pascha comprise two nocturns and three *missae* (readings): at one *missa* a brother reads three folios and they pray; he reads another three folios and arises, say an antiphon, a responsory, and another antiphon; the antiphons being according unto the order of the Psalter; at the 1st *missa* the reading is concerning the resurrection and at it all stand" (canon 21).

[^1545]: In Rome already under Pope Gregory I the Great there existed a martyrology with commemorations for each day of the year, as is evident from the reply of this pope in 598 unto the request of Eulogius, Patriarch of Alexandria, to send him the *Acts of All the Martyrs* collected by Eusebius of Caesarea; replying unto the patriarch that no such collection was found in the Roman archives, the Pope writes: 'But we possess a collection in one codex of the names of almost all the martyrs, with an indication of their passions for all days, and daily we celebrate masses in their honor; however, in this volume it is not shown how each suffered, but only the name, place, and day of the passion are indicated... We think that you also possess this, most blessed brother' (Gregory the Great, *Registrum epistolarum* VIII, 29). Blessed Jerome is considered the compiler of the most ancient Roman martyrology that has come down unto us, on the basis of the testimonies of Cassiodorus († 563): 'Read the passions of the martyrs, which you will find, among other places, in the letter of Jerome unto Chromatius and Heliodorus' (*De institutione divinarum litterarum*, c. 32; Migne, *Patrologia Latina*, Vol. 70, col. 1147), and of Bede († 735): '...a martyrology which by name and preface is attributed unto Blessed Jerome, although Jerome was not its author, but its translator, while the author, they say, was Eusebius' (*Liber retractationis in Acta Apostolorum*, c. 1; Migne, *PL*, Vol. 92, col. 997). The correspondence of Blessed Jerome with Bishops Heliodorus and Chromatius is appended unto the copies of the Hieronymian Martyrology; these bishops requested that he send them from Caesarea the calendar (*feriales*) of Eusebius for the greater veneration of the martyrs—in which Gregory, Bishop of Cordova, was especially renowned, as was revealed at a certain Council of Milan, celebrating 'on every non-fasting day morning masses, and during fasts evening masses, and commemorating therein many names of martyrs'; Blessed Jerome, in place of the calendar of Eusebius, sends unto the bishops his own, wherein, according unto his epistle, he 'inscribed the feasts for each month and for each day; but since for each day there occurred more than 800–900 names of martyrs of diverse regions, and for no single day, except January 1, fewer than 500, he records only the more renowned.' The authenticity of this correspondence is doubtful, since the style here is not Jerome's; and apart from it, the compilation of the martyrology by Blessed Jerome is corroborated by nothing else: neither he himself, nor Gennadius of Marseilles († after 495) in his work *De scriptoribus ecclesiasticis*, nor Gregory the Great speaks of such a compilation; furthermore, the martyrology contains commemorations later than Blessed Jerome. It was published according unto a 10th-century MS in Migne, *Patrologia Latina*, Vol. 30, and by the Bollandists, *Acta Sanctorum*, Novembris tomus II, Brussels, 1894, according unto 8th-century MSS (edited by Giovanni Battista de Rossi and Louis Duchesne).

[^1546]: If in the second century mass slaughters of Christians were represented by a figure of no more than 40 (Lyons, 177), then in the third century and especially under Diocletian the martyrs were numbered in the thousands; the magnitude of the figure here therefore excites no suspicion, nor does the frequent repetition of certain names (Felix 118 times, Saturninus 87, etc.), as well as the strangeness of other names (*Manira*, *Jtercola*, *Barbalabia*); one must bear in mind the Roman custom of showing no restraint in assigning nicknames unto slaves.

[^1547]: Archbishop Sergius (Spassky), *Polnyi mesiatseslov Vostoka* [Complete Menologion of the East], Moscow, 1875, Vol. I, pp. 58–61.

[^1548]: Now in the Bibliothèque nationale de France, Paris. Described by Kellner, Karl Adam Heinrich, *Heortologie*, Freiburg im Breisgau, 1906, pp. 213–215, after Morin, Dom Germain, *Anecdota Maredsolana*, Maredsous–Paris, 1893, Vol. I. Not mentioned by Archbishop Sergius.

[^1549]: Of other menologia in the West during this period are known: 1) that published by Leo Allatius (*De Ecclesiae Occidentalis atque Orientalis perpetua consensione*, Rome, 1648, p. 1487) from an 8th-century Breviary of the Vatican Library; of its 84 commemorations, half are present among us; certain commemorations fall on the same dates as among us, and not as at present among Roman Catholics; there is no Transfiguration nor Entry into the Temple; not all Apostles are present; from March 12 to April 14 there are no saints. 2) The calendar in the Sacramentary attributed unto Gregory the Great, 7th–8th century; similar unto the preceding, yet certain commemorations are lacking, while certain ones are new; all the Apostles are present. 3) The Gallican: the latest commemoration is that of St. Gregory the Great († 604); commemorations occur for all days, yet August 16 to December is defective. 4) That of Protasius, Bishop of Besançon († 625); similar unto the Sacramentary of Gregory; in March there are only 5 commemorations; later commemorations, such as Charlemagne, were inserted by another hand.—In addition, there appeared a whole series of abbreviations of the Hieronymian Martyrology. Such an abbreviation was first of all the martyrology of Bede († 735), the renowned English monk and writer, 'who strove carefully to note by what manner of torment and under which judge the martyrs conquered the world,' for which reason he retained for each day from the Hieronymian Martyrology only one or two saints, and left certain days without any commemorations at all. Nevertheless, the martyrology of Bede hath come down unto us only in the redaction expanded c. 830 by Florus Drepanius, canon of the diocese of Lyons; in this latter redaction, likewise, commemorations do not occur for all days. This last martyrology was reworked (with rearranged commemorations) and slightly augmented: in the diocese of Trier by Wandalbert c. 848; in Mainz by Archbishop Rabanus Maurus in the mid-9th century (commemorations for all days); in Lyons c. 858 by Ado, subsequently Archbishop of Vienne (containing very extensive narratives concerning the saints, beginning on December 25); by Usuard, a Benedictine near Paris, c. 858; and by Notker in Switzerland c. 894. (Archbishop Sergius, *Polnyi mesiatseslov Vostoka*, Vol. I, pp. 62–73.)

[^1569]: The kontakia and ikoi of the Annunciation, which now constitute the Akathist unto the Mother of God, yet which in ancient Menaia (for instance, Moscow Synodal Typography MS No. 68 of the 14th century) were placed under March 25 after Ode 6 of the canon, are attributed unto George of Pisidia as the author of the narrative of that very war against the Avars, upon the fortunate conclusion of which 'the people began annually to chant a thanksgiving hymn, naming that very day Akathist' (*Narrative of the Akathist*, Triodion). That the akathist kontakia and ikoi could originally have been composed for the Annunciation and only later adapted unto the commemoration of the war, apart from their content, is evident from variants in the 1st kontakion: 'Thy servants' (*rabi tvoi*), 'Thy servant' (*rab tvoi*), 'the city' (*grad*). Archimandrite Amphilochius, *Kondakarii v grecheskom podlinnike XII–XIII v.* [The Kondakarion according to the Greek Original of the 12th–13th Centuries], Moscow, 1879, p. 15.

[^1571]: Poetic meter in Greek ecclesiastical hymnography was discovered very recently by Cardinal Pitra (*Hymnographie de l'église grecque*, Rome, 1867; the book was reviewed by A. Vasiliev, 'O grecheskikh tserkovnykh pesnopeniiakh' [On Greek Church Hymns], in *Vizantiiskii Vremennik*, St. Petersburg, 1896, Vol. III, pp. 582 ff.). Prior unto this, it was believed that Greek ecclesiastical hymnody followed the rhythm of biblical poetry, concerning whose nature scholars still dispute, though the majority reduce it unto parallelism of members. Moreover, 17th-century scholars vaguely presaged Pitra's discovery. Thus Baronius observed that in manuscripts of canons there is punctuation that at times runs counter unto the sense and serves, apparently, to preserve a certain meter (Baronius, *Annales ecclesiastici*, Mainz, 1601, p. 1054 [Skaballanovich prints Moguntiae 1501, an obvious typographical error for 1601]). Vagnereck (*Pietas Mariana*, Munich, 1647, p. 32) states that upon reading the Menaia he seemed to hear as it were the voice of an unknown muse.

[^1574]: A difference here is introduced by organs. Yet they appeared in Roman Catholic worship very late. Although the *Liber Pontificalis* attributes unto Pope Vitalian († 671) the introduction of the organ into Italian churches, and a number of historians repeat the report concerning the sending of an organ by Constantine Copronymus unto Pepin in 757 and by Emperor Michael I unto Charlemagne, nevertheless even in the 9th–12th centuries the organ encountered opponents in the West: among these were the renowned liturgist Amalarius (9th c.), the Scottish [English Cistercian] Abbot Aelred (12th c.), the most eminent theologian Thomas Aquinas, and later Erasmus (Luft, *Liturgik*, Vol. II, p. 258; Binterim, *Die vorzüglichsten Denkwürdigkeiten*, Vol. IV, Part 1, p. 55; Archbishop Philaret [Gumilevsky], *Istoricheskii obzor pesnopentsev*, p. 264).

[^1575]: Published for the first time in full by Prof. A. A. Dmitrievsky from a 10th-century manuscript of the Patmos Library, No. 266, in *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei* [Description of Liturgical Manuscripts], Vol. I: *Τυπικά*, Kyiv, 1890, pp. 1–153. In addition unto this copy of the Sophia Typikon, two others are known at present: one in the Jerusalem Patriarchal Library, No. 40, of the 10th–11th century (reported by A. Papadopoulos-Kerameus in *Ἰεροσολυμιτικὴ βιβλιοθήκη*, St. Petersburg, 1897, p. 80); and another in the Dresden Royal Library, No. 104 (described in detail for the first time by A. Dmitrievsky, *Drevneishie patriarshie Tipikony Sviotogr. Ierus. i Velik. Konstantinopol'sk. tserkvi* [The Most Ancient Patriarchal Typika of the Holy Sepulchre of Jerusalem and of the Great Church of Constantinople], Kyiv, 1907, pp. 214 ff.). The most ancient is the Patmos redaction: the latest commemorations therein are Ignatius, Archbishop of Constantinople († 877), and the dedication of the Church of All Saints in the Boule, built by Leo VI in memory of his consort Theophano († 894) during her lifetime; there is also the 'commemoration of the Holy Martyr Bacchus, who suffered in our days under Irene (797–802) and Constantine (VI, 780–797)'; the feast of the Image Not Made with Hands on August 16, established in 944, is absent. The Jerusalem manuscript was written by order of Constantine VII Porphyrogenitus (912–959): the latest commemoration is Tryphon, Patriarch of Constantinople († 933). In the Dresden manuscript the Image Not Made with Hands is already present, as is likewise on January 25 the translation of the relics of Gregory the Theologian from Arianzus unto Constantinople, which occurred in 950; yet the commemoration of the Three Hierarchs on January 30, established in 1084, is absent.

[^1577]: The Typikon of Hagia Sophia hath been placed not merely in connection with, but in genetic dependence upon, the Jerusalem Typikon of the Holy Sepulchre (Church of the Anastasis). The grounds for this are the coincidence of later (9th–10th c.) redactions of both in certain minor details (for example, the distribution of the so-called *roga*—stipends or gifts—by the patriarch on great feasts, the chanting of certain hymns by cantors upon the ambo and others by monks, spoudaioi, etc.) and in a few rites: both possess *trithekte* and the *pannychis* (A. Dmitrievsky, *Drevneishie patriarshie Tipikony*, pp. 144, 147, 206, 208, 298). Yet the structure of the daily office according unto both Typika is entirely different: in the Jerusalem Typikon of the 9th century, at least, this structure is far closer unto our present order than unto that of Constantinople at that time, and concerning earlier worship in either church we know nothing (leaving aside the description of Sylvia [Egeria]).

[^1593]: Symeon of Thessalonica, *De sacra precatione*, ch. 349–351 (314–316). The 'Order' (*Taxis*) of the Athos-St. Andrew 15th-century manuscript and the musical manuscript *Papadike Techne* (*Παπαδικὴ τέχνη* [printed *Παλαδικὴ*]) of the 14th century of the Vatopedi Monastery Library No. 297 (931) depict in greater detail the Entrance, the singing of the Doxology, and the reading of the Gospel. The cantors chant **"Most blessed art thou"** only up unto the words: **"Blessed is Christ our God"**. At the Entrance the domestikos pronounces in an elevated voice these words of the Theotokion (musical MS); at the Entrance between the preceding candlebearers walks the reader in a (short) phelonion with the large cross (aforementioned); upon the entrance of the priests into the altar, he ascends two steps of the ambo and here pronounces in chant the beginning of the Doxology: **"Glory to God in the highest"** ('Order'). 'And the domestikos begins the sweet-sounding hymn (*ᾀσματικὸν ὕμνον*), that is, the Doxology, while the priests pronounce the remainder. (Following the Doxology) the domestikos: **"Having risen from the tomb and burst the bonds…"**, and the priests say the remainder. Then the domestikos: **"Glory to the Father, and to the Son…"**, and again the priests: **"Having risen from the tomb"**; the domestikos with a high voice: **"Having risen. A Psalm of David"**, and immediately the reader says: **"Arise, O Lord"** (musical MS). According unto the 'Order', the reader, following the Great Doxology, 'chants melodically: **"A Psalm of David"** and the remaining verses. The reader departs, and the domestikos or laosynaktes (*λαοσυνάκτης*) ascends unto the 3rd step of the ambo and chants the prokeimenon with the remaining readers; the domestikos likewise chants the musical intonation (*ἤχημα* / *ἤχισμα*) at the endings'. The verses unto the prokeimenon are indicated by the musical manuscript: **"I will confess Thee, O Lord…"**; **"I will be glad…"**; and further the rubric: *Θεσσαλονικαῖον* (according unto the Thessalonian order?): **"O Lord my God, let Thy hand be lifted up"**; *ἡ μεγάλη δοχή* (the great feast / agape feast): **"Arise, O Lord [my God, let Thy hand be lifted up,] forget not Thy poor unto the end"** [LXX Ps. 9:33 / 10:12]. Further according unto the 'Order', the priest in full sacerdotal vestments carries the Gospel, preceded by readers with two candlesticks and two deacons, ascends the ambo and sits with the deacons until the conclusion of the prokeimenon, after which is sung: **"Prolong the holy empire of our sovereigns"** and **"The Master…"** (*Τὸν δεσπότην*); at these hymns the sacred ministers stand up; upon the reading of the Gospel upon the ambo by the presbyter, it is borne by the deacon unto the hierarch to be kissed, and is carried into the altar by the deacon, preceded by the reader with the cross and accompanied by the presbyter (Dmitrievsky, *Τυπικά*, p. 164).

[^1605]: Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. II: *Εὐχολόγια*, p. 1009; Goar, J., *Εὐχολόγιον sive Rituale Graecorum*, p. 639; Trebnik of the Moscow Synodal Library, No. 371/675, fols. 21–24.

[^1606]: Euchologion of the Paris National Library (1027), No. 213, fol. 81v; Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. II: *Εὐχολόγια*, p. 1110; Vol. I: *Τυπικά*, p. 152. — Similar litanies existed and still exist in the Western Church: such are the *litania major* on April 25 on the feast of the Apostle Mark, and the *litania minor* or *rogationes* (petitions) on the three days before Ascension. The designations 'major' and 'minor' do not correspond unto the comparative solemnity of these litanies, but are explained historically: the former was instituted earlier (in all probability by Pope Liberius in the 4th century) in place of the very solemn pagan procession *ambarvale* (from *arva*, field—a procession into the fields, in contrast unto *amburbale*, a city procession, to propitiate the goddess Robigo, upon whom depended mildew [*robigo*] upon crops; for which reason April 25 was termed *Robigalia*); Pope Gregory the Great regulated the Christian litany that replaced this pagan procession, appointing seven distinct corporations of Rome (clergy, men, monks, handmaids, married women, widows, and the poor with children) to go forth with the litany from different churches (whence the name *litania septiformis*; *Epistolae*, II, 2). The *litania minor* was first instituted circa 470 by Bishop Mamertus of Vienne on account of earthquakes and other calamities; it was legalized for Gaul by the Council of Orléans in 511 (Canon 27); in the 6th century it received wide dissemination, yet in Rome it was adopted only under Pope Leo III (795–816). On the days of these litanies, especially the second, services were exceptionally lengthy; in the 9th century in certain churches heads were sprinkled with blessed ashes, sprinkled with holy water (as is still done at present according unto the Ambrosian rite), and all walked barefoot (according unto Canon 33 of the Council of Mainz [*Concilium Moguntinum*] of 813). A number of orders have been preserved for the minor litany: according unto them, following a series of antiphons and psalms there was a supplication very similar unto our litany **"Let us all say"** (it begins indeed: *Dicamus omnes, Domine miserere*); following a series of other antiphons reminiscent in general content of our penitential troparia, there followed a litany in the proper sense, termed *statio* (station, since it was performed at the stopping-places of the procession) or *oratio* (from the frequent singing of *ora*—'pray'), consisting of a long series of petitions (at times exceeding 100), addressed for the most part in order unto all the most renowned saints and executed antiphonally (by clergy and choir), for example: *Sancta Maria, ora pro nobis*; *Sancte Michael, ora*; *Sancte Gabriel, ora* or *intercede* ('intercede'); for the more venerated and intimate saints 'pray for us' (*ora pro nobis*), and for the others simply 'pray' (*ora*) (Martène, *De antiquis ecclesiae ritibus*, Vol. II, pp. 514 ff.; Kellner, *Heortologie*, p. 127).

[^1607]: Preserved in a manuscript of the year 1122 in the Jerusalem Patriarchal Library. It may be assigned unto the 10th century because on the litany occurs the name of Patriarch Nicholas (857–887 with an interruption), and the topography of the holy places corresponds unto their condition following the restoration from the Persian conquest under Patriarch Modestus (610–637) down to the devastation under Caliph al-Hakim in 1010. Published in full by A. Papadopoulos-Kerameus, *Ἀνάλεκτα Ἱεροσολυμιτικῆς σταχυολογίας*, St. Petersburg, 1894, Vol. II; in abridgment (the initial words of the hymns) with translation and extensive notes by A. A. Dmitrievsky, *Bogosluzhenie strastnoi i paskhal'noi sedmits vo sviatom Ierusalime IX–X v.* [Divine Worship of Passion and Paschal Weeks in Holy Jerusalem in the 9th–10th Centuries], Kazan, 1894 (where the chronology of the monument is established on p. XIII).

[^1608]: A courtier by birth, who took monastic vows together with his parents—wealthy individuals—and his wife at the onset of iconoclasm, St. Theodore laboured greatly for the defence of the Church and the faith against the encroachments of heretical emperors, for which he endured imprisonment and exile. He was likewise a gifted hymnographer.

[^1609]: Theodore the Studite, *Great Catechesis*, § 17.

[^1610]: Theodore the Studite, *Great Catechesis*, § 73.

[^1611]: Theodore the Studite, *Great Catechesis*, §§ 12, 6, 17; *Testament*, § 22; *Letters*, I.

[^1612]: Theodore the Studite, *Small Catechesis*, §§ 107, 59. Priest N. Grossu, *Prepodobny Feodor Studit* [St. Theodore the Studite], pp. 105–106.

[^1613]: The monument constitutes, as stated in the preface, a record of the 'tradition which we received from our great father and confessor Theodore, and which not only we, but also many of the best monasteries have chosen'. The record was made by one of the monks no later than the middle of the 9th century, because it served as the foundation for the *Diatyposis* of St. Athanasius of Athos and the *Typikon* of Patriarch Alexis (see concerning them below). Published from a very ancient manuscript by Mai, *Nova Patrum bibliotheca*, Rome, 1849, Vol. V, whence reprinted in Migne, *Patrologia Graeca*, Vol. 99; from a later manuscript with significant variants from Vatopedi Monastery No. 322 (956) of the 13th–14th century, published by Prof. A. A. Dmitrievsky, *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. I: *Τυπικά*; Russian translation according unto the 1st edition in Golubinsky, E. E., *Istoriia russkoi tserkvi* [History of the Russian Church], Vol. I, Part 2, p. 671.

[^1614]: Concerning the beginning of monasticism on Athos there is no reliable information; Athonite traditions trace the emergence of monasteries here as far back as pagan times (Emperor Caracalla, 211–217 AD) or at least unto the flourishing of Egyptian-Palestinian asceticism; the Arab devastations of Athos circa 670 AD and subsequently circa 830 do not permit us to say anything certain concerning Athos prior unto the 9th century, although the foundation of nearly all present Athonite monasteries traces its origin back unto this most ancient period. The first historically attested Athonite hermit ('hesychast') was St. Peter (who lived on Athos, 681–734 AD); during the era of iconoclasm many monks sought refuge here; St. Euthymius (circa 840 AD) restored monasticism on Athos after the second Arab devastation; his disciples established the first cenobium on Athos—Vatopedi, and John Kolobos built the first stone monastery on the isthmus at Hierissos and obtained from Emperor Basil the Macedonian in 872 AD a chrysobull whereby Athos was granted unto the exclusive possession of monks. But thereupon enmity arose between the cenobites and anchorites; the latter separated and established in Karyes their own administration ('Protat') headed by the Protos. After a new Saracen devastation circa the middle of the 10th century, monasticism on Athos was restored by St. Athanasius, who founded the Great Lavra on Athos with donations from his friend Emperor Nikephoros Phokas. Following a complaint of the anchorites against the predominance of the cenobitic life, alien unto ancient Athos, Emperor John Tzimiskes by his Typikon (*τυπικόν* or *tragos*) of 971 AD recognized equal rights for both modes of ascetic life on Athos. Unto this period belong the first reliable accounts concerning the majority of present Athonite monasteries, such as Iveron (founded by Georgians), Docheiariou or Daphne, Panteleimon or Thessalonian, Esphigmenou (founded by inhabitants of the Thessalian village of Sphigou), Chilandar (named after the founder's surname), Zographou, founded by the Greek George the Iconographer (in Greek *zōgraphos*), Koutloumousiou (after the founder's name), Xeropotamou ('dry torrent'), and Dionysiou, founded by Alexios Komnenos in 1118 [or later by Alexios III in 1374] at the request of Dionysius who asceticized here. Emperor Constantine Monomachos by his Typikon of 1046 forbade Athos commercial relations with laity. From this time onward the present strict order became established on Athos (absolute exclusion of women). Alexios Komnenos in 1090 recognized Athos as independent of the Metropolitan of Thessalonica and subject solely unto its own Protos. In the 11th century Bulgarian monks appeared on Athos, settling the Zographou Monastery; in the 12th century Serbian monks (headed by Prince Stefan-Simeon Nemanja and his son St. Sabbas), unto whom Chilandar was granted; and Russian monks, unto whom was given first the Monastery of Xylourgou, and in 1169 also St. Panteleimon ('Rossikon'). The majority of Athonite monasteries are dedicated unto the Most Holy Theotokos; there are also churches of the Forerunner, Chrysostom, and Nicholas. There was on Athos (in the 10th century) likewise a Latin monastery with the Rule of St. Benedict—Karakallou (after the surname of the founder). (Bishop Porphyrius Uspensky, *Istoriia Afona* [History of Athos], Kyiv, 1876, *passim*; Sokolov, I., *Sostoianie monashestva v Vizantiiskoi tserkvi s pol. IX v. do nach. XIII v.* [The State of Monasticism in the Byzantine Church from the Mid-9th to the Early 13th Century], Kazan, 1894, pp. 214 ff.).

[^1615]: The *Diatyposis* of St. Athanasius was published by Meyer, Ph., *Die Haupturkunden für die Geschichte der Athosklöster*, Leipzig, 1894, pp. 17 ff., and Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. I: *Τυπικά*, p. 238, from manuscripts of the 16th century (Athos-Iveron Library No. 754) and even of the 19th century, which nevertheless represent copies from 11th-century codices, perhaps even from the autographs of St. Athanasius (preserved in the skeuophylakion of the Lavra of St. Athanasius and shown unto no one), against which they were collated in Meyer's edition (Dmitrievsky, *ibid.*, p. XXXIII).

[^1616]: The first and most ancient manuscript is preserved in the Sinai Library in a separate gathering detached from a Gospel Book, lacking the beginning (the Triodion commences from Ascension) and the end (terminating on August 6); the second is in the same library appended unto Gospel Book No. 150, complete, but only for the major days. Its distinction in the synaxarion from the Typikon of the Great Church, apart from the order (the Triodion comes first): no patriarchal celebration is presupposed; there are no commemorations of church dedications or earthquakes (except October 26). In all probability it was utilized in a diocese subject unto the Patriarch of Constantinople. Judging by the mention of a church of Elijah, whither a litany took place on the 2nd Sunday after Pascha (according unto the Constantinopolitan Typikon on that day the litany was unto Chalkoprateia), one might suppose that it was used on Sinai, where a church of Elijah existed. Yet it is more probable that the Kanonarion is of Southern Italian or Sicilian origin, because it contains Western and Eastern commemorations according unto the dates of Western calendars (May 8: 'Archangels Michael and Gabriel—Apparition on Mount Gargano'). In the earlier copy the latest commemoration is that of Theodore the Studite; in the later, that of Paul the New († 784 AD) (Archbishop Sergius, *Polny mesiatseslov Vostoka* [Complete Menology of the East], Vol. I, p. 145). Published in Russian translation by Archimandrite Antonin, *Drevnii kanonar' Sinaiskoi biblioteki* [The Ancient Kanonarion of the Sinai Library], Kyiv, 1874; in the original Greek by Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. I: *Τυπικά*, p. 172.

[^1617]: The Chronicle states under the year 1051 that St. Theodosius, upon the multiplication of the brethren in his monastery, 'began to seek the monastic rule, and there was found then Michael, a monk of the Studion Monastery, who had come from the Greeks with Metropolitan George; and he began to seek from him the rule of the Studite monks, and having obtained it from him, transcribed it, and instituted in his monastery how to chant the monastic chants, and how to make prostrations, and to read the readings, and the standing in church, and the entire church order, the sitting at table, and what to eat on which days, all according unto the rule'; and from the Caves Monastery 'all monasteries took over the rule' (*Povest' vremennykh let* according unto the Laurentian Codex, ed. Archaeographic Commission, St. Petersburg, 1872, p. 156). But according unto the *Life of St. Theodosius* composed by Nestor, Theodosius 'sent one of the brethren unto Constantinople unto Ephrem the Eunuch, that he might send unto him the entire Typikon of the Studion Monastery copied in full', which took place circa 1062 AD (*Zhitie*, ed. O. Bodiansky in *Chteniia v Obshchestve istorii i drevnostei rossiiskikh*, 1858, fol. 111v). These conflicting accounts are reconciled by the supposition that the monk Michael possessed an incomplete copy of the Typikon, perhaps the *Hypotyposis*, for which reason ten years later St. Theodosius felt the need for a complete text ('the entire'). — Prior unto the appearance of the Studite Typikon and thereafter, until the Mongol-Tatar invasion, in cathedral and parish churches in Rus' it is thought that clergy were guided by the Typikon of the Great Church of Constantinople. The Council of Vladimir of 1274 and that of Constantinople of 1276 establish the existence in the Russian Church of certain customs of the Great Church, such as the celebration of the Proskomedia (*proskomisanie*) by a deacon (in all likelihood, such may have been the most ancient custom in general), the celebration of the Presanctified Liturgy on Cheesefare Wednesday and Friday and on Great Friday, the reading of the Gospel by the hierarch four times a year (the 1st Gospel of the Passion, at the Paschal Liturgy and Vespers, and at the litany of September 1), and the Elevation of the Cross with the peculiarities of the Great Church: some of these customs are affirmed by the councils, while others are abolished (Pavlov, A. S., *Russkaia istoricheskaia biblioteka*, St. Petersburg, 1880, Vol. VI, Nos. 6, 12), yet all persisted in places until the 15th and even 17th century, especially in the Novgorod lands, which encompassed the entire north of Rus'. Furthermore, in comparatively late manuscripts of the 14th–15th centuries there has been preserved the translation of entire offices of the Great Church into the Slavonic tongue, such as the Order of the Washing of the Holy Table on Great Thursday, the Order of the New Year (Indiction) on September 1, and the litany according unto the Typikon of the Great Church. Unto the same testify the Slavonic manuscripts of the Asmatika (refrains unto psalms in the Sung Office, for example, *Tin ikumenin* = *Vselennuiu* ['The world']), the phrase in ancient Typika 'chanting at Trithekte' indicating the existence of this office, and finally even a fragment of the Typikon of the Great Church in the Slavonic translation of Archbishop Clement of Novgorod († 1299) in the Moscow Khludov Library (Popov, A., *Pribavlenie k opisaniiu rukopisei Khludovskoi biblioteki*, Moscow, 1875, p. 117), and the like. See concerning this in greater detail and for citations in the work being printed by Archpriest M. Lisitsyn, *Pervonachal'nyi slaviano-russkii Tipikon* [The Original Slavonic-Russian Typikon], St. Petersburg, 1911 [1910], where the author indicates also another fragment of the Slavonic translation of the Typikon of the Great Church in Popov's description, p. 117, designated a Triodion.

[^1618]: The most ancient manuscripts of the Studite-Alexis Typikon: 1) In MS Moscow Synodal (Typography) Library No. 285/142/1206 of the 11th–12th century, lacking the beginning (Meatfare Sunday) and the end, containing only the Triodion Typikon, and instead of a Menology (which may, however, have been torn off) a Kontakionarion, in places noted with musical neumes, for the entire year; 2) nearly complete (from the Sunday of the Prodigal Son) and full (with the entire Menology and an extensive disciplinary section) in MS Moscow Synodal Library No. 330/380 of the 12th–13th century, of a more comprehensive redaction than the first; closer unto the Typikon of the Great Church than the first (in the commemorations of the Sundays of Great Lent), consequently no less ancient, although containing more commemorations (by 12); then 3) former St. Sophia Novgorod Library (now preserved in the St. Petersburg Theological Academy) No. 1136, in all likelihood of the 13th century—containing only the Triodion from Meatfare Vespers and the Menology through January 17; 4) Moscow Synodal (Typography) Library No. 144/1236; 5) Moscow Synodal Library No. 333/381, written in 1398 AD, without the disciplinary section; 6) No. 905/382 of the 15th century. All these manuscripts, beginning with No. 330, are nearly literally identical with one another, clearly transcribed from a single exemplar, though the later ones occasionally present slight alterations of specifically Studite practice (for example, omitting mention of the Lenten Liturgy on all days of the fast, observing with less solemnity the commemoration of St. Theodore on November 11, designating him 'father' rather than 'our father'). There exist also several fragments of the Studite-Alexis Typikon (for instance, in the miscellany of the former St. Cyril of Belozersk Library [in the St. Petersburg Theological Academy] No. 275 and others).

[^1619]: The Monastery of Grottaferrata and its Typikon long defended their independence from their mighty neighbour. The friends of the first hegumen, Nilus, could not persuade him even to visit Rome and its holy places. Hegumen Bartholomew, although maintaining friendly relations with Popes John XIX and Benedict IX and even participating in the Lateran Council of 1044, nevertheless oversaw the writing of Menaia that strictly preserve Eastern traditions and diverge from the later Grottaferrata Typikon. Only Hegumen Blasius is styled by Pope Martin IV as his vassal; in view of such new relations with Rome there was undertaken by this hegumen a renewal (*ἀνακαινίσθη*) of the Grottaferrata Typikon; it is readily apparent wherein it consisted: on November 1 it is prescribed to celebrate All Saints; on great feasts a Latin litany is appointed, and at the Liturgy a 'combined Greek and Latin order'; during Great Lent a daily Presanctified Liturgy is recommended in view of the custom of the Roman Church to celebrate a full Liturgy daily; on the Exaltation and during the Nativity Fast (Philip's Fast) cheese and eggs are permitted; in the litanies the Pope is commemorated with the title 'our most honorable and holy father *ho deina* [N.]', Latin terminology is introduced, etc. The Typikon has been preserved in a manuscript contemporary with Blasius bearing the date 1300 [1299/1300 AD] in the monastery library No. A. α. VII [A. II. 7]. Described by Toscani, Th., *Ad Typica Graecorum*, Rome, 1864; supplementary unto this description in Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. I: *Τυπικά*, pp. 899 ff.

[^1620]: Preserved 1) in MS Turin University Library No. 216 of the 12th–13th century; close unto this manuscript are: 2) the Typikon of the Barberini Library in Rome No. 69 III (77) of 1205 AD, 'according unto the order of the Monastery of San Nicola di Casole'; 3) MS Vallicelliana Library in Rome No. D. 61 of the 13th century; 4) MS Laurentian Library in Florence No. 15 of 1336 AD; 5) MS Barberini Library No. III, 102 (236) of 1583 AD, exhibiting literal agreement (with the exception of the ending) with the Turin manuscript.

[^1621]: The principal manuscript: 1) Vatican Library No. 1877, executed for this monastery in 1292 AD; close unto it are: 2) MS Vienna Imperial Library No. 1126/326/144 of the 13th century; 3) MS Barberini Library No. 67, written in 1552 AD for the cathedral of the city of Bova in Calabria; 4) MS Vatican Library No. 1609 of the 16th century, presented in 1585 AD unto Pope Sixtus V by the Chorepiscopus of Rhodes (containing as a distinct appendix a Latin Menology). Described (both redactions) in Toscani, Th., *Ad Typica Graecorum*; more fully and in greater detail in Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. I: *Τυπικά* (unto whom belongs likewise the division into two redactions).

[^1622]: Preserved in MS Athens National University No. 788 of the 12th century, whence published for the first time by Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. I: *Τυπικά*, pp. 256–656. — From that same era there have reached us several other ktetorial Typika, though of disciplinary content only, referring for liturgical practice unto the aforementioned Typika or unto the Studite rule in general, or finally unto the commonly used rule ('Synaxarion') without designating it specifically. Such are: the Typikon of 1130 AD of the Constantinopolitan Pantokrator (Almighty) Monastery, founded by Emperor John Komnenos (in an 18th-century manuscript of the Halki School in Constantinople No. 85), prescribing divine worship 'after the model of the great church in the palace', and referring occasionally also unto the 'order of the Hagiopolite' (as the name indicates—Jerusalemite; according unto Dmitrievsky, *Τυπικά*, p. LXIV—the 'court typikon'); the Typikon of the suburban Constantinopolitan Monastery of St. Mamas of 1159 AD, whose prologue is signed by several hegumens, among them the Studite hegumen, and which instructs that in divine worship one should be guided by the Evergetis Typikon 'as already accepted and established by custom' (ch. 8 and elsewhere; autograph manuscript in the Paris National Library); the Typikon of the Asia Minor Mother of God Monastery *tōn Hēliou Bōmōn ētoi Elegmōn* ('of the Altars of the Sun, that is, of the Rebukes') of 1162 AD, referring unto the Studite Synaxarion (ch. 8 and elsewhere; autograph in the Patmos Library); all published by Dmitrievsky, *Τυπικά*, pp. 656–715.

[^1663]: A native of Constantinople, Nikon was obliged to retire from the Rhaithou Monastery, where he had tonsured as a monk, on account of the dissatisfaction of the brethren, unto the Black Mountain Monastery; following its devastation first by the Turks in 1085, and later by the Crusaders in 1098, he returned unto Rhaithou. He is renowned as the author of the *Pandects* ('Compendium'), a work of canonical content, and the *Taktikon* ('Rule'), of liturgical and monastic-disciplinary character, among other writings. His works in Greek have not yet been published (they are preserved in manuscripts of the Sinai Library), with the exception of fragments (in Pitra and others); the *Pandects* and *Taktikon* enjoyed great veneration in ancient Russia and provided much material for the *Kormchaya Kniga* (Nomocanon). They were printed in Slavonic at Ostroh [printed: Otroge] in 1645, Pochaiv in 1795, and Moscow in the Edinoverie Typography in 1889. Mansvetov, I. D., *Tserkovny ustav*, pp. 187 ff.; Golubinsky, E. [^1667]: The most ancient manuscript of the Jerusalem Typikon in the Jerusalem Library is from 1201, MS No. 312. Yet as we shall see, this is not the most ancient manuscript of the Jerusalem Typikon.

[^1668]: Thus the Typikon of the Sinai Library MS No. 1094 of the 12th century; this is the most ancient witness of the Jerusalem Typikon in the form it possessed prior unto the adoption into it of the Studite canons and stichera.

[^1669]: In the library of the Sinai Monastery there is a Georgian manuscript of the 10th century: 'Jerusalem Typikon of Divine Worship for the 12 Principal Feasts.' *Soobshcheniya Imperatorskogo Pravoslavnogo Palestinskogo Obshchestva*, 1903, Issue 1, p. 27; Kekelidze, K., Archpriest, *Liturgicheskie gruzinskie pamyatniki*, p. 614. There is also a Menaion (December) of the 9th century according unto the Jerusalem order: Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Puteshestvie po Vostoku*, p. 66.

[^1670]: Such are the Typikon of the Sinai Library MS No. 1047 (1214) and an exact copy thereof, MS No. 1101 (1322). Analogous unto the Sinai recension of the Jerusalem Typikon there existed an Asia Minor, more specifically Trebizond recension thereof, bearing traces of local practice, witnesses of which may be considered the Typika of the Vatopedi Monastery: 1346, MS No. 320 (931); 1405, MS No. 324 (935); and 1446, MS No. 321 (932). Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Puteshestvie po Vostoku*, pp. 56, 57, 138. All these Typika, so important for the history of the Typikon, have not yet been published.

[^1671]: Migne, *Patrologia Graeca*, Vol. 119.

[^1672]: Typikon of Empress Irene, chs. 39, 33; Migne, *Patrologia Graeca*, Vol. 127, cols. 1054, 1059; published here from the original manuscript.

[^1673]: Miklosich, F. and Müller, J., *Acta et diplomata Graeca medii aevi sacra et profana*, Vienna, 1860–1890, Vol. V, Part II, p. 401.

[^1674]: Dmitrievsky, A. A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisei*, Vol. I: *Typika*, pp. 783, 788. Here this Typikon is published from an 18th-century manuscript, No. 85 of the Chalki Theological School, a manuscript representing a copy from a 15th-century manuscript of the Constantinople Metochion of the Holy Sepulchre, No. 160.

[^1675]: The most ancient witness of the Hagiorite Typikon following the *Diatyposis* of St. Athanasius (excerpts from which are repeatedly cited also by Nikon of the Black Mountain) still in its purely Studite form may be recognized in the Georgian 'Great Synaxarion,' translated for the Iviron Lavra by its dean (vicar hegumen, *kathegoumenos* / καθηγούμενος) George the Hagiorite (Mtatsmindeli) [printed: *Itatsmindeli*] in 1038–1042, which was utilized, as is evident from its contents, in other Athonite monasteries as well, and in all probability in the Lavra of St. Athanasius, upon which Iviron was dependent; this Synaxarion is close unto the *Hypotyposis*, *Diatyposis*, and the excerpts from the Hagiorite Typikon in Nikon (who likewise designates this Typikon a Synaxarion); it is a ktetorial typikon of Studite type (there are no all-night vigils; the number of kathismata is identical; festal antiphons at Matins), yet with local peculiarities (fasting regimen, Georgian commemorations). (The Synaxarion in Russian translation from manuscripts contemporary with St. George—Tiflis Ecclesiastical Museum MSS Nos. 193 and 222—is published in Archpriest Kekelidze, *Liturgicheskie gruzinskie pamyatniki*, pp. 228–272). From the 13th century complete Jerusalem Typika appear upon Mount Athos as well. The Typikon given by St. Sava of Serbia († 1237) unto the Hilandar Monastery upon Mount Athos refers for divine worship unto a certain 'Synaxarion,' perhaps that whose 14th-century manuscript is preserved at present in the Hilandar Library (MS No. 66/70)—of purely Jerusalem type (Dmitrievsky, A. A., 'Drevneyshiy Khilandarskiy Sinaksar po ustavu Ierusalimskomu,' *Trudy Kievskoy Dukhovnoy Akademii*, 1905, Vol. II, p. 473), just as it itself represents a mixture of Jerusalem practices (vigils) with local customs (fasting regimen). (The Hilandar Typikon from a manuscript contemporary with St. Sava was published by Bishop Demetrius, *Tipik Hilandarski*, Belgrade, 1898). Even after the introduction of the Jerusalem Typikon in the Athonite monasteries, previous and local customs continued to be maintained; for this reason this introduction did not extinguish the distinctive Hagiorite Typikon, but merely imparted unto it a Jerusalem coloring, as is evident from the very titles of its surviving manuscripts (none of which have been published). Thus the Greek Vatican Library MS *Codex Palatinus* No. 101 of 1474 is entitled: 'Typikon of the Lavra of St. Sabas and of the Lavra of St. Athanasius the Athonite'; in the same library, MS No. 38 of 1423: 'Typikon of the Holy and Great Monastery of St. Athanasius upon Mount Athos'; in the 1336 Typikon of the Laurentian Library in Florence, MS No. 15, there are 'Chapters of the Most Celebrated Ecclesiastical Ordinance of the Holy Mountain.' The most important Athonite monasteries possess at present written texts of their Typikon (of Jerusalem type); thus the Lavra of St. Athanasius in 15th–16th century manuscripts; the Pantokrator Monastery in a 15th-century manuscript. The ordinances of the Dionysiou Monastery were recorded in 1624 by its hieromonk Ignatius, and this record enjoyed great authority—copies thereof exist in many Athonite monasteries; thus for the St. Panteleimon Monastery there was made (in its library there is an 18th-century manuscript) a revised copy from the Dionysiou Typikon under the title: 'Accurate Typikon of the Ecclesiastical Office, Collected from St. Sabas and Diverse Others'; here, besides the Athanasian Typikon, references occur unto the Athonite Typika of the 'Right Choir,' of the 'Left Choir,' and the 'Great Typikon of the Adrianopolitan.' From the Dionysiou Typikon a copy was likewise made in 1875 for the Iviron Monastery under the title: 'Abridged Typikon in Conjunction with Local Custom' (all unpublished and unresearched). Yet unto this day, as ever, the Holy Mountain follows in divine worship unwritten traditions more than written texts; for this reason divine worship here is guided not so much by the Typikon as by the *typikaris* (a most honorable personage in the monastery), and in difficult cases by a council of *typikareis* (Dmitrievsky, A. A., 'Svyatogorskiy [afonskiy] Tipikon,' *Rukovodstvo dlya selskikh pastyrey*, 1897, Nos. 1, 3, 4, 5).

[^1698]: The repeated **"Lord, have mercy"** originally, in all probability, was repeated thrice, then 12 times, a trace of which remains at present at the conclusion of Lenten services and in Typika of the Studite recension; subsequently it could be increased unto 30, and then unto 40 times (in non-Chalcedonian Eastern churches even unto 60 and 100; thus among the Armenians, see above, p. 333); a trace of all these forms of practice is provided by the Horologion of the Moscow Synodal Typography Library MS No. 77/150/1223 of the 15th century, where at the First Hour **"Lord, have mercy"** is appointed 30 times, at the Third 40, and at the Ninth 12 times (fols. 20 and 38); in the Studite Typika for ordinary Hours 40 times, and for the Royal Hours 30 times (Typikon of the Moscow Typography Library MS No. 185/142/1206, fols. 20v and 7v); occasionally such a distinction is made: in Great Lent 50 times, in the Nativity (St. Philip's) and Apostles' (Sts. Peter and Paul's) Fasts 40 times, and on ordinary days 30 times (Horologion of the Imperial Public Library MS Q. I No. 67 of the 15th–16th [printed: XV–XII] centuries, fol. 154v); at the inter-hours in the majority of manuscripts 30 times (see below). Distinctive in the Ethiopian Horologion of the 14th century: 41 or 51 times: Turayev, B. A., *Chasoslov efiopskoy tserkvi*, pp. 39, 49, and elsewhere.

[^1699]: *Typikon* of the Moscow Rumyantsev Museum, Sevastyanov Collection No. 35/491 of the 13th century, fol. 122, and Moscow Synodal Library MS No. 381 of the 14th century, fol. 131v remark concerning these troparia that regarding them 'there is no agreement—each recites which he wisheth, for this reason there is no coincidence among manuscripts: each offereth his own.'

[^1717]: On Fridays of all fasts according unto this Typikon, in place of Compline, a special service was appointed: *hē presbeia* (ἡ πρεσβεία, intercession, supplication; cf. *preces* in Western offices), consisting of two parts: the first, after the exclamation **“Blessed is the Kingdom”** and the Great Litany, consisted of three pairs of Theotokia (with an intercalary **“Glory... Both now...”** proclaimed by the deacon within each pair), separated by Little Litanies; the second part was analogous unto the present Moleben, consisting of Psalm 112 [113], **“God is the Lord,”** a troparion, and a canon unto the Mother of God with the customary inter-ode responses, after which followed the Trisagion, after the **“Our Father”** a troparion, and a litany (augmented). The service was celebrated in white vestments. Dmitrievsky, *Typika*, pp. 874–877.

[^1785]: In the ancient Psalters (for example, one from the library of the Monastery of Xeropotamou on Mount Athos), the 'refrains' (*pripevy*) unto the chosen psalms—designated here as the 'work of Kyr Philotheus' and taking the place of the present-day Megalynaria (*velichaniya*)—have the following form: for the Nativity of the Most Holy Theotokos, Entry, and Annunciation: **"Rejoice, O full of grace, Mary, the Lord is with Thee; alleluia"** (*"Радуйся обрадованная Марие, Господь с Тобою, аллилуиа"*); for the Exaltation: **"Thou hast given a banner unto them that fear Thee, that they may flee from before the face of the bow: Thy Cross, O Christ God"** (*"Дал еси знамение боящимся Тебе еже убежати от лица лука крест Твой Христе Боже"*, LXX Ps 59:6); for the Apostle John the Theologian: **"Come, all ye, let us praise the son of thunder, John, the virgin, the confidant of Christ"** (*"Приидите вси сына громова восхвалим Иоанна, девственника, Христова наперсника"*); for the Great Martyr Demetrius: **"Come, ye lovers of the martyrs, let us praise Demetrius the Myrrh-gusher, zealous follower of the Savior"** (*"Приидите мучениколюбцы, восхвалим мироточного Димитрия, ревнителя Спасова"*); for St. Chrysostom: **"Come, all ye in unison, let us praise John Chrysostom, the universal teacher"** (*"Приидите вси согласно восхвалим Иоанна Златоуста, общаго учителя"*); for the Apostle Andrew: **"Come, all ye in unison, let us praise Andrew the First-called, the kinsman of Peter"** (*"Приидите все согласно восхвалим Андрея первозванного, Петрова сродника"*); for St. Sabbas: **"Come, all ye in unison, let us praise Sabbas the All-consecrated, the boast of the Cappadocians"** (*"Приидите вси согласно восхвалим Савву всеосвященного, Каппадоков похвалу"*); for St. Nicholas: **"Come, all ye earthborn, let us praise the hierarch Nicholas, instructor of the people of Myra"** (*"Приидите вси земнороднии восхвалим иерарха Николая, миряном наставника"*); for the Martyr Eustratius and his companions: **"Come, let us praise Eustratius and Auxentius, Eugenius and Mardarius, and glorious Orestes"** (*"Приидите Евстратия, Авксентия восхвалим, Евгения и Мардария и Ореста славного"*); for the Nativity of Christ: **"Come, with the angelic choirs let us cry aloud: Glory to God in the highest, born in a cave"** (*"Приидите с ангельскими лики возгласим: слава в вышних Богу, рожденному в вертепе"*); for Basil the Great: **"Come, all ye earthborn, let us praise Basil the Great, the boast of the Caesareans"** (*"Приидите все земленороднии в восхвалим Василия Великого кесареом похвалу"*); for Theophany: **"My Jesus is baptized by a servant in the river Jordan, illuminating all creation"** (*"Крещается Иисус мой от раба в Иорданстей реце просвещая всю тварь"*); for St. Theodosius (as also for St. Euthymius and St. Sabbas of Serbia): **"Come, all ye in unison, let us praise Theodosius the Cenobiarch, instructor of monks"** (*"Приидите вси согласно восхвалим Феодосия общежителя, иноком наставника"*); for Athanasius of Alexandria: **"Come, all ye in unison, let us praise great Athanasius, the glory of the Alexandrians"** (*"Приидите вси согласно восхвалим великаго Афанасия, александром славу"*); for Gregory the Theologian: **"Come, let us praise the depth of wisdom, the second confidant"** (*"Приидите, премудрости глубину возхвалим, второго наперсника"*); for the Three Hierarchs: **"Come, let us praise Basil, Gregory, and John Chrysostom, the champions of the Trinity"** (*"Приидите Василия, Григория восхвалим и Иоанна Златоустаго, Троицы поборники"*); on Meatfare Sunday: **"Turn back, O Lord, the captivity of our souls, and plant Thy fear within our hearts, O Planter of good things; alleluia"** (*"Возврати, Господи, пленение душ наших и страх Твой всади в сердца наша, Насадителю благих, аллилуиа"*); on Sunday of the Veneration of the Precious Cross: **"Thy Cross we adore, O Master..."** (*"Кресту Твоему покланяемся Владыко…"*, cf. LXX Ps 98:5); on Palm Sunday: **"Come, let us also, like the children, offer faith unto God as palm branches, and love as boughs"** (*"Приидите и мы яко отроцы принесем веру яко ваиа Богу, любовь же яко ветви"*); for St. George: **"Come, ye lovers of the martyrs, let us praise George the Victorious, the boast of the Cappadocians"** (*"Приидите мучениколюбцы, восхвалим победоносца Георгия, каппадоком похвалу"*); for Ascension: **"God is gone up with a shout, the Lord with the sound of a trumpet, to save the image of Adam"** (*"Взыде Бог в воскликновении Господь во гласе трубне, еже спасти образ адамов"*, LXX Ps 46:6); for the Nativity of John the Forerunner: **"Let us all in unison praise John the Baptist, the friend of the Bridegroom"** (*"Вси согласно восхвалим Иоанна Крестителя, друга Женихова"*); for the Apostles Peter and Paul: **"Let us praise the citizens of the Jerusalem on high, Peter and Paul, disciples of Christ"** (*"Вышняго Иерусалима гражданы восхвалим Петра и Павла, ученики Христовы"*); for St. Athanasius of Athos: **"Come, all ye in unison, let us praise Athanasius of Athos, the glory of monastics"** (*"Приидите все согласно восхвалим афонскаго Афанасия, инокующим славу"*); for the Transfiguration: **"My Jesus and Lord is transfigured upon Mount Tabor, having manifested His glory"** (*"Преобразуется Иисус мой и Господь на горе Фаворстей, показав славу свою"*); for the Dormition: **"[Thy choir] offers unto Thee a departure hymn, O Mary, for Thou hast translated from earthly unto eternal mansions"** (*"Песнь ти исходную приношает Марие, приставибося от земленых к вечным обителем"*); for the Beheading: **"Come, all ye, let us praise the friend of the Bridegroom, the reprover of Herod, John the Forerunner"** (*"Приидите вси Женихова друга восхвалим, Иродова обличителя, Предтечу Иоанна"*); after the Megalynaria: **"Glory to the Father... Glory to Thee, O Holy Trinity; glory to Thee, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; Holy Trinity, glory to Thee"** (*"Слава Отцу… Слава Ти Троице Святая, слава Ти Отце и Сыне ио Святым Духом, Троице Святая, слава Тебе"*), **"Both now and ever... Rejoice, O full of grace, Mary, the Lord is with Thee, and for Thy sake with us. Alleluia, alleluia, alleluia, glory to Thee, O God"** (*"И ныне и присно… Радуйся, обрадованная Марие, Господь с Тобою, и Тебе ради с нами. Алиилуиа 3, слава Тебе Боже"*). These refrains are also significant in that they demonstrate which feasts were regarded as the most important. In the printed Serbian Menaion of 1536, unto these refrains are added refrains for Pentecost: **"Glory to Thee, O Trinity: Father, Word [printed: *Лове*], and Holy Spirit; glory to Thee, O God"** (*"Слава Ти Троице, Отче и Лове и Душе Святый, слава Тебе Боже"*), six refrains unto the Mother of God at the Polyeleos, appended alternately unto the verses of Psalm 134 [LXX 134], and refrains for the 9th Ode. (Archimandrite Antonin [Kapustin], *Zametki poklonnika sv. Gory* [Notes of a Pilgrim to the Holy Mountain], Kyiv, 1864, p. 252). In the Kraków edition Horologion of 1491, there are cited in one place two Megalynaria for each feast—one differing from the present-day one, and the other identical with the present (fol. 270v ff.); in another place (fol. 163v), only the present-day ones are given.

[^1806]: The extent unto which this Typikon was implemented in the Athonite Iviron Lavra even prior unto the appearance there of a complete copy of it, under St. Euthymius, its second hegumen († 1012 AD), is evident from the life of the latter, compiled by his successor and translator of the described *Synaxarion*, Gregory Mtatsmindeli [the Hagiorite]. According unto the biographer, Euthymius stood in church without leaning upon a staff and without leaning against the wall. The brethren standing by the wall were likewise forbidden to lean against it. The younger brethren stood in the middle of the church in two or three rows, reverently, with hands lowered; when appointed, they sat upon their benches, which likewise stood in the middle of the church in two or three rows. Euthymius appointed two God-fearing *epitropoi*-overseers, one senior, and the other junior, subordinate unto the first. The junior *epitropos* was sent through the cells and brought unto church any brother who remained in his cell. If a brother did not arrive for the Six Psalms, Euthymius judged him according unto the rules of the father (Theodore) in the following manner: he did not punish one who labored much by day and had no time to sleep—his daytime labors were imputed unto him as an ascetic struggle of prayer. But those having leisure or performing petty tasks, as well as priests, deacons, and readers, he subjected unto such punishment: he compelled the younger to make 100 prostrations before the altar, while the elderly and infirm he deprived that day of wine and food. During the service, the senior *epitropos* stood at the doors and inquired of any departing brother concerning the reason for his exit; if no essential need was apparent, he did not permit him to leave; but in case of genuine necessity, he let him out only upon condition that he return immediately. In the event that the one who exited was late, he was subjected unto a rebuke; and if he was late a second time, on that day he was not admitted into the refectory, but was kept upon dry bread or sentenced unto 100 prostrations. Unto those laboring untiringly, the holy father permitted leaving the church after the Six Psalms to rest, but with the obligation to return by the second bell ringing. Unto the weak among the brethren, he allowed standing in the narthex and leaning upon a staff, and unto the sick even to sit. Younger brethren conversing, laughing, or taking each other by the hand were immediately reproved; and if caught a second time, they were reported unto the holy father, who judged them strictly. One guilty of quarreling or fighting was brought unto the cell of the holy father, who commanded him to lie upon the ground; the guilty brother, having made a bow, lay down; one of the brethren held him by the head, another by the feet, and a third struck him over his cassock with 30, 40, or 60 blows of a belt. If anyone interceded for the one being punished, he was subjected unto the very same punishment. Those punished, kneeling, bowed before the holy father and with tears in their eyes begged his forgiveness. Thereafter they lay upon the ground and remained in such a posture for two or three weeks (?). From time to time the holy father came unto them and comforted them in word and deed; unto the same he urged other monastic officials. The executor of the punishment likewise comforted the punished brother and presented him with a cassock, or a scapular, or some other necessary item. The punished regarded the punishment they endured as though they had suffered it from the hands of Christ Himself. Above all, the holy father took care that no one should give false testimony or witness in any matter without knowing its circumstances; whoever was convicted of this he expelled from the monastery. The blessed father provided the brethren with everything, down unto needle and thread, and thereby deprived all of an occasion to sell or acquire anything without his command; a brother could not transfer his property unto another without the consent of the holy father. If anyone transgressed the will of the holy father and acquired clothing or anything valuable, he strictly rebuked him. We have seen more than once how he burned in the monastic enclosure a scapular, clothing, or a klobuk (*koukoulion*). Frequently he said unto the brethren: "The saints denied themselves even water. Is it not sufficient for us to take food twice a day, and that in abundance—with wine and various dishes?" Therefore, if anyone bought wine and oil, the entire purchase was poured into the refuse pit. Kekelidze, *Liturgicheskie gruzinskie pamyatniki* [Liturgical Georgian Monuments], pp. 305 ff., 478 ff.

[^1807]: MS of the Moscow Synodal Library No. 330/380, fol. 214.

[^1808]: Golubinsky, E. E., *Istoriya russkoy tserkvi* [History of the Russian Church], vol. 1, p. 581.

[^1809]: A remnant of this practice exists presently in the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra—its closure on Monday and Tuesday of the first week of Great Lent.

[^1810]: The extent unto which St. Theodosius himself followed this requirement of the Studite-Alexis Typikon introduced by him in his monastery is attested by an incident recounted in his life: when the cellarer came unto him with a request to send some unoccupied monk to chop firewood, the venerable father, with the words: "I am unoccupied," set unto this work himself, which prompted the entire brotherhood to take up axes. St. Sergius, founder of the Holy Trinity Lavra, likewise was not ashamed of any labor, neither cooking nor shoemaking, and worked for two; his special tasks were the milling of flour for prosphora and baking them, rolling candles, and cooking kolyva (Golubinsky, E. E., *Prepodobny Sergy Radonezhsky i sozdannaya im Troitskaya lavra* [St. Sergius of Radonezh and the Trinity Lavra Created by Him], Sergiyev Posad, 1892, p. 26).

The customary mitigation of a severe regimen accompanied by fear of altering the text of an ancient Typikon to eliminate a contradiction.

[^1817]: Dmitrievsky, *Τυπικά*, pp. 671, 678. In the Typikon of Empress Irene this order of election develops into a very intricate rite. Upon three identical parchments was written the prayer: "O our Master Jesus Christ, through the intercessions of our all-immaculate Lady the Mother of God Full of Grace, reveal unto us sinners whether Thou deemest worthy of governance over us our sister [Name]"; the parchments, sealed by the patroness of the monastery, were placed upon the holy table for the duration of the Vigil and the Divine Liturgy. Immediately following the Divine Liturgy, the Trisagion is chanted and the troparia: **"Have mercy on us, O Lord..."** (*Pomiluy nas, Gospodi...*); **"Glory..."**, **"O Lord, look down from heaven and behold..."** (*Gospodi, prizri s nebese i vizhd...*); **"Both now..."**, **"O Only-Begotten, Consubstantial..."** (*Yedinorodne, Yedinosushchne...*). The priest (fully vested) intones the Insistent Litany, unto which he appends the petition: "Furthermore we pray that the Lord will show us one worthy of governance over us"; the sisters chant: "Lord, have mercy" thirty times with fifteen prostrations, lifting up their hands with the prayer: "O God Who knowest the heart, reveal unto us sinners one worthy of governance over us." The priest, having made three prostrations before the holy table with the same prayer, takes one of the parchments, and following verification of the seal, the woman who sealed it opens it; the elected nun, after three prostrations, takes from a special place before the sanctuary the Typikon and the staff, stands in the abbess's stall, is greeted by all, glory is sent up unto God, and the customary dismissal is given by the priest (Typikon of Empress Irene, ch. 11; Migne, *Patrologia Graeca*, vol. 127).

[^1830]: This office, which replaced the order for the kneading of dough of the Studite-Alexis Typikon (see above, p. 426) and was retained by certain Slavic MSS, consisted in the following: After Matins and the First Hour, if leavening of bread takes place in the bakery, the serving priest proceeds thither in epitrachelion with the censer and preceded by a candle, as for a Litiya, with the chanting of the Litiya sticheron of the temple with its Theotokion, accompanied by the brethren; in the bakery a Little Litany takes place and the prayer for the blessing of loaves, as at the Vigil (only instead of "these loaves," there is said: "this kneaded dough"); holy water (*hagiasma*) is poured into the leaven and the dismissal is given: "May Christ our true God, through the intercessions of His all-immaculate Mother and of all the saints, grant a heavenly reward unto our fathers and brethren who labor in this monastery, and have mercy upon us..." (MSS of the Moscow Synodal Library of the 16th century No. 379, fol. 290; No. 380, fol. 22; Slavic No. 678/386, fol. 233, and other MSS).

[^1834]: It is characteristic, however, that even in the Palestinian Lavra of St. Sabbas, whence issued our present Typikon, they are guided presently by this Constantinopolitan edition of the Typikon.

[^1835]: Plutarch, *De virtute*; Pape, W., *Griechisch-Deutsches Handwörterbuch*, Braunschweig, 1880, pp. 1162, 262. Later, the word *typos* (τύπος) was assigned the meaning of an edict, a law; the word already possesses such a meaning in Philostratus († 240) and the 5th-century ecclesiastical historian Socrates (Sophocles, E., *Greek Lexicon of the Roman and Byzantine Periods*, New York–Leipzig, 1888, p. 1099); yet *typos* with this meaning does not form the adjective *typikos* (τυπικός) in any author; among ancient Christian writers (Clement of Alexandria, Origen, Basil the Great) this adjective signifies "symbolic," "prefigurative" (see *ibid.*). The root meaning of the word cannot entirely vanish in all instances of its usage; and if *typos* sometimes denotes a law or rule, it is of a special character; thus, in Gregory Nazianzen it is used in relation to the Symbol of Faith [the Creed]; in Justinian it signifies the Divine Law (for citations see Sophocles); Emperor Constans designated his well-known confessional edict by this term [*Typos*]. But a religious law or rule differs substantially from every other kind, and its distinctiveness is well expressed by the basic meaning of the word *typos*. More precisely than "rule" [*ustav*], *typikon* (τυπικόν) could be translated as "ordinal" [*chinovnik*], Lat. *ordinarium* (thus translated by Toscani, *Ad typica graecorum*, Romae, 1864, p. 5).

[^1836]: The word *diatyposis* (διατύπωσις) is first used in ecclesiastical literature by the *Apostolic Constitutions* (4th–early 5th c.) applied, seemingly, to ecclesiastical constitution in general or, more immediately, to the rites of ordination and the Liturgy (*Apostolic Constitutions*, VIII, 3).

[^1837]: Golubtsov, A., *Sobornye chinovniki i osobennosti sluzhby po nim* [Cathedral Ordinals and Peculiarities of Service according to Them], p. 10 // *Chteniya v Imperatorskom Obshchestve Istorii i Drevnostey Rossiyskikh*, 1907, Book 3.

[^1838]: Dmitrievsky, A., *Opisanie liturgicheskikh rukopisey, khranyashchikhsya v bibliotekakh pravoslavnogo Vostoka* [Description of Liturgical Manuscripts Preserved in the Libraries of the Orthodox East], Vol. I, *Typika* (Τυπικά), 1895, p. 833.

[^1839]: Dmitrievsky, A., Review of the book *Tserkovny ustav (tipik), ego obrazovanie i sudba v grecheskoy i russkoy Tserkvi* [The Church Rule (Typikon), Its Formation and Destiny in the Greek and Russian Church] by Prof. I. Mansvetov // *Khristianskoe Chtenie*, 1888.

[^1854]: "Tiyazhkaya" [*tyazhkaya*], *tas bareas* (τὰς βαρέας; not found in the nominative case), may signify, however, not the instruments of ringing, but the strokes themselves upon the semantron (or bell), as is evident from the following expressions in the Greek Euchologion and Typikon: "he strikes the heavy strokes (*tas bareas*) upon the iron [semantron]," "he strikes the heavy strokes alternately, and upon the completion of the heavy strokes he descends." The Russian expression "vo vsya tyazhkaya" [ringing all the heavy bells/strikes] is not used in Greek (Kazansky, P., *O prizyve k bogosluzheniyu v vostochnoy Tserkvi* [On the Summoning to Divine Service in the Eastern Church] // *Trudy pervogo arkheologicheskogo syezda v Moskve 1869 g.*, Moscow, 1871, p. 313).

[^1855]: Archimandrite Antonin (Kapustin) recounts concerning the bell-chime for the All-Night Vigil in the Athonite St. Panteleimon Monastery (Rossikon): "They struck the great 'semantron' [*bilo*] in the bell-tower; they beat upon it at first slowly, then swiftly and briskly with rising and falling of pitches, with their playful permutation, with a rapid transition from lower to higher and vice versa, or by slowly modulating them through semitones with gradual fading to complete cessation, and with an unexpected resurrection to new vivacity and vigor. Such tolling or beating was renewed three times after pauses for the rest of the 'artist,' as the Greeks called the ringer, visibly moved by his art; and each time he allowed them to hear new tone combinations, diverse from the first strike of the mallet to the last. This music continued for half an hour" (*Zametki poklonnika Svyatoy Gory* [Notes of a Pilgrim to the Holy Mountain], Kyiv, 1864, p. 29).

[^1856]: Gregory of Tours, *De virtutibus S. Martini* [On the Virtues/Miracles of St. Martin], III, 22; II, 28; *Historia Francorum* [History of the Franks], 23.
