[^1859]: By the word *kōdōn* (κώδων) the LXX designates the bells on the vesture of the High Priest (Exod 28:34). Presently among the Greeks *kōdōn* denotes a small bell of about 3 pounds in weight; a larger one is called *kampanon* (κάμπανον). On Athos, *kōdōn* designates the altar bell in the shape of an inverted bowl with a small striker above; this bell is struck at the conclusion of the Proskomedia, and at this signal the brethren, standing each in his place, having removed their klobuks and bowed low, commemorate their relatives, the living and the reposed, while the priest in the altar extracts particles; at the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, at the signal of this bell they fall prostrate during the transfer of the Holy Gifts, and then at a new signal they rise. The word "kandiya" does not exist in the Greek language, neither ancient nor modern (Kazansky, P., *O prizyve k bogosluzheniyu v vostochnoy Tserkvi* [On the Summoning to Divine Service in the Eastern Church] // *Trudy pervogo arkheologicheskogo syezda v Moskve 1869 g.*, Moscow, 1871, p. 316).

[^1860]: According to Allatius, Michael Psellos said in an oration to Emperor Constantine Monomakhos (1042–1054): "The sacred *kōdōn* shall awaken thee, and thou shalt prostrate thyself upon the sacred dais" (*Graeciae orthodoxae scriptores*, Romae, 1652, VII, 3). According to Pachymeres, upon the return to his see of Patriarch Joseph I of Constantinople (in 1282), he entered the patriarchate amidst the ringing of numerous church *kōdōnes* (κωδώνων) (*Michael Paleologus...*, VII, 3). According to Eustathius, Metropolitan of Thessalonica, the Crusaders who captured Thessalonica were always agitated by the sounds of semantra, but remained calm upon hearing the sounds of "great *kōdōnes*" summoning to psalmody at the Church of St. Demetrius, evidently because these latter sounds were familiar to them (*De Thessalonica a Latinis capta* // Migne, *Patrologia Graeca*, Vol. 136, col. 121).

[^1874]: Thus, the Liturgy of the Apostle James has the following beginning (that which is found in its earliest 11th-century Rossano manuscript is printed in italics, and in later ones in ordinary type): *Deacon: Let us pray to the Lord. People: Lord, have mercy.* Priest: In the Name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, now and ever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen. *Deacon: Let us stand aright. In peace let us pray to the Lord. Priest, a prayer: Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit...* and 2 more prayers. *Deacon: Master, give the blessing. Priest, a prayer...* etc. (Petrovsky, A., *Apostolskie liturgii vostochnoy Tserkvi* [Apostolic Liturgies of the Eastern Church], St. Petersburg, 1897, Appendix, pp. 1–3). The Liturgy of the Evangelist Mark, used from antiquity in the Alexandrian Church, begins according to the Rossano Codex: Deacon: Stand for prayer. Priest: Peace to all. People: And to thy spirit. Deacon: Pray. People: Lord, have mercy. Priest, 1st prayer (Swainson, C. A., *The Greek Liturgies*, Cambridge, 1884, pp. 2–3). But in its more ancient redaction (in Renaudot, *Liturgiarum Orientalium Collectio*, Paris, 1716, II, 184) it begins with three prayers of the priest, to each of which the people respond Amen; then the Deacon: Give the peace. Priest: The grace... be with you all, etc. The Liturgy of the Mesopotamian Church, attributed to the Apostle Thaddaeus—a Nestorian liturgy, but in its ancient core (printed here in italics) originating before the Nestorian heresy and, in any case, not later than the 7th c.—begins: *In the Name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace, good will among men, now and ever...* Choir: *Amen and Our Father.* Priest, bowing his head, makes the exclamation: *Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now...* Choir: *Amen.* Deacon: *Pray. Peace be with you.* Priest, a prayer (Petrovsky, A., *Apost. lit.*, p. 61).

[^1923]: In the Roman Catholic Church the Psalms enjoy in divine service no less wide an application than in the Orthodox Church, and likewise initiate every office (with the exception of the Mass, where individual verses of Psalms are sung rather than entire Psalms). More restricted is the liturgical use of the Psalter in the Anglican Episcopal Church; more restricted still in the Scottish Presbyterian, and it is almost completely excluded among the other Protestants.

[^1926]: For this one would have to abbreviate the word "God," just as in the Hebrew *Hallelujah* the word *Yahweh* (*Jehovah*) is abbreviated to *Yah* (cf. our Russian *spasibo* [from *Spasi Bog*]). The word is used untranslated in Roman Catholic (*alleluja*) and Protestant (*Hallelujah*) worship; but in Anglican worship it is translated: "Praise ye the Lord."

[^1927]: The Raskolniks [Old Believers] in this refrain pronounce *Alleluia* twice, replacing the third *Alleluia* with what is, in their opinion, its equivalent: **"Glory to Thee, O God."** Such a manner of chanting *Alleluia* in this refrain was prescribed by the Stoglav Council (1551), which designated the custom of tripling the *Alleluia* a "Latin heresy" (the Latins, according to the Council, "do not glorify the Trinity, but quadruple It," which the Council apparently deduced in some fashion from the *Filioque*). The Council appealed in defense of the twofold *Alleluia* to the *Life of St. Euphrosynus of Pskov* (†1481), to whom the Most Holy Theotokos Herself "revealed and forbade concerning the threefold *Alleluia*, commanding Orthodox Christians to say the twofold *Alleluia*" (*Stoglav*, ch. 42 / ed. D. Kozhanchikov, St. Petersburg, 1863, p. 148). In this *Life*, composed shortly before the Council, it is recounted moreover that St. Euphrosynus was in great sorrow concerning the mystery of the most holy *Alleluia* (that is, whether one ought to double or triple it) and, failing to find an answer to this in Russia, undertook a journey to Greece, where from a conversation with Patriarch Joseph and from the practice of the Hagia Sophia Cathedral and all the monasteries he became convinced of the necessity of doubling the *Alleluia* and introduced this custom into his monastery, on account of which he was denounced and persecuted by the people of Pskov headed by the Priest Job. The question of the *Alleluia* occupied other church figures of that era as well; even prior to St. Euphrosynus, St. Photius, Metropolitan of Moscow, in response to an inquiry from the people of Pskov on this point, expressed himself in an epistle of 1419 in favor of the threefold *Alleluia*; Bishop Gennadius of Novgorod did likewise in 1491. Consequently, in the 15th century the question was disputed, and ground for dispute was provided by diversity not only in practice, but also in liturgical books themselves. Whereas in some Greek and Slavic manuscripts of the 13th–16th centuries it was indicated to triple the *Alleluia* (for example, the Horologia of the Rumyantsev Museum, Sevastyanov Collection Nos. 496–497 of the 13th–16th c., the *Ordinance on Chanting the Great Compline [Mephemon]* in MS of the St. Sophia Library of the St. Petersburg Academy No. 1264); in others it was indicated to double it (for example, Slavic Typika of the Moscow Synodal Library Nos. 383 and 384 of the 14th–15th c., Potrebnik No. 259 of the 16th c.). Not only in Russia, but also in the East there was no uniformity in this regard, as witnessed by the observations of so impartial an observer in this matter as the monk Arseny Sukhanov in his *Proskynitarion* (*Pravoslavny Palestinsky Sbornik*, Vol. VII, fasc. 3, St. Petersburg, 1889, pp. 44, 205, 225). The presiding hierarch of the Stoglav Council himself, Metropolitan Macarius, in his *Great Menion Reader* (*Chet'yi Minei*) denounces those who double the *Alleluia* and symbolically grounds its threefold nature (April, fol. 816), on which basis scholars even suspect in the decree of the Stoglav Council, as well as in the remarks of the *Proskynitarion* concerning the twofold *Alleluia*, a later interpolation. (Concerning all this, see: Macarius, Metropolitan, *Istoriya russkoy Tserkvi* [History of the Russian Church], St. Petersburg, 1868, Vol. VIII, pp. 131ff.; Odintsov, N., *Poryadok obshchestvennago i chastnago bogosluzheniya v drevney Rossii do XVI v.* [The Order of Public and Private Worship in Ancient Russia up to the 16th Century], St. Petersburg, 1881, pp. 243–249; Dmitrievsky, A., "Bogosluzhenie v russkoy Tserkvi v pervye pyat' vekov" [Divine Service in the Russian Church in the First Five Centuries], in *Pravoslavny Sobesednik*, 1882, VII–VIII, pp. 359–362; Nikolsky, K., Archpriest, *Posobie k izucheniyu ustava bogosluzheniya pravoslavnoy tserkvi* [Manual for the Study of the Typikon of Divine Worship of the Orthodox Church], 7th ed., St. Petersburg, 1907, pp. 178–179). Originally and for a long period the *Alleluia*, following the example of the Old Testament church, was chanted, of course, neither twice nor thrice, but once; so it was in the 3rd century, according to the testimony of the *Testamentum* and Tertullian (see Introductory Chapter, p. 108). The dogma of the Trinity, brought to the forefront in theology by the heresies of the 3rd century, compelled Christians to make it the primary subject of their confession; hence, presumably, the extensive application of the number 3 in subsequent worship (the three-psalm group, the three-antiphon structure); hence could easily arise the tripling of the *Alleluia*. But direct witnesses to such a manner of chanting the *Alleluia* prior to the 13th century are lacking; on the contrary, wherever the chanting of the *Alleluia* is discussed, it is presented as if it were uttered once (Cassian, *De institutis coenobiorum*, II, 11; Horologion of the Sinai Library No. 863, 8th c.). Judging from the fact that in the Catholic Church the *Alleluia* is chanted once, twice, and thrice (*Missale Romanum*, Campidonae, 1823, p. 255; *Breviarium*, pp. 22, 29, and others), one may suppose that this threefold manner of its chanting existed. With three antiphons, on the first antiphon the *Alleluia* could be chanted once, on the second twice, and on the third thrice; thus in the order of monastic burial according to MS of the Moscow Synodal Library No. 396 of the 13th–14th c. (Dmitrievsky, A., *ibid.*). The single *Alleluia* is preserved among us in Psalm 136 and at the Liturgy. In the Ethiopian Horologion after the psalms our current refrain apparently possesses its simplest and most ancient form: "*Alleluia*. Glory to the Lord," 3 times (Turayev, B., *Chasoslov efiopskoy tserkvi*, p. 63).

[^1951]: In the Roman Catholic Church the Hours contain all the component parts of our Hours, but in a different order. As with us, the Hours there all possess a single scheme, excluding Prime (the First Hour), which also among us in antiquity differed in structure from the other Hours, as is evident from the Sinai Horologion of the 8th–9th c., concerning which discussion will follow in the explanation of Chapter II of the Typikon. Following the usual beginning in the Roman Catholic Hours there follows a hymn, proper to each Hour, corresponding to our troparion, ending with *Alleluia* (on Sunday) or another refrain, an "antiphon" (on weekdays). Next follow three sections from Psalm 118 [LXX 118 / MT 119] (which is divided among all the Hours into 11 sections; two of them are read at Prime together with other psalms, and three at each of the remaining Hours without other psalms); these three sections of Psalm 118 at each of the Roman Catholic Hours impart to it the same three-psalm type that our Hours possess (Psalm 118 was chosen for the Hours, presumably, because it speaks of ceaseless meditation upon the Law of God, of glorifying God seven times a day, and the like). After the three sections of Psalm 118, ending with *Alleluia* or another antiphon, there follow two brief excerpts (*capitula*) from the New and Old Testaments (from the Epistles and the Prophets) with concluding refrains (*responsoria*), occasionally changing according to feasts (corresponding to our "verse of the Hour," as well as to paremias and prokeimena). After these *capitula* follows *Kyrie eleison, Christe eleison, Kyrie eleison* (corresponding to our "Lord, have mercy" 40 times) and the *Pater noster* ["Our Father"], again with responsories (the first of which consists of the final words of the Lord's Prayer: "and lead us not into temptation...", corresponding to our exclamation). Then follows the collect/prayer proper to the day (among us, to the Hour), which is preceded and concluded by exclamatory responsories: "The Lord be with you" (*Dominus vobiscum*), with the response: "And with thy spirit. Let us pray (*Oremus*)." After the prayer there is likewise a responsory: "Let us bless the Lord (*Benedicamus Domino*)." The exclamation "The Lord be with you," which is also pronounced by a deacon officiating at the Hours in the absence of a priest, and even in his presence with his permission, is replaced, if a lector reads the Hours, by another: "O Lord, hear my prayer, and let my cry come unto Thee" (*Breviarium Romanum*, pp. 52, 62, 43ff., 24ff.). These responsories surrounding the concluding prayer of the Hour clearly correspond to our priestly exclamation after the Prayer "Thou Who at all times." This is the Roman order of the Hours; the Ambrosian (Milanese) and Mozarabic orders are somewhat longer (Seredinsky, T., Priest, *O bogosluzhenii zapadnoy tserkvi* [On the Divine Worship of the Western Church], St. Petersburg, 1849, Article 2, p. 6). The proximity of the Roman Catholic Hours to our order of the Hours clearly bears witness, of course, to the fact that the order of the Hours in its fundamental structure was formed both there and here during the period of living fraternal communion between the two Churches. Closely related to both the Roman Catholic and Orthodox orders of the Hours are the Hours of the Eastern Monophysites, for example, the Copts. According to the ancient Horologion of the Copts, the order of the Hour consists of an introductory troparion (cf. the hymn of the Hour among the Roman Catholics), 12 psalms (a vestige of the "Order of the 12 Psalms"), a Gospel reading corresponding to the commemoration of the Hour (cf. *capitula* and paremias), troparia with verses and Theotokia identical to our first troparia, a verse of the Hour identical to ours, other troparia largely coinciding with our second troparia (after the Trisagion), the Symbol of Faith [Creed], the Trisagion with "Our Father," and a concluding prayer proper to the Hour (Archimandrite Porphyrius, *Doctrine, Divine Service... of the Copts*, pp. 57ff.). Still closer to our order are the Hours according to the Ethiopian Horologion of the Coptic Church (14th c.): after the usual beginning, 12 psalms, the Gospel, our troparia of the Hour and Theotokia, the verse of the Hour, the Trisagion, *Alleluia*, troparia largely coinciding with our troparia after the Trisagion, the Creed, *Kyrie eleison* 41 times, a brief penitential prayer beginning "Holy, Holy, Holy...", a dismissal troparion, the prayer "Thou Who at all times," *Kyrie eleison* 41 times, and a prayer of blessing common to all the Hours—with the invocation of a succession of saints by name, similar to our litany prayer **"Save, O God, Thy people"** (Turayev, B., *Chasoslov efiopskoy tserkvi*, pp. 47ff., 33ff., 39ff.). In both Horologia there is a special office similar to the conclusion of our Matins and the First Hour; but this office is not called an Hour.

[^1952]: Matt. 27:46; Mark 15:34.

[^1953]: Luke 23:46.

[^1954]: 1 Pet. 3:19–20.

[^1955]: Duhm, B. *Die Psalmen erklärt*. Tübingen, 1899, p. 213. Previously, on the basis of v. 10: "look upon the face of Thine anointed [Christ]," this psalm was referred to David's yearning for the temple during his flight from Absalom; but by "anointed" [*christ*] the psalmist could also have understood the superior of the temple or any king as the supreme guardian of the temple.

[^1956]: Obscure expressions in the psalm: "For the sparrow [bird]" [Ps. 83:4 / MT 84:3], literally from the Hebrew "even the bird," that is, just like a bird. "Blessed is the man whose help is in Thee" [v. 6 / MT 5], whom God helps on the pilgrimage to the temple, which is especially arduous from the lands of the diaspora. "He hath set ascents in his heart," that is, he undertakes the journey to the temple across both mountains and "into the vale of tears [weeping]" [v. 7 / MT 6]—a valley of weeping on account of its barrenness and perils, "unto the place which He hath appointed," keeping ever before him the longed-for goal of the pilgrimage; but from the Hebrew: "they (those journeying to Jerusalem) make it (the valley of weeping) a place of springs"—God miraculously sends them water. "For the lawgiver shall give blessings" [v. 8 / MT 7]: God, Who prescribed in the Law such pilgrimages to the temple (three times a year), blesses these pilgrims (from the Hebrew: "the early rain also covereth it—the valley—with blessings"), and they "shall go from strength to strength," that is, during the arduous pilgrimage their vigor will continually increase, whereas with ordinary travelers the opposite occurs. In relation to the crucified Christ, the vale of weeping is earthly life with its sufferings and death, through which He elevated His human nature unto heaven.

[^1957]: Gunkel, H. *Ausgewählte Psalmen*. Göttingen, 1904, p. 133. This author, as well as Duhm cited above, are rationalists, but this did not prevent them from providing an excellent explanation of the present psalms, as well as certain others.

[^1958]: As we have seen, in antiquity these troparia actually served as the prayers of the Hour, since the present two concluding prayers of the Hours did not exist; moreover, in certain ancient Horologia the concluding prayers have the form of our present troparia. Hence the petitionary character of all troparia of the Hours.

[^1964]: In the Coptic and Ethiopian Horologia at the Ninth Hour there are appointed Psalms 95–115 (omitting 112 in the first Horologion and 113 in the second), the Gospel of Luke 9:10–17 (on the feeding of the 5,000—a connection with the agape feast?), our troparion and Theotokion after the Psalms, but between them at "Glory" an additional special one, containing a prayer for worthy communion (connection with the agape feast?); among the troparia after the Trisagion—the second is different from ours, but likewise concerning the thief; the prayer is different, but of the same content (Turayev, B. *Chasoslov efiopskoy tserkvi*, pp. 63ff.; Bishop Porphyrius Uspensky, *Verouchenie, bogosluzhenie... koptov*, pp. 66ff.).—At the Roman Catholic None (Ninth Hour), the hymn entreats God, as the steadfast strength of all things (*rerum Deus tenax vigor*), abiding motionless within Himself and determining the succession of the hours of daylight by consecutive declines, to grant light at the evening (of life), from which our life might never depart, but that holy death might be followed by eternal glory as a reward; then three assigned sections of Psalm 118 [LXX 118 / MT 119]; the antiphon to them on weekdays (in place of the Sunday *Alleluia*) is "Look upon me" (*Aspice in me*); the *capitulum* is 1 Cor. 6:20 ("For ye are bought with a price: therefore glorify God..."); the responsories to it on Sunday: "I cried with my whole heart; hear me, O Lord"; "I will search out Thy statutes"; "Cleanse Thou me from secret faults, O Lord, and spare Thy servant from the sins of others"; on weekdays: "Deliver me (according to the Vulgate: 'redeem me', *redime*), O Lord, and have mercy on me"; "My foot hath stood in uprightness"; "From my secret faults..."; another *capitulum*, Isa. 14:1: "Her time is near to come, and her days shall not be prolonged, for the Lord will have mercy on Jacob, and will yet choose Israel"; responsories to it: "Upon thee, Jerusalem, the Lord shall arise, and His glory shall be seen upon thee"; "Come, O Lord, and do not tarry"; "Forgive the transgressions of Thy people." Thus the Roman Catholic service of the Ninth Hour does not directly commemorate the sacred events of this hour and in content differs little from the services of the Third and Sixth Hours (as we shall see).

[^2055]: Citations on p. 62. In accordance with this, on Mount Athos the introductory psalm at the Vigil, according to the description of Archimandrite Antonin Kapustin (from 1859), is executed in the following manner: "The reading of the introductory psalm began rapidly, seemingly presaging a very swift course of the service. But at the midpoint of verse 28 of the psalm the reading ceased. There began what chanters call the *anoixantaria* (*ἀνοιξαντάρια*), that is, the remaining words of the verse and the remaining verses of the psalm, chanted by both choirs alternately. The first choir, or rather the chanter (*psaltēs*) of the first choir, began to intone the words: 'When Thou openest (*ἀνοίξαντος*) Thy hand...' etc., and he concluded the psalm two hours later. After each verse the singers alternated, standing afterward, drenched in sweat, in the rank of refrain-chanters" (*Zametki poklonnika sv. gory* [Notes of a Pilgrim to the Holy Mountain]. Kyiv, 1864, p. 31).

[^2063]: "Who hast pledged (*κατεγγυήσας*) unto us the promised Kingdom," that is, given under surety; "unto the promised good things, Who hast made a way for us"—*διὰ τῶν ἤδη κεχαρισμένων ἀγαθῶν ὁδοποιήσας ἡμῖν*, that is, guiding us unto the Kingdom through the good things already bestowed; so according to the Greek text in Goar, *Euchologion*, p. 29; in the Slavonic, "by the promised [goods]" (*obeshchannymi*) is an inaccurate translation and the punctuation marks are improperly placed.

[^2080]: Phil. 4:7.

[^2081]: Unto the petition "for the good estate (*εὐσταθείας*) of the holy churches of God" is joined the supplication "and for the union of all," *καὶ τῆς τῶν πάντων ἑνώσεως*, in ancient Slavonic "for the gathering together (*sŭvŭkuplenii*) of all"—a union, as the masculine/neuter gender *πάντων* shows, not of the holy churches of God (although in ancient texts of the litany it read: "for the union of all, *πασῶν*, the holy churches of God"—see below), but of all in a general sense—according unto St. Symeon of Thessalonica, "for the union of all in orthodox faith, love, and a life well-pleasing unto God" (*On Divine Prayer*, chap. 332/296). Perhaps there is here an intentional indefiniteness, pointing unto all humanity and therein, consequently, unto the heterodox Churches (see in greater detail concerning this: Z. *O kakom soedinenii vsekh molimsya my v 3-m proshenii velikoy ektenii* [For What Union of All Do We Pray in the 3rd Petition of the Great Litany?] // *Rukovodstvo dlya selskikh pastyrey*, 1910, No. 24; Paktov, N. *K iz"yasneniyu tret'yego prosheniya velikoy ektenii* [Toward the Explanation of the Third Petition of the Great Litany] // Ibid., Nos. 36–38).

[^2082]: "Diaconate in Christ"—the addition "in Christ" corresponds unto "honourable" with "presbyterate" and at the same time more closely defines the "diaconate" ("ministry") as ecclesiastical; "clergy" (*prichte*)—*κλήρου*; "people" (*lyudekh*)—*λαοῦ*.

[^2083]: "Palace" (*palate*)—*παλατίου*, palace (properly the Palatine Hill in Rome), the court, the civil government.

[^2084]: "Suffering" (*strazhdushchikh*)—*καμνόντων*, that is, those engaged in heavy labor; in ancient litanies: "for those who are in the mines, in imprisonment, in exile" (see below).

[^2085]: John 17:1, 2, 4; Prov. 16:4; Heb. 2:10.

[^2087]: Clement of Rome, *Epistle to the Corinthians*, 59:2, 4–61:3. In the well-known 5th-century Codex Alexandrinus this section of Clement's epistle is absent; for this reason it remained unknown until 1875, when Philotheos Bryennios discovered it in a Jerusalem manuscript.

[^2088]: By this light is shed upon the original meaning of the first petitions of the Great Litany: these were petitions for that peace which is requisite for the offering of the Eucharist, and whose outward expression was the kiss of peace prior unto this offering. In the Persian-Nestorian Liturgy attributed unto the Apostle Thaddaeus, before the prayer for the hallowing of the Gifts, the deacon says: "In your minds pray for peace with us"; before communion: "Let us pray for our peace among ourselves"; after communion—the same (*Sobranie drevnikh liturgiy* [Collection of Ancient Liturgies], St. Petersburg, 1874–1878, IV, pp. 22, 30, 36). In the Liturgy of Nestorius at the beginning of its "canon," the deacon says: "Let us pray for our peace among ourselves" (ibid., p. 47). In the Gallican and Mozarabic Liturgies, instead of this, the priest or deacon says: "Offer peace unto one another." The choir responds: "My peace I give unto you" thrice with the refrain of the lesser doxology, and then the priest says: "Give the kiss of love and peace, that ye may be prepared for the Holy Mysteries of God" (ibid., IV, pp. 106, 144).

[^2089]: *Sobranie drevnikh liturgiy*, III, pp. 95–101. Cf. Porphyrius Uspensky, Bishop. *Vostok khristianskiy. Bogosluzhenie abissin.* [The Christian East: Abyssinian Divine Services]. Kyiv, 1869, pp. 61 et seq.

[^2101]: Goar, J. *Euchologion*, p. 38. In the Gallican Liturgy, after the Trisagion, before the readings, there is appointed *Kyrie eleyson* or *rogationes*, by which is understood a litany, and it is restored according unto Eastern models (which ones?) in the following form:
Deacon: In peace let us pray unto the Lord. Choir: Lord, have mercy.
D.: Let us pray for the peace of the whole world, for the good estate and union of the Holy Churches of God. Ch.: Lord, have mercy.
D.: Let us pray unto the Lord for the pastors of the Church, bishops, deacons, for all the clergy and all the Christian people. Ch.: Lord, have mercy.
D.: Let us pray unto the Lord for sovereigns and all that are in authority, that they may execute the work of their governance in righteousness and love. Ch.: Christ, have mercy.
D.: Let us pray unto the Lord, that He may grant us a temperate balance of the air and an abundance of the fruits of the earth. Ch.: Christ, have mercy.
D.: Let us pray for the salvation of travelers, the sick, prisoners, and all that suffer. Ch.: Christ, have mercy.
D.: Let us pray unto the Lord for the preservation of peace among all nations. Ch.: Lord, have mercy.
D.: Let us pray unto the Lord, that He may deliver us from every evil, spiritual or temporal. Ch.: Lord, have mercy.
D.: Let us pray unto the Lord, that He may forgive our transgressions and account us worthy to live holily and receive everlasting life. Ch.: Lord, have mercy.
Thereupon follows a prayer (*collectio*) with the response of the choir: Amen (*Sobranie drevnikh liturgiy*, IV, p. 97).

[^2200]: In the Typikon it is said: "having drawn a cross with the censer" [*nachertav krest s kadil'nitseyu*]; this is too literal a translation of the Greek *meta tou thymiāmatos* [μετὰ τοῦ θυμιάματος]: *meta* designates merely the instrument of action (I. Mansvetov, *The Church Typikon* [*Tserkovny ustav*], p. 344).

[^2201]: In greater detail, with additions unto the Typikon and *Sluzhebnik* produced by general and local practice, the rite of the Entrance is set forth in the *Order of Matins, Vespers, and Midnight Office* [*Posledovanie utreni, vecherni i polunoshchnitsy*], edition of the Kiev Caves Lavra, 1861, 1884. According unto this *Order*, the deacon before the Entrance censes the Holy Table round about, and the priest kisses the Holy Table; upon departing through the north door they proceed behind the ambo; when they halt, the deacon censes the holy icons and the priest, takes the censer in his left hand and the orarion in his right, and requests a blessing upon the Entrance; unto the blessing of the Entrance by the priest the deacon replies: "Amen", censes again the priest and the choirs and, having taken his stand in the Royal Doors, awaits the conclusion of the Theotokion; upon entering into the sanctuary, the deacon censes the Holy Table and the High Place, while the priest kisses the Holy Table and takes his stand at the High Place unto the right of the Holy Table facing west; the deacon goes forth again through the Royal Doors, censes the people, and upon returning into the sanctuary censes the front side of the Holy Table and the priest, after which he takes his stand at the High Place unto the left of the Holy Table; here he stands with the priest until the end of the prokeimenon, making exclamations unto it.

[^2215]: In the Roman Catholic Church even now there is something analogous unto our entrances, and in certain ancient orders of the Latin Liturgy there was an entrance entirely according unto our order. Thus, at the Gallican Liturgy at the very beginning after the "Introit", at the Canticle of Zechariah, "the priest, representing Christ coming into the world, appeareth before the congregation, preceded by the deacon bearing the Gospel and ministers bearing lamps, in token of the evangelical light brought by Jesus Christ into the world." In exactly the same manner at this Liturgy there are transferred from the side altar unto the main altar by the deacon the paten (*patena*) with the bread, and by the priest the chalice with the wine before the consecration of the Gifts (*Collection of Ancient Liturgies* [*Sobranie drevnikh liturgiy*], Vol. IV, p. 102). In the present Roman order of the Mass, unto our Little Entrance corresponds at the beginning thereof the so-called *introitus* ("entrance"), consisting of an antiphon and psalm, appointed for each feast, during which the priest, after censing the altar, approacheth from its right side unto the center with hands folded upon his breast, having previously made a bow of the head unto the cross. Unto the Great Entrance in the Roman Catholic Mass corresponds the transference before the Mass of the chalice and other Eucharistic vessels from the sacristy unto the altar, preceded by a cleric with the Missal unto the ringing of a bell.

[^2219]: Apparently, the kernel of the hymn "O Gladsome Light" is given in a prayer of the *Egyptian Church Order* (see Introductory Chapter, p. 72) in its Ethiopic recension; therein is found the following section: "Rule concerning the bringing in of the lamp at Vespers in the assembly. When evening hath come, the deacon bringeth in the lamp (cf. the Entrance). The bishop greeteth the assembly: The Lord be with you all. The people reply: And with thy spirit (cf. now 'Peace be unto all' after the Entrance). The bishop: Let us give thanks unto the Lord. The people: It is meet (cf. 'worthy art Thou') and right, majesty and glory be unto Him (cf. Introductory Chapter, p. 125). Yet he must not say: 'Lift up your hearts'; this is said only at the Oblation. The bishop: We give thanks unto Thee, O God, through Thy Son, our Lord Jesus Christ, that Thou hast enlightened us through the revelation of the immaterial Light. Having finished the length of the day and reached the beginning of the night, having been satisfied with the light of day, created by Thee for our gratification, we now lack not also evening light through Thy mercy. We hallow and glorify Thee through Thine only Son, our Lord Jesus Christ, through Whom and with Whom unto Thee be glory and power and honor now..." (I. Karabinov, *Eucharistic Prayers* [*Evkharisticheskie molitvy*], p. 15).

[^2256]: The designation of this litany as "augmented" (*sugubaya*) is not in use in the liturgical service-books and is not entirely precise, yet for clarity and brevity it is convenient.

[^2257]: In ancient monuments it was termed simply "litany" (*ektenēs* [Gr. ἐκτενής]), whereas the Great Litany was termed *synaptē* [Gr. συναπτή], and the Litany of Fervent Supplication [Litany of Petition] *aitēseis* [Gr. αἰτήσεις] (petitions). So, for example, in the Evergetis Typikon (A. Dmitrievsky, *Typika* [*Typika* / Gr. Τυπικά], p. 607). The designation *ektenēs* is correctly translated into Slavonic as "fervent prayer" [*prilezhnoe molenie*]; at the Liturgy of the Evangelist Mark, before the words of institution concerning the Body the deacon proclaims: *ekteinate* [Gr. ἐκτείνατε] ("pray earnestly"), and before the words concerning the Blood: *eti ekteinate* [Gr. ἔτι ἐκτείνατε] ("pray yet more earnestly") (C. A. Swainson, *The Greek Liturgies*, p. 52).

[^2260]: *Kopiaō* [Gr. κοπιάω]—to labor unto exhaustion (Matt. 6:28). Thus were called the gravediggers [fossarii / kopiatēs], of whom Constantine the Great constituted a special corporation, exempt from taxes and subject unto ecclesiastical authority, consequently, a certain order of clergy. It is scarcely probable that they are meant here and placed prior unto the singers because they stood higher than them (Veniamin (Krasnopevkov-Rumovsky), Archbishop, *Novaya Skrizhal'* [The New Tablet], in 4 parts, St. Petersburg, 1858, p. 26); rather, here are meant the manual laborers of the monastery, and they are placed higher than the singers on account of the arduousness of their service.

[^2264]: The petition "for those who bear fruit", not encountered in earlier monuments of the Augmented Litany, arose from a special litany—the "prayer of fruit-bearing" (*euchē karpophorias* [Gr. εὐχὴ καρποφορίας]), appointed in the Liturgy of the Apostle James after the Our Father and "Holy Things unto the holy" (later manuscripts of the Liturgy of the Apostle James, Paris 14th c., no longer possess this litany) and containing a supplication for "those who have borne fruit" (*karpophorēsantōn* [Gr. καρποφορησάντων]), wherein are meant those who brought bread and wine for the Eucharist (in antiquity, perhaps, also other provisions for the agape). This litany contained 1–3 petitions—for the archbishop, clergy, and people (Messina, Rossano, and Sinai MSS) and together with or in place of this (Paris 14th c.) for help unto all the afflicted ("for every suffering soul", or the like) and the repose of the departed, and thereafter or between these petitions (in the Messina, Sinai MSS, and the Holy Sepulchre *Order of Passion and Paschal Week* of the 9th–12th c.) also a petition for those who brought fruits in such forms: "For those who have brought fruit-offerings on this day, that they may receive defense, forgiveness, and communion of eternal life, having finished [their course] ineffably (?), let us pray" (Jerusalem Order); "Furthermore for the salvation and remission of sins of our brother who hath offered" (Messina); "For the health, salvation, and remission of sins of the servants of God N. and N., who have borne fruit on this day" (Sinai) (Swainson, *The Greek Liturgies*, 312; Dmitrievsky, A., *Bogosluzhenie strastnoy i paskhalnoy sedmitsy v Ierusalime IX–X v.*, 30, 284).

[^2304]: *Litē* [Gr. Λιτή] in Homer, although it still signifieth also an ordinary request (*Odyssey*, XI, 34), yet *litai* [Gr. λιταί] with him are personified penitential prayers, daughters of Zeus, following after the wicked goddess *Atē* [Gr. Ἄτη] and making amends for her ruinous deeds (*Iliad*, IX, 502 [printed 52]); among the classics, however (Pindar, the tragedians), the word has only a religious meaning; later there appears the synonymous word *litaneia* [Gr. λιτανεία], used of prayer in the Septuagint (2 Macc. 2:16; 5:5; 10:16; Dionysius of Halicarnassus, 1st c., and others); of a religious procession *litaneia* appears for the first time in the 4th century in John Chrysostom and Basil the Great; *litē*, presumably, for the first time in the 6th century, and among the Greeks it becomes predominant in this sense (Theodore the Reader, 6th c.; *Spiritual Meadow* [*Pratum Spirituale*]), whereas among the Latins the second term [*litania*] does so. For detailed citations: Pape, *Griechisch-Deutsches Handwörterbuch*, II, 1, 54; Sophocles, *Greek Lexicon*, 716.

[^2310]: The exit unto the litiya through the north doors with the Holy Doors closed, which are not opened at all at Vespers after the Entrance, is observed in major monasteries and cathedrals (for example, in the Kyiv-Caves Lavra, the Dormition Cathedral) and in many Great Russian villages; but in the majority of churches the exit is made through the Holy Doors.

[^2373]: The Liturgical Apostle has, in addition to the division into chapters and pericopes [*zachalá*], special sections numbering 72, in the margins before which are placed indications of the days of the week: "Sunday", "Monday"... To use this division for the present readings (Nikolsky, K., Archpriest, *Posobie k izucheniyu ustava bogosluzheniya* [Manual for the Study of the Typikon of Divine Worship], 230) is impossible, if only in view of the discrepancy between the number of sections and the Sunday vigils at which such readings are appointed, which number fewer than 50.

[^2436]: In the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra, by tradition, presumably from the dominance of the Studite Typikon and other recensions of the Octoechos, they sing different Sunday Theotokia compared with those placed in the present Octoechos. Here they are. Tone 1 after Kathisma 2: "Having conceived unconsumed the Divine fire and given birth without seed unto the Fountain of life, the Lord, O full of grace, Theotokos, save them that magnify thee." Tone 2 after Kathisma 2: "Concealed from the laws of nature, thou hast united virginity unto Divine childbearing; for thou alone hast given birth unto Him Who was begotten of the Father before thee without mother, therefore thee, O Theotokos, do we magnify." Tone 3 after Kathisma 1: model (*podoben*), like the sessional hymn, **"Today the Virgin"** (*Deva dnes'*): "The heavenly hosts rejoiced with love and the earthly trembled with fear, when the most pure voice came upon thee, O Theotokos; for a single triumph shone forth for both, when it delivered the first-formed from death." Tone 4 after Kathisma 1: "Thee do we magnify, O Theotokos, crying aloud: Thou art the mountain wherefrom ineffably was cut a stone, which shattered the gates of Hades." Tone 5 after Kathisma 1: "A wondrous miracle of conception and an ineffable manner of birth was made known in thee, O Pure Virgin; it astonisheth my mind and terrifieth my thought; thy glory, O Theotokos, is spread abroad unto all, for the salvation of our souls." In Tones 4 and 5 the Theotokia of the 1st Kathisma are transferred unto the 2nd. In Tones 6 and 7 the customary Theotokia are transposed. Tone 8 after Kathisma 1: "Rejoice, thou that through the Angel receivedst the joy of the world; rejoice, thou that didst bear thy Creator and Lord; rejoice, thou that wast vouchsafed to be the Mother of God." **"All of creation rejoiceth in thee"** (*O Tebe raduetsya*) is model (*podoben*) to the 1st sessional hymn. After each kathisma there is one sessional hymn, sung once: for Tones 1–6 the present 1st and 2nd after the 1st Kathisma; for Tone 7 the first after the 1st and 2nd Kathismata, but transposed; for Tone 8 the same without transposition (*Notny Obikhod of the Kyiv-Pechersk Dormition Lavra*, Part 1: *All-Night Vigil, Score*, Kyiv, 1910, pp. 104ff.).

[^2473]: In other liturgies the exclamations preparatory unto the Gospel received even greater development than among us. Thus, in the present Abyssinian Liturgy the priest says: "The holy Gospel, the word of the Son of God, preached and proclaimed by N"; the people: "Glory to Thee ever, O Christ our Lord and God" and Psalm 80:2, 3 [LXX; Masoretic 81:1, 2]; after the Gospel: "Cherubim and Seraphim send up glory unto Him. Holy, Holy..." (Bishop Porphyrius Uspensky, *The Christian East. The Divine Services of the Abyssinians*, 60). In the Coptic Liturgy, after a twofold prayer of the Gospel, the second with the exclamation "Let us pray for the holy Gospel," Deacon: "Arise." Priest: "In the Name of God." Deacon: "Bless, master." Priest: "The beginning of the holy Gospel according to N." The interpreter (reader?) of the Gospel: "Stand with fear of God [and hear the holy Gospel] of our Lord and God." After the Gospel, a silent prayer (Bishop Porphyrius Uspensky, *Doctrinal Teaching, Divine Services... of the Copts*, 128). In the Nestorian Liturgy of the Apostles Addai and Mari, likewise after 2 prayers: "Deacon: Prepare yourselves for the hearing of the holy Gospel. Priest: Peace be with you. People: With thee and with thy spirit. After the proclamation whence the Gospel is taken, Deacon: Glory to Christ our God. After the Gospel he says: Glory to Christ our God, let us commit ourselves and one another unto the Father, and unto the Son, and unto the Holy Spirit." In a later recension of the same Liturgy: "Priest: Peace to all. Deacon: To thee and to thy spirit. The clergy [sing] a chant unto the four Evangelists concerning the worthy hearing of the Gospel. Priest: The holy Gospel of our Lord Jesus Christ. Reading from St. N. After the Gospel the clergy sing: Glory to Christ our Master" (Petrovsky, A., *The Apostolic Liturgies of the Eastern Church*, 65, 66).

[^2498]: *Omphalion* [Gr. ὀμφάλιον] or *omphalos* [Gr. ὀμφαλός] is a colored circle or oval inlaid into the floor in the center of the church (Golubinsky, E., *Istoriya Russkoy Tserkvi* [History of the Russian Church], I, 2, 204). Such an *omphalos* is indicated in St. Sophia by *De cerimoniis aulae Byzantinae*, pp. 15, 63, 87, 130, 524; it is also arranged in reception chambers of Byzantine palaces (ibid., 130, 524; Du Cange, *Glossarium*, s.v.).

[^2537]: Regarding the doubling of stichera and troparia, the general rule is that a stanza appointed to be doubled must be chanted twice in succession, and not repeated subsequently; this is required by the antiphonal character of liturgical chanting in general.

[^2538]: The fundamental rule of this combination is that the entire kanon of Sunday Matins must not increase beyond 14, that is, that each of its odes must contain no more than 14 troparia including the heirmos. How consistently and strictly the Typikon enforces this principle of combining other kanons with the Sunday kanon is shown by the circumstance that even when a Great Feast of the Theotokos having a kanon to 16 coincides with Sunday, Sunday Matins does not increase its kanon by a single verse, and the festal kanon for this reason is halved and chanted only to 8. It is as though the number 14—this doubled sacred 7 (7 troparia for each choir)—constitutes an inviolable prerogative for the kanon of Sunday Matins.

In correspondence with the inter-ode interval of Odes 3 and 6, a reading would be required after the exapostilarion. But it is absent, presumably in order not to interrupt the unbroken stream of hymnody in this portion of Matins, where the solemnity of the divine service reaches its apex. Moreover, the readings of Matins increase in their importance and compunction in the course of its progression: first the commentary on the Epistle, then on the Gospel, then the *Margarita* (the finest works of the greatest Father [St. John Chrysostom]), and finally the Prolog (frequently replacing the entire service of a saint); here an even superior reading would be required; such could be only Sacred Scripture itself (cf. the conclusion of Vespers); in antiquity here there was indeed a Gospel reading (as also at Roman Catholic Matins). As a replacement for a reading here serves the "Catechesis" [Oglashenie] after Matins.

[^2718]: Here is the hymn from October 1 until Advent, authored by St. Ambrose: "Eternal Creator of all things, / ruling over night and day / and granting seasons in their times, / to alleviate weariness (*fastidium*); / a nocturnal light for wayfarers, / dividing night from night (the dawn), the herald of the day already sounds, / calling forth the ray of the sun. Awakened by him, the daystar (*Lucifer*) / unbinds the heaven from darkness; / thereby the whole troop of errors / abandons its harmful path. / Thereby the sailor gathers strength, / the waves of the sea subside; / thereby the very rock of the Church with song washes away sin. / Let us arise then briskly: / the cock awakens the sleeping, / rebukes the sluggish, / reproaches those who deny (*negantes*); / at cockcrow hope returns, / health is breathed into the sick, / boldness leaves the robber, / unto the fallen faith returns. / Jesus, look upon the wavering, / and correct us with Thy gaze; / if Thou lookest, weakness passes away, / guilt is washed away in tears; / Thou, O Light, shine upon our senses, / dispel the slumber of the mind; / unto Thee first our voice sounds, / and unto Thee we perform our vows. / Unto God the Father be glory, / and to His only Son, / with the Spirit the Paraclete, / now and unto all ages. Amen" (*Breviarium Romanum*, IV, 23).

[^2727]: Manuscript of the Moscow Synodal Library, no. 678/386, fol. 14v.
The Coptic and Abyssinian [Ethiopian] Churches, which contain in their other services so much that is similar to our own, do not possess the First Hour; however, the latter, at a service analogous to our Matins, contains a prayer quite close to **"O Christ, the True Light"** (B. Turayev, *Chasoslov efiopskoy tserkvi* [Horologion of the Ethiopian Church], p. 13).
— In the Roman Catholic Church, the First Hour [Prime] differs considerably from the others in structure and is longer than they. Into the customary beginning the Creed is inserted; the hymn at dawn (*Jam lucis orto sidere*) invites to prayer that God may preserve us throughout daily affairs from everything harmful, bridle the tongue, shield sight from vanity, cleanse the heart, crush the pride of the flesh, so that toward the close of day, purified by abstinence, we may sing His glory; the antiphon on Sunday is *Alleluia*, on ordinary weekdays: **"Blessed are the undefiled in the way, who walk in the law of the Lord"** (Ps 118:1 [LXX 118:1; Ps 119:1]), Psalm 53 [LXX 53; Ps 54], then on Sunday Psalm 117 [LXX 117; Ps 118], Monday 23, Tuesday 24, Wednesday 25, Thursday 22, Friday 21, Saturday and feasts—nothing; then always two sections of Psalm 118, vv. 1–16 and 17–32; on Sunday the Athanasian Creed with the antiphon *Alleluia* (3); the *capitulum* on Sunday 1 Timothy 1:17 (the doxology **"Unto the King of ages"**), on weekdays Zechariah 8:19 (according to the Vulgate: *"Love ye truth and peace, saith the Lord of hosts"*) with the responsory **"Thanks be to God"** (*Deo gratias*). **"O Christ, Son of the living God, have mercy on us"** (twice). **"Thou Who sittest at the right hand of the Father, have mercy on us. Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit. O Christ, Son... Arise, O Christ, help us, and deliver us for Thy name's sake."** Then follow prayers with confession (omitted on major feasts): *Kyrie eleison, Christe eleison, Kyrie eleison*, the Our Father and the Creed in secret with the audible proclamation of the final verse in each; responsories: verses from Psalm 87:14 [LXX 87:14; Ps 88:13]; 70:8 [LXX 70:8; Ps 71:8] (= with us); 50:11–14 [LXX 50:11–14; Ps 51:9–12] (on weekdays, a further 12 responsories from Psalms 139:1; 88:1; 60:9; 64:1; 69:1 [LXX], containing petitions for deliverance from enemies, vows to God, divine assistance, our Trisagion, and again 5 responsories from Psalm 102:1–5 [LXX] concerning the mercies of God); 123:8 [LXX 123:8; Ps 124:8] (for help); the priest pronounces the confession of sins (*confessio*) unto God and various saints, with a petition to the saints for their prayers (*Confiteor*); the choir responds: **"May Almighty God have mercy upon thee (*Misereatur*), forgive thee thy sins, and bring thee unto everlasting life. Amen"**; then the choir makes confession, and the priest gives the same response, adding: **"May the Almighty and merciful Lord grant us pardon (*indulgentiam*), absolution, and remission of our sins,"** and the responsories: **"Vouchsafe, O Lord, to keep us this day without sin. Have mercy on us, O Lord, have mercy on us,"** and Psalm 32:22 [LXX 32:22; Ps 33:22]; 106:2 [LXX 106:2; Ps 107:2], **"The Lord be with you."** A prayer to Almighty God for a sinless and virtuous coming day, **"The Lord be with you..."**, **"Let us bless the Lord, Thanks be to God,"** and the reading of the Martyrology followed by the responsories: **"Precious in the sight of the Lord"** (Ps 115:6 [LXX 115:6; Ps 116:15]), a brief prayer to the Most Holy Virgin Mary and to all the saints for obtaining assistance and salvation from God, again Psalm 123:8 [LXX], **"Glory to the Father..."**, *Kyrie eleison*... Our Father in secret with the audible proclamation of the end; responsory Psalm 89:16–17 [LXX 89:16–17; Ps 90:16–17] (= with us at Matins at the Aposticha); a prayer to God the Savior of the world for the direction and sanctification of our feelings, words, and deeds of this day according to the commandments of God; the blessing: *"Iube, Domine, benedicere"* (Bid, master, a blessing); **"May the Lord Almighty order our days and our deeds in His peace. Amen."** A brief reading from 2 Thessalonians 3:5, responsory again from Psalm 123:8 [LXX], and the final blessing: **"Blessed be God. May the Lord bless us and preserve us from all evil, and bring us unto life eternal; and may the souls of the faithful departed, through the mercy of God, rest in peace. Amen"** (*Breviarium Romanum*, I, 24–43; II, 25–44; III, 23–41; IV, 24–42).

[^2729]: John 19:14; 18:28.

[^2730]: **"They are filled with children, and have left the rest to their babes"** [Ps 16:14 LXX]—they possess a multitude of children (having many children was considered a blessing—not only did they taste of this blessing, but were filled with it) and leave them an inheritance while yet in infancy (they have children until old age). In Hebrew, a somewhat different thought is expressed (see Russian Synodal Bible). **"From the few of the earth divide them in their life"**—remove them while yet alive (not only in death) from the "few," the oppressed; but in Greek: "destroying (*apolyon*) them from the earth, divide them in their life." **"Of Thy hidden things"**—of treasures.

[^2731]: C. B. Moll, *Der Psalter*, I, 26; B. Duhm, *Die Psalmen*, 46–50, 77–80; H. Gunkel, *Ausgewählte Psalmen*, 111–122.

[^2732]: E. Diakovsky, *Posledovanie chasov i izobrazitel'nykh*, p. 29.

[^2733]: E. Diakovsky, *Posledovanie chasov i izobrazitel'nykh*, pp. 135 et seq. In the Coptic and Ethiopian (Abyssinian) Churches, the Third Hour has Psalms 19, 22, 23, 25, 28, 29, 33, 40, 42, 44, 45, 46 [LXX]; our troparion with its verse and Theotokion, but at **"Glory..."** yet another troparion, longer, with a petition for the renewal within us of the Spirit of all charisms, among them the Spirit of prophecy; our verse of the Hour; after the Trisagion: **"O Heavenly King,"** **"As Thou wast in the midst of Thy disciples,"** **"Standing in the temple of Thy glory"**; the concluding prayer asks for the sending down of the grace of the Holy Spirit and a virtuous life; in the Ethiopian Horologion there are two further prayers of analogous content (Bishop Porphyrius, *Verouchenie, bogosluzhenie... koptov* [Doctrinal Teaching, Worship... of the Copts], pp. 57–61; B. Turayev, *Chasoslov efiopskoy tserkvi* [Horologion of the Ethiopian Church], pp. 47–53). At the Roman Catholic Terce [Third Hour], the hymn asks the Holy Spirit (*Nunc Sancte nobis Spiritus*) to pour forth into us, that tongue, mind, and feeling may resound with confession, and that love toward neighbors may blaze; three sequential sections of Psalm 118 [LXX]; the antiphons to them on ordinary Sundays: *Alleluia*, on weekdays: **"Lead me"** (Ps 118:35 [LXX]); a *capitulum* according to the season from the Old or New Testament with corresponding responsories; on weekdays kneeling (as at the Sixth and Ninth Hours): *Kyrie eleison, Christe eleison, Kyrie eleison*, the Our Father in secret with its conclusion as a responsory, and further responsories: Psalm 79:8 [LXX 79:8; Ps 80:7] (a petition for conversion), **"Arise, O Christ, help us and deliver us for Thy name's sake"** (after Ps 43:26 [LXX 43:26; Ps 44:26]), Psalm 101:2 [LXX 101:2; Ps 102:1] (for the hearing of prayer), and the collect corresponding to the commemoration of the day (*Breviarium Romanum*, I, 43–46).

[^2734]: **"Faintness of heart"** [*Malodushiya*], Greek ὀλιγοψυχίας (*oligopsychias*), from ψῦχος (*psychos*)—cold—most likely signifying stifling, oppressive heat.

[^2735]: **"Equal-minded"** [*Ravnodushie*], Greek ἰσόψυχος (*isopsychos*)—from the Hebrew "equal to me" or "that which I am," an *alter ego*; **"my master"** [*vladyko moy*], Greek ἡγεμών (*hegemon*), likely in the sense of "greatly respected"; in Hebrew, "friend" or "companion."

[^2736]: **"From the pestilence that walketh in darkness"** [*Ot veshchi, vo t'me perekhodyashchiya*]—Hebrew "plague" or "pestilence"—perhaps referring to the nighttime destruction by the Angel of the Egyptian firstborn and of the Assyrian army; **"from the encounter"** [*ot sryashcha*], Greek σύмпτωμα (*symptoma*)—attack, mishap, or stroke; **"and the noonday demon"** [*besa poludennago*], Hebrew "destruction that wasteth at noonday"; "1,000"—as in an epidemic or in war.

[^2737]: C. B. Moll, *Der Psalter*, I, 271–277; B. Duhm, *Die Psalmen*, 149–153; H. Gunkel, *Ausgewählte Psalmen*, 145–151.

[^2738]: Diakovsky, E., *Posledovanie chasov i izobrazitelnykh* [The Order of the Hours and Typika], 123–137. In the Coptic and Ethiopian (Abyssinian) Horologia, the Sixth Hour has Psalms 53, 56, 62, 66, 69, 83–86, 90, and 92; the troparion and Theotokion following the Psalms are our own, but at *Glory...* there is an additional troparion petitioning the Crucified One to put passions to death and turn the mind from passions to the commandments; the troparia after the Trisagion are ours, but the Theotokion is solely "Most praised art Thou" (*Preproslavlena yesi*); the concluding prayer gives thanks for attaining so momentous an hour and petitions for the mortification of passions, a virtuous life, and non-condemnation at the Dread Judgment (Bishop Porfiry, *Verouchenie, bogosluzhenie... koptov* [Doctrine, Worship... of the Copts], 64–65; Turayev, B., *Chasoslov efiopskoy tserkvi* [Horologion of the Ethiopian Church], 55–61). In the Roman Catholic Sext (Sixth Hour), the hymn petitions the mighty Ruler (*Rector*), the true God Who orders the alternation of things, the Dispenser of light and fire, to extinguish the flames of strife, remove harmful heat, and grant health to the body and peace to the heart; as Psalms, three divisions of Psalm 118 [LXX]: vv. 81–96, 97–112, 113–128; the antiphon before them is usually Alleluia on Sunday, and on weekdays "Help me" (*Adjuva me*, v. 117); after them, Alleluia thrice; a *capitulum* from the New or Old Testament suited to the season of the year or feast, with corresponding responsories; on weekdays, identical prayers (*preces*) for each hour and an oration (prayer) varying according to seasons of the year and feasts (*Breviarium Romanum*, I, 46–49; II, 48–51, et al.).

[^2752]: For this reason, one cannot speak without inner grief of the fact that ordinarily the troparia at the Beatitudes are omitted. And this occurs at the Divine Liturgy — a service so concise and so sacred that omissions here are less permissible than anywhere else. The omission of the troparia at the Beatitudes reduces the festive Divine Liturgy to the level of a weekday service and ruins the feast. Reading them instead of chanting them does so all the more.

This departure from the Typikon is connected with an erroneous, even perverse view of the Alleluia as a conclusion to the Epistle rather than as a preparation for the Gospel—a view that demeans both the Alleluia and the Gospel.

[^2774]: *Missale Romanum*, 4; J.-P. Migne, *Patrologia Latina*, 85, 110–111. In the Eastern non-Chalcedonian and other non-Orthodox liturgies, the preparatory exclamations before the Epistle, as before the Gospel (see above, p. 657), grew to large proportions, especially in view of the aforementioned multiplicity of readings. In the Nestorian Liturgy, before the Old Testament reading, the reader or deacon says: "Sit in silence. The prophecy of the Prophet N, master, bless." The priest says: "May Christ bless." Before the Epistle, a special prayer is said aloud by the priest, varying for fasts and feasts, for divine assistance and understanding; the deacon says: "The Epistle of the Apostle Paul unto N, master, bless"; the priest: "May Christ bless thee in His holy teaching, that thou mayest be a radiant mirror unto them that listen unto thee"; after the Epistle, the deacon says: "Glory to the Master of the Apostle Paul" (A. Petrovsky, *Apostolic Liturgies of the Eastern Church*, Appendix, 64, 95). In the Coptic Liturgy, before the reading of the Pauline Epistles, the Catholic Epistles, and the Acts—before each reading separately—there is censing, but no exclamations of any kind are indicated; after each of the readings there is a prayer: the prayer after the 1st reading asks for understanding to comprehend it and the possibility of imitating the Apostle; after the 2nd, for being accounted worthy of the lot of the Apostles, imitating them, and preserving the Church; after the 3rd, for the acceptance of the incense and for purification. In the Abyssinian Liturgy, before the Pauline Epistle the prayer is the same as in the Coptic after it, and the exclamation of the deacon is: "The reading from the Epistle of Paul, a servant and apostle of our Savior Jesus Christ, called, chosen, set apart for the preaching of the holy Gospel, unto N; whose prayer and blessing be with us, amen"; after the reading, the deacon says: "The grace of the Father, the love of the Son, and the gift of the Holy Spirit (a paraphrase of 2 Cor. 13:13), Who descended upon the blessed and pure Apostles in the upper room of holy Zion, be multiplied upon us Christian people, unto ages of ages, amen. O holy Apostle Paul, good servant, physician of the infirm, pray and intercede for us with Christ God, that He may save our souls according to the multitude of the mercy of His holy name." The priest says: "Peace be to all," and a prayer for the abiding of God with us and for purification. Before the Catholic Epistle, the priest says: "This is the reading from the Epistle of N, a disciple and apostle of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, whose prayer and blessing be with us, amen"; after the reading, the deacon says 1 John 2:15–17 (on not loving the world); the people pray to the Holy Trinity for the preservation of the assembly for the sake of the holy Apostle, and for comfort. Preparation for the Acts: the deacon: "Arise for prayer"; the priest: "Peace be to all"; "And to thy spirit"; prayers the same as in the Coptic Liturgy after the Catholic Epistles and after the Acts, and two others of general content; exclamation: "The Acts of the ministers of this preaching, our fathers the Apostles, pure and filled with grace, chosen and righteous, abounding in the grace of the Holy Spirit. May their prayers and blessings preserve all of us Christians unto ages of ages, amen"; after the reading, a paraphrase of Acts 6:7 (on the increase of the believers) and: "Holy (thrice) is God the Father Almighty, Holy (thrice) is the Only-begotten Son, the living Word of the Father, Holy (thrice) is the Holy Spirit, everywhere present" (Bishop Porphyrius, *Verouchenie, bogosluzhenie... koptov*, 125–127; *Bogosluzhenie abissinov*, 56–59).

While on a journey, one must carry with oneself 'the image of the Most Pure Panagia' (the *panagiarion*) and, having taken from the church the 'bread of the Mother of God', perform even alone the entire order, beginning with 'Great is the name'; if several brothers are traveling, the senior performs it, yet he does not break and distribute the bread unto the others, which only a priest and deacon have the right to do, but each breaks off a portion for himself. If there is no panagiarion, the bread is placed upon the right hand with the words: 'Blessed is God Who hath mercy upon us and nourisheth us'; 'and then having formed the fingers of the hand as a priest maketh the cross, and holding the bread in the hand, he signeth a cross with his hand and, lifting the bread on high, saith: "Great is the name"; and if there be no other brethren, he himself saith: "Of the Most Holy Trinity", and all together (at this place?) he signeth with a cross his image (icon?) with the hand wherewith he holdeth the bread, saying: "Most Holy Lady", and bringing his hand to his forehead; "Theotokos", his hand to his breast; "help", his hand to his right shoulder; "us", his hand to his left shoulder; then he saith: "Through her intercessions"' etc.; instead of 'Merciful and compassionate' [there is] Psalm 121, Trisagion, etc. (fols. (50) 99v–100).

[^2825]: The custom of elevating the panagia at the Liturgy was not alien to the Slavic Churches either. Thus, in a certain Slavic order of the Hierarchical Liturgy according to a 17th-century manuscript, after **'Especially'** [*Izryadno*] and the rubrics for the commemoration of the living and the dead, there is the note: 'then he elevateth the panagia, that is to say the *hypsoma* ("elevation"), if he wisheth, unto the help of soul and body; and it is done thus: the hierarch, taking the particle of the prosphora with the edges of two fingers of both hands, maketh a cross with it above the Most Pure Mysteries of the holy chalice and the Holy Diskos, saying thus: Great is the name of the Holy Trinity; Most Holy Theotokos, help Thy servant, our right-believing Tsar Aleksey, and deliver him from every adversity, and vouchsafe unto him the standing at the right hand of Thy Son and God; in like manner he doth also for the other names, as many as he wisheth: and they preserve it for the benefit of every need and infirmity' (MS of the Moscow Synodal Library, No. 670/368, fol. 47. Nevostruyev K., Gorsky I., Archpriest, *Description of the Slavic Manuscripts of the Moscow Synodal Library* [Opisanie slavyansk. rukopisey Mosk. Sin. bibl.] (Moscow, 1869), Vol. III, p. 107).

[^2830]: Pseudo-Kodinos, *De officialibus palatii Constantinopolitani*, p. 7. In the Roman Catholic Church there is likewise an order for the blessing of the table that recalls our own. There is a distinct order for the midday meal (*prandium*, strictly speaking luncheon) and the evening meal (*coena*). Each consists of two parts: prayers before the taking of food and after. The order begins with the exclamation of the priest: **“Bless”** (*Benedicite*), repeated by those present. Before the midday meal (or luncheon), the priest begins: **“The eyes of all...”**, and those present complete this passage from Psalm 144:15–16 [145:15–16]. **“Glory... Both now... Kyrie eleison, Christe eleison, Kyrie eleison, Our Father.”** The prayer with the customary exclamation before it, **“Let us pray”**: “Bless us, O Lord, and these Thy gifts, which we are about to receive from Thy bounty, through Christ our Lord, amen.” The reader: “Vouchsafe (*jube*), master, to bless.” The blessing: “May the King of eternal glory make us partakers of the heavenly table, amen.” After the midday meal (or luncheon): When the reader says: “Thou, O Lord, have mercy upon us”, the response: “Thanks be to God”, all arise, and the priest says: **“Let all Thy works praise Thee, O Lord...”** (Ps. 144:10 [145:10]), **“Glory... Both now...”** and the prayer (*absolute*): “We give thanks unto Thee, Almighty God, for all Thy benefits, Who livest and reignest unto the ages of ages.” There is recited alternately Psalm 50 [51] or 116 [117], **“Glory... Both now... Kyrie eleison”**, etc., **“Our Father”** (secretly, with the aloud proclamation of the conclusion). The response from Psalm 111:9 [112:9] (“He hath dispersed, He hath given to the poor...”), Psalm 33:2–4 [34:2–4], Psalm 112:2 [113:2] (“Blessed be the name of the Lord...”). The prayer (without **“Let us pray”**): “Vouchsafe, O Lord, to reward with eternal life all who do good unto us for Thy name's sake, amen.” The response: “Let us bless the Lord. Thanks be to God. May the souls of the faithful, through the mercy of God, rest in peace.” **“Our Father...”** (secretly). The priest: “May God grant us His peace, amen.” Before the evening meal the priest, following an introduction identical to that before the midday meal, begins: **“The poor shall eat...”** (Ps. 21:27 [22:26]) (cf. our own practice!), **“Glory... Both now...”** and further as at the midday meal, only with the concluding blessing: “May the King of eternal glory bring us unto the supper of eternal life.” At the conclusion of the evening meal there is said: **“He hath made a remembrance...”** (Ps. 110:5 [111:5]), **“Glory... Both now...”** The priest: “Blessed is God in His gifts, and holy in all His works, Who liveth and reigneth unto ages of ages, amen.” Psalm 116 [117] and all further as at the midday meal. If there be only one meal in the day, then the second order is performed. For the meals of Great Thursday, Friday, and Saturday, Bright Week, and the days from the Ascension unto the Leave-taking of Pentecost, and on the days of the Nativity of Christ and Theophany with their afterfeasts, there is a special order—with distinct responses at the beginning and end and a psalm (*Breviarium romanum*. Ratisb., 1897, I, (238)–(270); II, (247)–(250); III and IV (233)–(234)).

[^2831]: In the ancient Coptic and Abyssinian Churches, other great events as well were celebrated not merely annually, but monthly—for example, the Nativity of Christ (*Introductory Chapter*, 325–326); on the other hand, in the Orthodox Church as well, what is celebrated weekly is not the Resurrection of Christ, so to speak, in its pure form, but in conjunction with the commemoration of the death of Christ (see above, pp. 112, 677).

[^2832]: It is not named, probably because it constitutes a comparatively later addition unto the Polyeleos.

[^2833]: These troparia in antiquity were Paschal (see above, p. 715).

[^2834]: In Greek κόλλυβα (*kollyba*), but this word is not Greek, as is evident from the fact that the holy Great-Martyr Theodore the Tyro explains its meaning unto the bishop to whom he appeared in a dream.
